    #7804. Engineering Manager, Automation Intelligence at Fingerprint

    <div class="content-intro"><p><strong>Fingerprint</strong> empowers enterprises to detect and stop online fraud with the world’s most accurate device intelligence.&nbsp; We lead our industry with bleeding-edge identification capabilities and work on turning new ideas and discoveries in the fraud detection space into reality. Our customers range from innovative startups to leading enterprise companies, including Plaid, Dropbox, and Booking.com.&nbsp;</p> <p><strong>Fingerprint is a globally dispersed, 100% remote company.</strong> We were named on on the 2026 Forbes Best Startup Employers list and ranked #803 on the 2026 Inc. 5000 list of America’s fastest-growing private companies.&nbsp;</p> <p><a href="https://www.crunchbase.com/organization/fingerprintjs">We have raised $77M </a>and are backed by Craft Ventures (<a href="https://www.tesla.com/">Tesla, </a><a href="https://facebook.com/">Facebook, </a><a href="https://www.airbnb.com/">Airbnb </a>), Nexus Venture Partners ( <a href="https://www.postman.com/">Postman</a>, <a href="https://www.apollo.io/">Apollo.io,</a> <a href="https://min.io/">MinIO</a>, Druva) and Uncorrelated Ventures ( <a href="https://redis.io/">Redis, </a><a href="https://rollbar.com/">Rollbar, </a><a href="https://gradle.org/">&nbsp;Gradle</a>).</p> <hr> <p>&nbsp;</p> <p>&nbsp;</p></div><h3>The Role in Context</h3> <p>Fingerprint's device intelligence platform processes billions of identification events for companies that need to know who — or what — is on the other end of a request. Increasingly, the answer is not a person. AI agents, AI assistants, crawlers, headless browsers, and malicious automation now make up a large and fast-growing share of internet traffic, and telling them apart from humans — and from each other — is becoming one of the most important problems our customers ask us to solve.</p> <p>We are standing up a dedicated Automation Intelligence engineering team to own that problem end to end: detection in the browser, detection at the edge, the classification taxonomy that tells customers what kind of automation they're seeing and how confident we are, and the API that customers and their agents call.</p> <p>We're looking for an <strong>Engineering Manager</strong> to lead this team. You'll inherit a small, senior group (three engineers to start, growing to four or five) and a clear mandate. You report directly to the VP of Engineering.</p> <hr> <h3><br>Your Mission</h3> <ul> <li><strong>Ship the product</strong> — Take the automation intelligence product through Beta to GA, and then to a standalone commercial launch. You own engineering readiness: scope, estimates, service reliability, operating limits, API and MCP coverage, and the evidence that it's ready</li> <li><strong>Make detection accurate and keep it that way</strong> — Build the team's quality loop: a test corpus of known bots, agents, and automation frameworks; continuous false-positive and false-negative testing; a regression test for every confirmed gap; and coverage benchmarked against the best in the industry. Detection quality is the product</li> <li><strong>Build a team that runs itself</strong> — Set the team up so adding a detection, shipping a change, or running a staged rollout doesn't depend on another team's calendar. Grow the engineers, hire the next seats, and create an environment where senior people do their best work</li> <li><strong>Lead the AI-native engineering model</strong> — AI agents write the code by default and engineers decide what gets built and whether it's right. You'll operate that model, measure it honestly, and produce the playbook the rest of engineering adopts</li> </ul> <hr> <h3><br>What You'll Do</h3> <p><strong>Delivery &amp; Product Readiness</strong></p> <ul> <li>Own the engineering plan for Beta → GA → standalone launch: PRD partnership with the product manager, estimates, sequencing, and the readiness evidence that gates each stage</li> <li>Define and prove the service's operating envelope — throughput, latency tails, rate limiting, availability SLOs — before customers depend on it</li> <li>Run staged rollouts for detection changes that affect customer bot rates, with feature flags, rollback, and clear go/no-go evidence for customer-facing teams</li> <li>Turn design-partner feedback and customer-reported false positives into prioritized, owned engineering work</li> </ul> <p><strong>Detection Quality &amp; Coverage</strong></p> <ul> <li>Stand up and run a weekly detection quality loop: prioritized test corpus, automated FP/FN testing across browser and edge detection, regression coverage, and drift monitoring</li> <li>Drive coverage of the automation that matters most to customers — AI agents and assistants first — benchmarked against industry leaders, and prioritized by customer exposure rather than catalogue size</li> <li>Guide the hard problems: self-hosted agents, stealth and anti-detect browser automation, and fingerprinting agent infrastructure across browser, network, and runtime evidence</li> <li>Evolve the classification contract from "automation detected" to rich agent context — automation type, verified vs. inferred identity, confidence, and reason codes — and keep detection and attribution measured separately</li> </ul> <p><strong>Team Leadership</strong></p> <ul> <li>Manage, coach, and grow a team of senior engineers; run hiring for the next seats, starting with a detection research and quality engineer</li> <li>Set the team's goals against its charter and bring them to leadership for alignment, not approval; communicate decisions as intent with reasoning before acting</li> <li>Establish healthy engineering practice from day one: written ownership lines, review tiers per repository, specs before epics, and baselines for cycle time, throughput, predictability, change failure rate, and recovery time</li> <li>Negotiate clean working agreements with the sibling team the codebase is shared with today, and drive gradual decoupling prioritized by what blocks independent deploys</li> </ul> <p><strong>AI-Native Engineering</strong></p> <ul> <li>Operate the agent-first development model: spec → coding agent → review agent → security review → human merge, with low-risk changes merging on agent approval and mandatory human review on critical paths</li> <li>Treat the agent harness — repository instructions, skills, review personas — as infrastructure maintained like code, and publish it so other teams start ahead</li> <li>Measure the model against the team's own baseline, run 30- and 60-day reviews, and write up what worked and what didn't for the rest of engineering</li> </ul> <p><strong>Contract-First APIs</strong></p> <ul> <li>Ensure every surface the team ships is contract-first: OpenAPI in the repo, breaking-change detection in CI, generated docs and SDKs, and the API kept readable by both developers and AI agents</li> <li>Own the team's observability: logs, metrics, traces, and SLOs for the detection service</li> </ul> <hr> <h3><br>What We're Looking For</h3> <p><strong>Required</strong></p> <ul> <li><strong>Engineering management experience:</strong> 3+ years managing software engineers, ideally including senior and staff-level ICs, with a track record of shipping products — not just features — through launch</li> <li><strong>Strong technical foundation:</strong> 8+ years in software engineering before or alongside management. You can read a design doc and a PR critically, engage senior engineers on architecture, and make technical calls when the team is split</li> <li><strong>Relevant domain depth</strong> in at least one of: bot detection or bot management, fraud or abuse prevention, web security, browser or client-side engineering, or high-volume API services. You understand that this is an adversarial problem where the other side adapts</li> <li><strong>Quality-driven mindset:</strong> Experience building or running measurement-driven engineering — test corpora, precision/recall or FP/FN tracking, regression suites, SLOs — and using that data to prioritize</li> <li><strong>Hands-on with AI coding tools:</strong> You use AI coding agents in your own work, have opinions about where they help and where they don't, and are excited to lead a team where they are the default</li> <li><strong>Cross-functional operator:</strong> You've worked closely with product managers, researchers, and customer-facing teams, and you're comfortable making dependencies and trade-offs explicit rather than absorbing them</li> <li><strong>Clear communicator:</strong> You write well, make decisions visible with your reasoning, and can explain detection quality and launch readiness to executives and customers alike</li> </ul> <p><strong>Nice to Have</strong></p> <ul> <li>Direct experience with <strong>bot detection, bot management, or anti-automation products</strong> (e.g. at a CDN, WAF, fraud, or security vendor)</li> <li>Familiarity with <strong>AI agent ecosystems</strong>: agent frameworks, verified-agent protocols, and how agents interact with websites and APIs</li> <li>Experience with <strong>browser internals or headless/automation frameworks</strong> and the techniques used to detect or evade them</li> <li>Experience running <strong>API-first products</strong> with public OpenAPI specs, SDKs, and MCP or similar agent-facing surfaces</li> <li>Experience <strong>forming a new team</strong> out of an existing one — separating codebases, setting ownership lines, and keeping the sibling relationship healthy</li> <li>Experience in a <strong>high-growth SaaS company</strong> with enterprise customers</li> </ul> <hr> <h3><br>The Shape of This Role</h3> <p>This is a hands-on-adjacent engineering manager role for a small, senior, high-leverage team. You are not expected to write the production code — your engineers and their agents do that — but you are expected to be close enough to it to set direction, evaluate the work, and hold the quality bar.</p> <p>The team has unusual autonomy and unusual visibility. It owns a product that leadership considers strategically critical, it is the first team operating Fingerprint's AI-native engineering model, and its results — good and bad — will be used as evidence for how the rest of engineering works. That means two things: you'll have real room to decide how the team operates, and you'll need to be honest about what the data shows.</p> <p>In the first 90 days you will likely: finalize the roster and ownership lines with the sibling team, get the quality loop and engineering baselines running, deliver the engineering evidence for the Beta gate, and run the first review of the AI-native operating model.</p> <hr> <h3><br>Why This Role?</h3> <ul> <li><strong>A problem that is getting harder and more important every quarter:</strong> The internet is filling with agents. Customers need to tell useful automation from abuse, and the answer isn't "block everything." The team that solves this well defines a category</li> <li><strong>A product with a clear path to market:</strong> Beta to GA to standalone launch, with product, go-to-market, and executive support already lined up</li> <li><strong>A senior team and a dedicated PM from day one:</strong> You're not starting from zero. The engineers already know the problem; your job is to focus them and remove what's in their way</li> <li><strong>Lead how engineering will work next:</strong> AI-native development, contract-first APIs, and a self-directed team model — piloted here first, with your findings shaping the whole organization</li> <li><strong>VP direct line:</strong> You report to the VP of Engineering, set your own goals against a clear charter, and bring recommendations rather than requests for direction</li> </ul> <p>&nbsp;</p> <p>&nbsp;</p> <hr> <h3><strong><em><br>Compensation &amp; Transparency</em></strong></h3> <p><em>At Fingerprint, we believe in pay transparency — it's part of how we operate as a distributed, remote-first team.&nbsp; Salaries are based on function, level, and geographic location, benchmarked against similar stage growth companies.&nbsp;</em></p> <p><em>Because this role is open across the United States and can be based anywhere our team members work from, the salary range reflects that geographic breadth: $159k - $215k</em><em><br></em></p> <p><em>Some example salaries for specific locations are below:</em></p> <p><em>NYC: $187k - $215k</em></p> <p><em>Chicago: $159k - $183k</em></p> <p><em>Final offer amounts are determined by multiple factors, including the successful candidate's location, experience, and expertise, and may vary from the amounts listed above.</em></p><div class="content-conclusion"><p>Due to regulatory and security reasons, there’s a small number of countries where we cannot have Fingerprint teammates based. Additionally, because Fingerprint is an all-remote company and people can join our workforce from almost any country, we do not sponsor visas. Fingerprint teammates need to be authorized to work from their home location.</p> <p>We are dedicated to creating an inclusive work environment for everyone. We embrace and celebrate the unique experiences, perspectives and cultural backgrounds that each employee brings to our workplace. Fingerprint strives to foster an environment where our employees feel respected, valued and empowered, and our team members are at the forefront in helping us promote and sustain an inclusive workplace. We highly encourage people from underrepresented groups in tech to apply.</p> <p>If you are applying as a resident of California, please read our CCPA notice <a href="https://dev.fingerprint.com/docs/dpa-ccpa">here</a>.</p> <p>If you are applying as a resident of the EU, please read our GDPR notice <a href="https://dev.fingerprint.com/docs/dpa-gdpr">here.</a></p> <p><em>**We have noticed a rise in recruiting impersonations across the industry, where scammers attempt to access candidates' personal and financial information through fake interviews and offers. All Fingerprint recruiting email communications will always come from the @fingerprint.com domain. Any outreach claiming to be from Fingerprint via other sources should be ignored.</em></p></div>

    #7805. Business Operations Manager (Finance & Analytics) at Metabase

    Metabase is the easiest way for people to get insights from their data, from tiny startups who get up and running quickly to major corporations with tens of thousands of users. That's why people love us.
     
    We bring data tools with the elegance and simplicity of consumer products to the crufty world of enterprise business intelligence. We provide an opinionated open source starting point for how companies should measure, analyze and share their data, which is used by tens of thousands of companies.

    About the role

    Our Finance & Analytics team owns the authoritative view of Metabase's business performance, and the opinionated read on what's driving it. Internally, this is used to drive decisions; externally, this is used to meet our obligations to investors, auditors, and tax authorities.

    We’re looking for a quantitative-minded generalist to lead a portfolio of analytical projects across the business and own an area of finance operations. Your analytical projects will focus on how we measure and understand the broader business: establishing authoritative definitions for key metrics and investigating what’s driving changes in the data. In your operational area, you’ll run the work and build systems that make it reliable and increasingly automated, drawing on external specialists for technical expertise.

    This isn’t a traditional finance role: we’re a small team of generalists who think in systems, learn unfamiliar domains quickly, and reason through problems from first principles.

    Responsibilities

  • Leading analytical projects that change how we understand the business. Examples include:
  • Adapting our ARR definition as our business model evolves
  • Evaluating the health of our sales and marketing funnel
  • Finding relationships between product initiatives and expansion or churn
  • Owning a traditional finance domain (such as Accounting or Tax). This will involve a combination of operational work, managing external experts, and evaluating the overall health of the operation
  • Automating the workflows in your domain so our operational and compliance requirements take as little manual effort as possible. Our aspiration is that areas of traditional finance are treated similarly to analytical systems: a set of inputs that are transformed via defined logic to produce a targeted outcome or insight
  • Leading cross-functional projects to tackle operational problems as they arise

    • About you

  • You think in systems: you understand how pieces fit together, build reliable processes, and automate repetitive work
  • You think in terms of numbers and are comfortable in a wide range of analytical domains, whether that’s financial modeling, doing intermediate statistical analysis, reviewing the methodology of external vendors, or transforming raw data. You can spot inconsistencies quickly, and enjoy getting to the right answer. SQL experience strongly preferred (nice-to-have: analytics engineering experience)
  • You know how to use AI to improve efficiency, and enjoy experimenting with novel applications of the technology. Just as importantly, you know the limitations of AI and don't use it as a substitute for your own thinking
  • You learn fast. For example, you don't need to be an accountant, but you can learn the rules of accounting (or other specialist domains) and apply them to day-to-day problems
  • You are adept at managing vendors, building strong relationships, and holding them accountable when needed
  • You have strong organizational skills with a high attention to detail: you enjoy checking items off your checklist, and can seamlessly balance multiple threads of work
  • Minimum 3–5 years experience, preferably in a high-growth startup
  • Strong quantitative undergraduate degree (nice to have: MBA)
  • Based in the US, Canada, or UK
  • Low-ego attitude: no job should be too small or too large
  • Nice-to-have: some familiarity with accounting principles or coursework
    • Compensation
    The US base salary range for this role is $150K–$200K annually, plus equity and benefits.
    Compensation is determined by role, level, location, and individual qualifications, including skills, experience, and scope of responsibility. Pay may vary by geographic location and other job-related factors.

    Benefits
    We offer meaningful equity and a comprehensive US benefits package, including fully employer-covered medical, dental, and vision insurance (with 75% coverage for dependents), a 401(k) with employer match available to enroll after the first 30 days, flexible and unlimited paid time off including sick time, and an asynchronous, remote-first environment.

    We're a global team (50% outside the US), fully distributed (from Thailand to California), who get things done asynchronously, with plenty of uninterrupted time, supporting each other to do the best work of our careers. We offer flexibility (define your own schedule and work from wherever you want), autonomy, and an environment that fosters growth, learning, and development. We're relentlessly user-focused and believe in building long-term value, not short-term hacks. And we raised a $30M Series B to take our approach to the next level for years to come.
     
    For U.S. applicants: Metabase participates in the federal E-Verify program, which confirms employment authorization of newly hired U.S. based employees. E-Verify is not used as a tool to pre-screen candidates and is only initiated upon hire.

    #7806. SDK Engineer - Python at Supabase

    About Supabase

    Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

    About the Role

    We're looking for a SDK Engineer - Python to join our SDK team and own how developers talk to Supabase from Python. You'll work closely with the rest of the SDK team, our Auth, Realtime, Storage, PostgREST, Functions, CLI and Docs teams, and the open-source community, helping us make the Python SDK a library Python developers genuinely enjoy using.

    Python developers reach Supabase from backend services, data pipelines, notebooks, and a growing number of AI agents and agent frameworks. The Python SDK is how all of them talk to the platform: database queries, auth, storage, realtime and edge functions, in both sync and async flavors.

    This is a library-authoring role, and the work happens in the open: public repos, public issue trackers, and API decisions whose consequences last for years. If you get satisfaction from a type checker that catches the bug before your users do, from an async client that cleans up after itself, and from an issue tracker that isn't a graveyard, you'll like it here.

    This role is ideal for someone who thrives in async, fast-paced environments and is excited about building developer tools that scale to millions.

    What You'll Be Responsible For

    • Build and evolve our Python SDK across database, auth, storage, realtime and functions, with both sync and async APIs that feel natural in Python.

    • Start new things. Alongside the maintenance work, find what's missing, make the case for it, and build it. Nobody will hand you the list.

    • Work in the open. Triage inbound issues, review and shepherd outside contributions, keep CI and release automation healthy, and hold a clear, kind line on scope.

    • Own the typing experience. Make the SDK fully typed and checker-friendly, so autocomplete and static analysis stay correct in real codebases.

    • Keep us honest across environments. Multiple Python versions, sync and async frameworks, workers, serverless and notebooks — with dependency ranges that don't fight our users' projects. Test it, don't assume it.

    • Work on realtime. Long-lived websocket connections, reconnection, and subscription and presence semantics.

    • Track the platform. When auth, realtime, storage, postgrest or edge functions ship a change, make sure the SDK reflects it, and that we say so in the changelog.

    • Design in public. Write the RFCs, migration guides, release notes and docs. Treat a breaking change as a deprecation path plus a migration story, not a version bump.

    • Make our SDK legible to AI coding agents, which are now a major consumer of it.

    You Might Be a Good Fit If You

    • Know modern Python typing deeply. You use generics, protocols, overloads and TypedDict as design tools, run strict type checking, and fix type errors without reaching for Any.

    • Have authored or maintained a published library with a public API and users outside your own company. You know what it costs to change something people depend on.

    • Know the maintainer side of open source. Issue triage at volume, reviewing outside PRs, release automation, changelogs and semver, security reports, and saying no to a feature request without losing the person who asked.

    • Are fluent in asyncio and have opinions on how a library should serve both sync and async callers.

    • Know Python packaging for libraries: pyproject.toml, wheels, modern tooling, version support policy, and why libraries shouldn't pin their dependencies tightly.

    • Test as a habit: unit, type-level and end-to-end against a real stack. Every fix comes with a regression test.

    • Write well. Design docs, changelogs and issue replies are a large part of the output here, much of it public, and we're async by default.

    • Have real autonomy: you find the important work, scope it, ship it, and say what you didn't do.

    • Communicate clearly across both technical and non-technical audiences

    • Have experience in async or globally distributed teams

    • Are energized by solving real-world problems for developers

    • Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and moving quickly

    Bonus points if you: have run a major-version migration for a public package, know your way around JWT/OAuth, have built integrations for AI agent frameworks, or have generated idiomatic clients from a schema.

    What We Offer

    • Fully Remote

      We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

    • ESOP

      Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

    • Tech Allowance

      Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

    • Health Benefits

      Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

    • Annual Off-Sites

      Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

    • Flexible Work

      We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

    • Professional Development

      Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

    About the Team

    Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

    • ~400 team members

    • 60+ countries

    • 20+ languages spoken

    • Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

    • 540,000+ community members

    We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

    #7807. DevOps Engineer (Vac DST) at Institute of Free Technology

    <h3 id="About-Logos-λ" class="part" data-startline="3" data-endline="3">About Logos (λ)</h3> <p class="part" data-startline="5" data-endline="5"><a href="https://logos.co/">Logos (λ)</a> is a movement of technologists, creatives, strategists, and activists working together to build a sovereign, people-owned technology stack that revitalises civil society and protects digital liberties at scale.</p> <p class="part" data-startline="7" data-endline="7">This position is within the IFT Technical Services (IFT-TS) Distributed Systems Testing (DST) Team, a team that primarily supports Logos, while also contributing to other IFT projects.</p> <h3 id="The-role" class="part" data-startline="9" data-endline="9">The role</h3> <p class="part" data-startline="11" data-endline="12">We are seeking an experienced Distributed Systems Evaluation Engineer to join the Institute of Free Technology - Technical Services, on the Distributed Systems Testing (IFT-TS DST) team.     #7808. Senior Software Engineer, API Products at 0x

    COMPANY

    At 0x, our mission is to "Create a Tokenized World Where All Forms of Value Can Flow Freely". We deliver this to web3 builders with a delightfully simple suite of developer APIs that provide faster trading, better prices, and superior user experience.

Our investors include Greylock Partners, Pantera Capital, and Jump Crypto.

    When you join 0x, you’ll become part of a veteran team of crypto builders who know the importance of code quality, team cohesion, and a culture of learning.

    About the team

    Behind every 0x trade is a lot of hard work. We integrate hundreds of liquidity sources, optimize for gas, slippage and latency, split routes and simulate every result to get traders the best possible outcome. The API Products team turns that routing logic into easy-to-integrate APIs that wallets, exchanges, trading apps, bridges and developers around the world rely on.

    This team of four built, maintains and continues to evolve our flagship Swap, Gasless and Cross-Chain APIs, which have moved billions in volume. Next, they’re building new products that give developers even more tools for building trading apps.

    • Investigate failed trades and stuck transfers using logs, traces and onchain data, and resolve the root cause.

    #7810. Revenue Accounting Manager at LiveKit

    About LiveKit

    LiveKit is building the infrastructure layer for the voice-driven era of computing. Founded in 2021, LiveKit powers voice AI applications for OpenAI, xAI, Salesforce, Coursera, Spotify, and thousands of others, collectively facilitating billions of calls each year.

    About You

    Our revenue is credit-denominated and consumption-based — usage billing, prepaid credit drawdown, and concurrency pricing across a growing enterprise base — which is close to the most complex revenue profile in infrastructure software. The load-bearing questions are open and there are few set policies and processes.

    You will be the technical authority on revenue. You will own the entire Order to Cash cycle, from interfacing with our sales team, reviewing contracts, assessing terms in accordance with ASC 606, implementing workpapers and schedules, documenting conclusions, and assisting with billing and collection efforts. If building processes from square one and taking ownership of some of the most complex revenue arrangements excites you, this is the role for you.

    You'll thrive as a Revenue Accounting Manager if you:

    • are obsessed with making the best product for our customers

    • are known as the go-to person for tackling tough problems

    • work hard and can build and ship fast

    • are focused on polish, detail and quality

    • are a fast learner, frequently picking up new tips, tricks, and skills

    The best way to impress us is with thoughtful ways you'd implement LiveKit, and potentially tinkering with it 😊

    What You'll Do

    • Author our revenue accounting policy set — principal versus agent on brokered inference, breakage on expiring credits, performance obligation identification and allocation, minimum commitment versus overage, and contract modifications — and make each one stand up to an external auditor

    • Own the monthly revenue close: recognition, the deferred revenue rollforward, contract asset and liability balances, and the flux analysis

    • Own the revenue portion of our external audit, from the PBC list through the technical positions

    • Replace manual revenue entries with an automated billing-to-revenue reconciliation and a documented exception process — revenue should be computed by a system and reviewed by a person, with invoice accuracy above 99% and root-cause tracking on the misses

    • Govern order to cash: billing accuracy, dispute resolution, and an AR and collections process with real DSO and aging targets

    • Own commission capitalization and amortization under ASC 340-40

    • Review non-standard deal structures before they're signed, so the accounting consequence is a decision rather than a discovery

    Who You Are

    • You're a licensed CPA with a Big 4 or comparable technical accounting foundation, plus real industry experience in a high-growth startup environment

    • You have demonstrable ASC 606 experience with usage-based or consumption revenue

    • You write technical memos that a reviewer can follow without a meeting: facts, guidance, analysis, conclusion

    • Experience assessing GAAP guidance and implementing it in real-world situations

    • You've owned a monthly revenue close hands-on, and you've closed revenue through at least one external audit

    • You're comfortable being the technical authority without a bench behind you

    Nice to Have

    • Billing system implementation or migration — Metronome, Stripe, Zuora

    • Pre-IPO or first-audit experience

    • Enough SQL to validate a recognition schedule against raw usage data yourself

    Our Commitment to You

    • An opportunity to build something truly impactful to the world

    • Contribute to open source alongside world-class engineers

    • Competitive salary and equity package

    • Flexible vacation policy

    #7811. Customer Growth Specialist at Uscreen

    What we're doing at Uscreen

    Uscreen is the video monetization platform for businesses building recurring revenue from video content. Our customers replace a patchwork stack of separate hosting, community, courses, email, and app vendors with one branded experience across web, mobile, and TV.

    The platform is only half of it. We believe growth requires behavior, not just software. Customers don't stall because a feature is missing. They stall because they priced wrong, never built an email list, or launched an offer nobody wanted. Coaching is how we fix that, and it's part of the product rather than an add-on.

    The job

    You own a segment.

    We have a cohort of smaller video businesses where retention is not where it should be. Nobody owns it today. You will. That means the revenue that stays in that segment, and the revenue that grows inside it, is yours. Your lemonade stand, your numbers, your call on how to run it.

    This is a coaching role measured in dollars. You'll be accountable for gross revenue retention in your cohort first, and expansion second. Not tickets closed, not calls booked.

    It's also a one-to-many job. Your cohort is larger than a 1:1 book, so the work is designing coaching that scales: cohort programs, group sessions, office hours, triggered outreach when the data says a business is drifting. You'll build that motion rather than inherit it.

    What you'll do

    • Own gross revenue retention and expansion for an assigned segment of customers
    • Find the pattern in why businesses in your cohort stall, and build coaching that addresses it at scale rather than one conversation at a time
    • Run group coaching sessions, cohort programs, and asynchronous coaching inside Membership+
    • Coach customers through the decisions that move revenue: pricing, packaging, positioning, offer design, email and funnel strategy, retention mechanics
    • Read your own data. Spot the accounts drifting before they churn, and know which interventions actually worked
    • Build the playbooks and session formats that make this repeatable. You're the first person in this seat, and what you build becomes how we scale it
    • Bring what you learn back to Product and Success. You'll be closer to this segment than anyone at the company.

    Who we're looking for

    Someone who has grown something online and can explain how. Maybe you ran marketing for a small business, coached founders, built your own audience, or worked inside a membership or subscription business. What matters is that you can sit with an operator, see the real constraint, and say the honest thing.

    • 3 to 5 years in coaching, consulting, customer success, or digital marketing, with real ownership of an outcome
    • Comfortable advising solo operators and small teams on how to grow
    • Strong live facilitation: group sessions, workshops, webinars, 1:1 calls
    • Can read a dashboard and form a point of view on retention curves, cohort behavior, and funnel conversion
    • Working fluency with AI tools for research, prep, and synthesis. Our team uses Claude daily to prep sessions, analyze customer data, and build coaching assets. You don't need to be technical, but you should reach for these tools rather than around them.
    • Motivated by being paid on results. A third of this package is variable and there's no ceiling on it.
    • Clear, simple communication. Complex idea in, plain sentence out.

    Bonus points if you:

    • Have been a creator, coach, or small business owner yourself
    • Have worked with video businesses, online educators, or subscription and membership businesses
    • Know the surrounding stack: Kit/ConvertKit, YouTube, Kajabi, Circle, Squarespace, Meta and IG
    • Have built a scaled or one-to-many customer program before

    What's hard about this job

    We'd rather you know now.

    • You'll own a number you can't fully control. Some customers won't take the advice and will churn anyway. You have to be able to own influence over an outcome rather than the outcome itself.
    • It's a lagging metric. Retention moves slowly. Your first two quarters are guaranteed at target because of this, but you'll be doing the work for months before the number reflects it.
    • The motion doesn't exist yet. Playbooks exist for our 1:1 coaching. The scaled version for this segment is an open question you'll help answer. If you need a finished playbook on day one, this will frustrate you.
    • You'll deliver honest assessments to people emotionally invested in what they've built. That takes a specific kind of nerve.

    What we offer

    • 100% remote first company
    • Flexible PTO + 8 company holidays
    • Remote, global team
    • Paid parental leave
    • Medical, dental, and vision (US Based)
    • Technology and home office stipend
    • Coworking space stipend
    • Annual learning stipend

    Note: Benefits may vary depending on location and local regulations. Final eligibility and coverage details will be shared during the hiring process.

    About the Role

    Supabase manages millions of Postgres instances and is growing. We have strong teams across observability, release engineering, and incident management — and we're concentrating our reliability efforts into a dedicated SRE practice that ties the discipline together across the platform.

    You'll be embedded within Service Operations, and your primary job is to make every engineering team more reliable — not by owning their infrastructure, but by establishing the practices, frameworks, and feedback loops that let them own reliability themselves. You'll work across the org: sometimes setting the standard, sometimes pair-programming a fix, sometimes helping a team define their error budget, sometimes telling them it's exhausted.

    This role is ideal for someone who has a strong vision for how SRE should work and thrives in async, fast-paced environments where influence matters more than authority.

    What You'll Own

    • Partner with service teams to define meaningful SLIs and SLOs grounded in customer experience, and build the error budget policies that turn them into engineering decisions

    • Own and evolve the Operational Readiness Review (ORR) process — conducting reviews for new services and major changes across observability, alerting, runbooks, capacity, and graceful degradation

    • Strengthen the incident-to-improvement pipeline: connecting postmortem findings to operational readiness gaps, identifying repeat failure patterns, and driving systemic fixes

    • Act as the reliability expert teams pull in for architecture reviews, failure mode analysis, dependency mapping, and resilience design

    • Identify and quantify operational toil across the org, and build or advocate for automation that eliminates it

    • Help teams design sustainable on-call practices: alert quality, escalation paths, runbook coverage, and noise reduction

    • Track and report on org-wide operational maturity, surfacing systemic gaps and driving remediation

    You Might Be a Good Fit If You

    • Have 7+ years of experience in SRE, production engineering, or reliability-focused roles, including experience shaping SRE practices and driving adoption across engineering teams

    • Have a software engineering mindset — you write code and build tools, not just configure them

    • Have hands-on experience defining and operationalizing SLOs/SLIs at scale, including error budget policies that actually influenced engineering decisions

    • Have deep experience with incident response, postmortem facilitation, and turning incident learnings into systemic improvements

    • Have worked with large-scale multi-tenant systems (bonus: managed database platforms or Postgres)

    • Are proficient with cloud infrastructure (AWS preferred) and infrastructure-as-code (Pulumi preferred, Terraform/CDK also acceptable)

    • Communicate clearly and persuasively — this role requires influencing without authority across a distributed org

    • Have experience in async or globally distributed teams

    • Are energized by making other teams more effective rather than being the one who fixes everything

    Nice to Have

    • Experience with Kubernetes-based platform operations

    • Familiarity with OpenTelemetry, VictoriaMetrics, Grafana, or similar observability tooling

    • Experience building developer-facing reliability tooling (SLO dashboards, ORR frameworks, toil tracking, DORA metrics)

    What We Offer

    • Fully Remote

      We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

    • ESOP

      Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

    • Tech Allowance

      Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

    • Health Benefits

      Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

    • Annual Off-Sites

      Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

    • Flexible Work

      We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

    • Professional Development

      Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

    About the Team

    Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

    • ~400 team members

    • 60+ countries

    • 20+ languages spoken

    • Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

    • 540,000+ community members

    We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

    #7877. YouTube now gets twice as much watch time on U.S. TVs as any other platform

    Each month, Nielsen breaks down U.S. watch time on TV screens across all major media distributors. That particular measurement is known as The Gauge, and in recent years, it has shown favorable results for YouTube. Google's video platform typically leads rivals like Netflix, Prime Video, and Disney+ by claiming the biggest watch time share of any single distributor.

    Even with that context in mind, in the August 2026 edition of The Gauge, YouTube had an especially strong showing. It claimed its biggest watch time share ever -- 15.8% -- as streaming platforms accounted for nearly half of the TV screen traffic recorded by Nielsen.

    Netflix ranked second in the latest Gauge report with a 7.7% share of U.S. watch time on TV screens.

    #7930. Mexico: Delay in Legal Reform Harms People With Disabilities

    Click to expand Image A dog and a woman with a disability outside the Supreme Court of Justice of the Nation in Mexico City, Mexico, March 24, 2026. © 2026 Gerardo Vieyra/NurPhoto via AP Photo

    (Mexico City) – Mexico’s Senate should amend legislation approved by the Chamber of Deputies on October 20, 2026, to ensure that people with disabilities can appoint supporters to make legal decisions by April 1, 2027, as originally planned, Human Rights Watch said today. The current legislation would postpone the deadline for implementing the National Code of Civil and Family Procedures, which requires these appointments, to July 1, 2031.

    The code, adopted in June 2023, introduced reforms to improve access to justice and guarantee the right of people with disabilities throughout Mexico to make legally recognized decisions. However, implementation has lagged. Mexico City is the only jurisdiction to have declared the code partially in force, including provisions allowing adults to designate supporters to help them exercise their legal capacity.

    “If the Senate fails to amend the legislation to allow the original deadline for appointing supporters to take effect, local judges will face a legal vacuum,” said Carlos Ríos Espinosa, associate disability rights director at Human Rights Watch. “While judges can no longer bar someone from making autonomous decisions, the procedure to help people make those decisions has not yet entered into force. This could leave people with disabilities at risk of losing their property or suffering harm to their health and physical integrity.”

    The 2023 code abolished interdiction, a discriminatory guardianship procedure that allowed courts to deprive adults, particularly people with intellectual or psychosocial disabilities, of their right to make legally recognized decisions. Under this system, guardians could make decisions concerning a person’s property, finances, and personal affairs, often without respecting the person’s will and preferences.

    Interdiction was abolished on June 8, 2023. Mexico’s Supreme Court has recognized that these procedures are incompatible with international human rights standards and that their repeal does not depend on the gradual implementation of the new code.

    However, abolishing interdiction without providing effective alternatives can leave some people without essential legal protections.

    The new code establishes an extraordinary judicial procedure for appointing supporters when a person’s will and preferences cannot be determined despite considerable efforts. This mechanism allows a judge to appoint someone to facilitate the exercise of legal capacity, subject to strict safeguards against abuse and requirements to ensure the best possible interpretation of the person’s will and preferences.

    Without this procedure, people may face serious consequences. Consider, for example, someone who falls into a coma without having left any advance directives concerning their property, health care, or family affairs. If urgent decisions are required, the absence of a legal mechanism to appoint supporters could put their property at risk or jeopardize their health and physical integrity.

    Unlike interdiction, the extraordinary appointment of supporters does not authorize courts to declare a person legally incapable or place them under a general guardianship regime that substitutes another person’s decisions for their own.

    International human rights law recognizes that everyone has the right to make decisions about their own lives and requires governments to ensure that people with disabilities have access to the support they require to do so.

    Organizations of people with disabilities, older people’s rights groups, professional associations, and human rights organizations have called for the extraordinary procedure for appointing supporters to be exempted from postponement.

    The Senate still has an opportunity to amend the legislation approved by the Chamber of Deputies and to introduce an exception allowing this procedure to be implemented as originally planned.

    “The government may reasonably require additional time and resources to implement a reform of this magnitude, but delaying a critical safeguard for people with disabilities for nearly five more years is unjustifiable,” Ríos Espinosa said. “The Senate still has an opportunity to correct this situation and prevent people from being exposed to abuse and serious violations of their rights.”

    #7931. ICC Wins EU Parliament’s Sakharov Prize

    Click to expand Image The International Criminal Court in The Hague, Netherlands, July 2025. © 2025 Nicolas Economou/NurPhoto via AP Photo

    (Brussels) – The European Parliament on October 22, 2026, awarded the Sakharov Prize for Freedom of Thought to the International Criminal Court (ICC) and its officials. 

    The following quote can be attributed to Philippe Dam, EU advocacy director at Human Rights Watch:

    “By awarding the Sakharov Prize to the ICC, the European Parliament recognizes the court’s vital role for victims of the gravest crimes around the world, at a time of unprecedented attack from the Trump administration and others. This prize should be a rallying cry for ICC members to stand up for the court and for the EU Commission to urgently activate the EU’s ‘blocking statute’ to protect the court and those supporting its work from effects of US sanctions.” 

    #7932. Tajik Activist Given Lengthy Prison Sentence

    Click to expand Image Asadullo Boboev. © Private

    A court in Tajikistan has sentenced Asadullo Boboev, an opposition activist deported from Germany in June, to 17 years in jail. Human Rights Watch and other groups previously warned the German authorities that activists like Boboev could face mistreatment and criminal prosecution on politically motivated charges if returned to Tajikistan.

    Boboev, 54, had lived in Germany with his family since 2017. He was involved in the Reforms and Development of Tajikistan movement, an opposition group critical of President Emomali Rahmon’s government. After Boboev was convicted under three articles of the criminal code, including “extremism through the internet,” a court sentenced Boboev on September 21, according to a media report. Tajik authorities have not published information on the case.

    Boboev, who was deported from Germany on June 20, previously told media that Tajik authorities had opened a criminal extremism case against him because of social media posts criticizing the Tajik authorities. He said that the German court gave him until July 1 to prove that such a case exists, but he was deported before this deadline.

    Boboev was deported to Dushanbe with his adult son, Muhammad. Boboev was detained on arrival.

    Boboev is not the first Tajikistani whom Germany has deported, despite human rights groups’ concerns. Three other opposition activists deported from Germany since 2023 were also imprisoned after being returned to Tajikistan. Abdullohi Shamsiddin, Bilol Qurbonaliev, and Dilmurod Ergashev were sentenced to 7, 10, and 8 years in prison, respectively, on charges that appear to be fabricated.

    International law prohibits refoulement—returning anyone to a country where they face a real risk of irreparable harm on account of serious human rights violations. While the German government has committed itself to countering governmental transnational repression, important gaps remain.

    Germany has continued to return Tajik nationals even as evidence mounts that doing so delivers critics directly to the hands of the repressive security services they had, in many cases, fled.

    German courts and immigration authorities should not readily deport dissidents to Tajikistan. German authorities should explain why Boboev was deported despite the concrete warnings of serious human rights risks and should press Tajikistan to disclose information about his conviction, his treatment, and ensure that he has access to independent legal counsel.

    #7933. Uganda: Ensure Justice for Killing of Rwandan Dissident

    Click to expand Image The funeral of of Sgt. Maj. Robert Kabera on October 21, 2026, in Kamengo, Uganda. © Private

    (Nairobi) – Ugandan authorities’ obligation to conduct an effective investigation into the brutal killing of a Rwandan dissident requires examining all evidence that points to a possible politically motivated killing, Human Rights Watch said today. Ugandan authorities should ensure a prompt, impartial, credible investigation that leads to accountability.

    The body of Sgt. Maj. Robert Kabera, a singer, former Rwanda Defence Force member, and outspoken critic of the Rwandan government, was found on October 17, 2026, in Luwala village near Kampala, where he was constructing a house. He had fled Rwanda in November 2020 and claimed asylum on political persecution grounds. He had reported to Human Rights Watch multiple times that he had been threatened and attacked in Uganda. In a September 2022 interview with Human Rights Watch, Kabera said: “They will kill me. They know where I am … I will die before I get resettled.”

    “It was clear from our conversations that Robert Kabera knew his criticism of the Rwandan government made him a target, and that he feared for his life,” said Clémentine de Montjoye, senior Great Lakes researcher at Human Rights Watch. “Ugandan authorities should investigate all possible motives for the killing and take immediate steps to protect Rwandan dissidents and refugees in the country.” 

    Kabera’s body was found beheaded and with other injuries, according to a relative and a photograph and three videos analyzed by Human Rights Watch. A photograph published by local media on October 18 shows Kabera’s decapitated naked body in a wooded area with blood on the ground next to it. The police reported that Kabera was found dead in a eucalyptus plantation on October 17. 

    Witnesses provided three videos that showed Kabera’s body being transferred from a vehicle onto a trolley in a hospital. A forensic medical expert told Human Rights Watch that Kabera’s body was likely mutilated after he died, the unnatural position of his shoulders seen in the images and the ligature marks on his wrists suggested that his hands were tied behind his back when he died. According to a document seen by Human Rights Watch, the Ugandan police requested a postmortem medical examination, the results of which are not yet available. 

    There are many reasons to treat Kabera’s death as suspicious, Human Rights Watch said, and foremost that he was an outspoken critic of the Rwandan government. In a video published on YouTube days before his death, titled “Kill Us if You Want,” he spoke about the Rwandan ruling party’s alleged crimes, as well as the threats and persecution he had faced since fleeing to Uganda.

    A relative told Human Rights Watch that a man speaking Kinyarwanda, who presented himself as a political opponent in need of assistance, had traveled to the construction site with Kabera on the afternoon of October 16. “[Kabera] called me around 11 p.m., and after that no longer picked up the phone,” the relative said. “The man he was with gave me a fake number to call him. The next day, when I couldn’t reach [Kabera], his number didn’t go through.”  

    On November 23, 2020, a news release by the Rwanda Defence Force Military Prosecution Department announced an investigation into “defilement allegations” against Kabera, and alleged that the crime was committed on November 21, 2020, two days after Kabera said he left the country.

    In a May 2023 letter to Human Rights Watch, the Ugandan police inspector general confirmed that Kabera was granted asylum in Uganda on the basis of his claims of political persecution, was being provided with additional security by the police and security forces, and was in the final stages of resettlement to a third country. 

    Kabera faced arrest and multiple physical attacks in Uganda. On May 16, 2022, dozens of Ugandan police and military men arrested Kabera at his temporary home in Kampala, reportedly on charges of smuggling weapons. “They were going to take me away to give me to Rwanda like a gift,” he told Human Rights Watch in 2022. He was eventually taken into custody. Kabera said Ugandan police officers beat him and fractured his ankle. A witness who saw Kabera after his release confirmed his injury. 

    During his detention, Kabera said he saw Rwandan officials at the police station, including some who work at the High Commission in Kampala. Kabera was released on bail on May 20 and moved to a new location in Uganda. His arrest warrant was later canceled, and his case closed.

    In late July 2022, a group of young men assaulted him and took his phone. “I couldn’t fight because I was still injured from when I was beaten in detention,” he told Human Rights Watch. “The men said in Kinyarwanda: ‘we know where you are. We will take you back home, you will see.’” Kabera reported the incident to the police on August 1, 2022.

    In October 2023, three men attacked him and told him: “You are lying, even if you go to America we will find you and kill you,” he told authorities, based on a report Human Rights Watch reviewed.

    Rwandan authorities openly—and reportedly also privately—included Kabera in demands to Ugandan authorities to deport several high-profile government opponents to Rwanda. In a September 14, 2021 police statement reviewed by Human Rights Watch and written in response to a protection request, a Kampala city official stated that Kabera “needs help for protection because we cannot offer him the needed security” because “his country is putting him in newspapers that he is training terrorists (rebels).” The official asked the United Nations refugee agency and the Norwegian Refugee Council to ensure his safety and security. He has been repeatedly featured in online posts about opponents in Uganda who were critical of the Rwandan government.

    It is tragic that despite multiple warnings and official recognition by Ugandan security forces and the UN of the risks he faced, Kabera remained in Uganda exposed to threats and attacks, Human Rights Watch said.

    Rwanda and Uganda are both parties to the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights and to the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights, which impose obligations to guarantee the right to life (articles 4 and 6 respectively). The treaties include an absolute prohibition on arbitrary deprivation of life and a strict obligation to conduct an independent, impartial, prompt, transparent, and thorough investigation into a suspicious death that is capable of establishing the facts and identifying those responsible. Failure to conduct such an investigation itself constitutes a violation of the right to life. 

    In a 2023 report, Human Rights Watch documented that Rwandan authorities and their proxies are using violence, judicial mechanisms, and intimidation to silence criticism from Rwandans living around the world. Human Rights Watch documented over a dozen killings, kidnappings or attempted kidnappings, enforced disappearances, and physical attacks targeting Rwandans living abroad. 

    Uganda and other host countries should protect Rwandan refugees and asylum seekers, enhance security for those reporting threats or abuse, and thoroughly investigate all reported incidents to ensure accountability.

    UN agencies should strengthen existing refugee resettlement programs by including resources to address the threat of extraterritorial abuses against refugees by agents or proxies of country-of-origin governments known for such abuses, including Rwanda.

    Robert Kabera’s brutal killing is a reminder of the gravity of the risks faced by Rwandan refugees,” de Montjoye said. “Governments and regional and international institutions should hold Rwanda accountable for its human rights violations at home and abroad.

    #7934. Mali: Army, Russia’s ‘Africa Corps’ Commit New Abuses

    Click to expand Image Aftermath of the apparent Malian military drone strike on September 16, 2026, in Guélédié village, Mopti region, Mali. Screenshots from video analyzed and geolocated by Human Rights Watch. © Private

    (Nairobi) – Mali’s armed forces and the allied Russia-controlled Africa Corps have committed new atrocities against civilians in central Mali during renewed fighting, Human Rights Watch said today. 

    On September 16, 2026, an apparent military drone strike in Guélédié village, Mopti region, killed eight civilians and injured at least six others. Between May and August, Malian and Africa Corps forces held numerous detainees inhumanely in a metal container at an army base in Sévaré town, Mopti region, where many were tortured, including with severe beatings, electric shock, and waterboarding. 

    “Civilians are bearing the brunt of the Mali junta’s failure to rein in its abusive forces and allies,” said Ilaria Allegrozzi, senior Sahel researcher at Human Rights Watch. “Recent atrocities implicate the Malian army as well as Russia-controlled Africa Corps, for which Russian officials should also be held accountable.”

    The Malian army’s offensive followed coordinated attacks across Mali by the Al Qaeda-linked Group for the Support of Islam and Muslims (Jama’at Nusrat al-Islam wal-Muslimin, JNIM) and the Azawad Liberation Front (Front de Libération de l’Azawad, FLA) in late April. Malian forces and the allied Africa Corps killed civilians, including many children, and destroyed civilian property and health facilities in airstrikes and other attacks.

    Human Rights Watch remotely interviewed fifteen people between August and September, including three witnesses in Guélédié, four former detainees at the Sévaré military base, and eight members of nongovernmental organizations. Human Rights Watch sent letters to the Malian and Russian defense ministers in early October but received no responses.

    Guélédié

    On September 16 at about 10 a.m., an apparent military drone delivered a munition on civilians on the outskirts of Guélédié village, killing eight men, ages 25 to 40, and injuring six others. The villagers had gathered around a tree, where a man was providing paid internet access via Starlink. Residents said JNIM fighters often used this service to support operations. 

    Witnesses said a drone flew over the village for about one hour before delivering its munition, suggesting that the area was being surveilled. “The drone passed over the village at about 9 a.m. and came back at least four times,” said a 40-year-old man. “It was white and had long, thin, straight wings. It would spin around, disappear, and come back again.” This description is broadly consistent with larger drones the Malian government is known to use. 

    Witnesses said that JNIM has a base in Guélédié and over 100 JNIM fighters were present in the village at the time of the strike, although no JNIM fighters were among the casualties. They also said JNIM fighters alerted the local population via WhatsApp about the possibility of a drone strike at the Starlink location, before leaving the area along with many civilians. Witnesses said that only 14 civilians had remained at the site, continuing to use the connection, when the drone delivered its munition.

    “The explosion was deafening, really strong,” said a 30-year-old villager. “I saw very thick black smoke rising from the Starlink connection area.”

    A 52-year-old health worker said he went to the scene about an hour later and found six men dead and seven injured “with their bodies burned,” one of whom died there. He said he called the Ténenkou military base commander and that he would take the injured to the Ténenkou Referral Health Center there. “But he did not only categorically refuse our evacuating them,” the health worker said. “But he also warned me not to provide them with any support. For him, they were JNIM fighters and he told me that the drone could not have targeted civilians.”

    The injured were eventually evacuated. In a September 24 statement, Doctors Without Borders (Médecins Sans Frontières, MSF) said its staff at the health center treated four “seriously injured” people with “burns over their entire bodies,” and one “died shortly after arrival.”

    Human Rights Watch verified a video shared by witnesses of the aftermath of the strike and geolocated it to an area about 600 meters northeast of the village center. It shows the bodies of three men, two under a burned and broken tree and one in a nearby pond. Two burned motorcycles and a destroyed solar panel are also visible.

    In a September 17 statement, the armed forces chief of staff said that on September 16, military units “spotted terrorist elements concealed under vegetation northeast of Guélédié” with “war equipment and satellite communication devices,” after which they “neutralized” them and destroyed their equipment.

    Mali’s junta has increasingly used airstrikes against armed groups. Human Rights Watch, other human rights groups, and severalmedia and investigative organizations have documented airstrikes that killed civilians. 

    Sévaré

    The Malian military and the Africa Corps have taken hundreds of civilians into custody during counterinsurgency operations across Mali and unlawfully detained many in military bases. Human Rights Watch, other human rights groups, and the media have documented torture at several bases.

    On May 7, soldiers and Africa Corps fighters apprehended a 30-year-old civilian at his home in Guirowel, a village in a JNIM-controlled area in Mopti region. On July 4, they arrested three Quranic students, ages 32, 37, and 40, in Thy village, Mopti region, following a JNIM attack on a military position there.

    The four men later said the forces took them to the Hamadoun Bocary Barry army base in Sévaré, holding them incommunicado in a shipping container about 20 meters long with no windows, toilet, or washing facilities, in extreme heat without ventilation, alongside dozens of other detainees. The men said they received a small amount of poor-quality food once a day, were denied necessary medical care, and were verbally abused.

    “We got a small piece of bread and sometimes beans and half a bottle of water once a day,” said the 32-year-old man. “The heat was unbearable. On the first day, we were 27, but over the days we reached more than 70 people. We relieved ourselves in a bucket, it was always full and spilling onto the floor. They didn’t empty it.”

    The men said they identified Malian soldiers by their uniforms and languages, including French, Bambara, and Fulfulde. They said Africa Corps fighters had “light skin” and spoke a foreign language they did not understand. 

    Three of the men said Malian soldiers and Africa Corps fighters repeatedly tortured them in a room in a building near the base entrance, alongside Sévaré’s main road, so they could hear traffic sounds. The room had a metal bed, a barrel filled with water, a table, and electric wires on the floor, where the men said they were tortured and interrogated to confess to their alleged association with JNIM. 

    The man from Guirowel said Malian and Africa Corps personnel tortured him twice for about two hours each. “I almost lost my life when two Russian soldiers drowned me in a barrel of water,” he said. “Every time I lost consciousness, I was resuscitated with an electric shock and drowned again. “They burned my buttocks with hot plastic, and they lit a cloth on my back. They forced me to give information about JNIM, and to admit I was a fighter.”

    The 37-year-old man said a Malian soldier tied him to a bed and demanded: “Tell us everything or we’ll kill you.” Then “the blows started raining down on my buttocks and back. ‘Talk!’ they said to me. [A] soldier choked my neck and banged my head against the bed. I don't remember anything after that.”

    The 40-year-old man said soldiers tied his feet to a bed and threatened to kill him if he withheld information about JNIM fighters. “I was hit on my buttocks, fire [put] on my back,” he said. “A soldier held my penis and put electricity on it,” while another “poured water on my back and hit me.”

    The men said several detainees died in the container following torture. “When a prisoner was about to die, we would bang on the door to alert the guards,” said the 40-year-old man. “They told us to inform them only when a person died. They had no mercy. Eight people died during my detention.”

    The military released the three men from Thy on July 20 and the man from Guirowel on August 6. Relatives reportedly paid the military 1.8 million CFA francs (about US$3,000) for the three Thy men and 1 million CFA (about $1,800) for the Guirowel man to secure their release.

    All parties to Mali’s armed conflict are bound by international humanitarian law, notably Common Article 3 and Protocol II to the Geneva Conventions of 1949 as well as customary laws of war. Parties must take all feasible precautions before an attack, including verifying that the target is a military objective. Attacks with no military objective or that do not distinguish between civilians and military personnel are unlawfully indiscriminate. 

    Anyone deprived of their liberty must be treated humanely in all circumstances and protected against murder, torture, and cruel, humiliating, and degrading treatment. Denying medical care to the wounded or detainees can be a war crime. Individuals who commit serious violations of the laws of war with criminal intent or as a matter of command responsibility may be prosecuted for war crimes.

    In March, the United Nations Human Rights Council renewed the mandate of the independent expert on Mali to assess the human rights situation and assist national authorities in promoting human rights and the rule of law.

    “These recent abuses should serve as a stark reminder that concerned governments and international institutions should press the Malian authorities to conduct credible investigations into military abuses and bring those responsible to justice,” Allegrozzi said. “The UN Human Rights Council should increase its scrutiny of the situation in Mali and take steps to prevent further abuses."

    #7935. Burkina Faso Restores Death Penalty

    Click to expand Image Burkina Faso soldiers patrol aboard a pickup truck on the road from Dori to the Goudebo refugee camp, on February 3, 2020. © 2020 OLYMPIA DE MAISMONT/AFP via Getty Images

    Burkina Faso’s unelected parliament has unanimously adopted a new penal code that restores the death penalty for high treason, terrorism, acts of espionage, parricide and importation of expired or prohibited products. The country had abolished the death penalty for ordinary crimes in 2018 while its last known judicial execution took place in 1988.

    Reinstating capital punishment sends a chilling message to critics of the military junta and risks becoming another tool to suppress dissent. Since the September 2022 military coup, Burkina Faso’s junta has steadily dismantled civic and political rights. Authorities have banned media outlets, suspended unions and civil society organizations, abolished multiparty politics, and subjected critics to arbitrary detention, enforced disappearances, unlawful conscription, and torture.

    Justice Minister Edasso Rodrigue Bayala defended the decision to reinstate the death penalty as a necessary measure to combat terrorism. He said that armed groups exploited the previous law’s leniency by promising new recruits they would face no more than life imprisonment if convicted of terrorist acts.

    Human Rights Watch opposes all capital punishment because of its inherent cruelty. The unfairness of death sentences in Burkina Faso would be exacerbated by the country’s ill-equipped justice system. All terrorism-related cases are handled by the Specialized Judicial Unit Against Terrorism-Related Crimes, established in 2017. Human Rights Watch documented in April the special court’s slow investigations and serious procedural failures that undermine defendants’ fair trial rights.

    A former terrorism court judge once explained to me that judges sometimes kept suspects in detention despite insufficient evidence, fearing the military might summarily execute them if released. He described the dilemma as “better to violate the right to liberty in order to preserve the right to life.” With the death penalty’s return, even that grim calculation may no longer hold.

    Reversing course on the death penalty runs counter to a broader international movement toward abolition. The United Nations General Assembly and the African Commission on Human and Peoples’ Rights have urged states to establish moratoriums on executions, narrow the scope of capital punishment, and progressively reduce the number of offenses punishable by death with a view to its eventual abolition. 

    Restoring capital punishment will not resolve Burkina Faso’s security crisis. In a system already plagued by serious due process failures, the death penalty merely heightens the risk of irreversible injustice.

    #7936. Vietnam: Independent Trade Unions Suppressed

    Click to expand Image A worker walks past a sign with the Vietnamese Communist Party flag in Hanoi, October 5, 2020. © 2020 NHAC NGUYEN/AFP via Getty Images The Vietnamese government is systematically suppressing the rights of workers to organize independent unions despite trade agreements that require it to support labor rights.Officials have repeatedly made false, misleading, and deceptive claims about supporting independent unions while making clear that they would target anyone who tried to exercise labor rights. The government routinely used criminal laws to prosecute rights activists.Vietnam’s government needs to carry out reforms it pledged to implement. Its trading partners need to face reality and make clear to the government that its broken pledges on labor rights will carry consequences.

    (New York) ‒ The Vietnamese government is systematically suppressing the rights of workers to organize independent unions, Human Rights Watch said in a report released today. Despite pledges to trading partners in several free trade agreements, the government has failed to put in place meaningful reforms to enable trade unions to form and operate freely, and it routinely represses independent labor organizing. 

    October 21, 2026 “There Are No Trade Unions in Vietnam”

    Hanoi’s Continuing Assault on Workers’ Right to Organize

    Download the full report in English

    Download the Summary & Recommendations in Vietnamese

    The 49-page report, “There Are No Independent Trade Unions in Vietnam: Hanoi’s Continuing Assault on Workers’ Right to Organize,” documents the Vietnamese governments’ continuing use of vague, overbroad criminal laws to suppress the activities of human rights and labor rights defenders, while maintaining an atmosphere of repression to thwart independent labor organizing. The government has issued decrees and articles in state-run media that reaffirm that all labor organizations in Vietnam are under the government’s authority, and portray independent trade unions as dangerous, anti-state entities. 

    “Officials in Hanoi have repeatedly made false, misleading, and deceptive claims about labor rights, while lauding Vietnam’s supposed reforms to suggest they allow unions to form,” said John Sifton, Asia advocacy director at Human Rights Watch. “In reality, the Vietnamese government remains deeply hostile to the very idea of independent trade unions, which is why none exist.” 

    Human Rights Watch interviewed more than 30 workers, human rights organizers, labor rights and legal experts, and others familiar with the labor rights situation in Vietnam. It also analyzed Vietnamese laws and regulations and reviewed articles about labor issues in Vietnamese state-run media.

    Vietnamese officials have repeatedly stated publicly that legal reforms in 2021 allow workers to organize and that Vietnam has thousands of “grassroots” trade unions. These claims have primarily been directed at trading partners in the context of various economic agreements. They include the 2020 European Union-Vietnam Free Trade Agreement (EVFTA), later agreements with the United Kingdom and European Free Trade Association, and the 2019 Comprehensive and Progressive Agreement for Trans-Pacific Partnership (CPTPP), which includes Canada, Australia, the United Kingdom, Japan, Brazil, and Mexico, among other countries. 

    These agreements obligated Vietnam to undertake reforms that would allow independent trade unions to exist. In 2021, the government made some legislative changes that purportedly authorized the creation of independent “worker organizations,” but subsequently failed to create any regulatory framework that would allow workers to form them.

    The only legal mechanisms that Vietnamese workers can utilize to address labor rights concerns exist within entities, or so-called “trade unions,” set up under the authority of the Vietnam General Confederation of Labor (VGCL). Under Vietnamese law, the confederation and its sub-units explicitly fall under the control of the ruling Communist Party of Vietnam. Party officials choose or approve leaders at every level, and the government provides their budget, which is funded by mandatory fees paid by employers and workers. These worker entities do not meet legal criteria under international law to be considered independent trade unions, Human Rights Watch said. 

    While VGCL entities sometimes engage in activities ostensibly addressing labor issues, their efforts are limited and do not genuinely represent the workers, and they ultimately act in the interests of the Communist Party. None of these entities has ever called for a strike or led any confrontational activities against employers.

    “A union has to have its own freedom, and freedom to grow,” one exiled labor organizer told Human Rights Watch. “But the party remains the leadership of all things in Vietnam.”

    Even if independent worker entities could form in Vietnam, existing provisions of Vietnamese labor laws do not meet international standards and are inadequate to protect labor rights, Human Rights Watch said.

    The government’s intolerance for independent trade unions has harmed other labor rights of Vietnamese workers, Human Rights Watch said. Without the freedom to form independent unions, workers in Vietnam are less able to bargain effectively or seek higher wages, better working conditions, or remedies or redress for individual and collective work-related grievances.

    Workers said that even if the government adopted procedures to allow independent labor organizations to form, authorities would misuse provisions in Vietnam’s penal code to target organizers. The government has long used criminal laws to prosecute labor rights and human rights activists and suppress the creation of independent organizations. 

    For decades, the authorities have imprisoned prominent independent journalists, bloggers, and activists, several of whom have advocated labor rights. At any given time in the last 10 years, Vietnam had in detention at least 150 to 180 political prisoners, either awaiting trial or imprisoned after unfair convictions. Repression has increased more recently: in 2025 and 2026 alone, the authorities have jailed over 50 dissidents.

    Authorities have specifically targeted human rights defenders and labor organizers who raised rights issues in the context of the 2021 trade agreement with the EU, and even jailed some of its own officials involved in negotiations with the EU on labor rights issues.
