    Numerous Vietnamese-language statements and articles by officials reveal the negative views and deep hostility of the authorities toward independent trade unions. Officials cited in government-controlled media outlets portray independent trade unions as “reactionary” forces opposed to the Communist Party, engaged in “subversive” and “sinister” plots to undermine and sabotage the government,” with the “true objective” of undermining government-controlled unions, “confronting the State,” and “sowing social instability.” The heading over one such article is “Crime Warnings.”

    The Vietnamese government should ratify and put into effect all relevant International Labour Organisation treaties without delay and set a clear timeline for when it will allow genuinely independent trade unions to form, Human Rights Watch said. Vietnam’s trading partners should press the government to meet these commitments and release all human rights and labor rights activists imprisoned for exercising their right to free expression. 

    “Many of Vietnam’s trading partners have accepted Hanoi’s claims of reform without scrutiny, and some still contend that trade agreements have improved Vietnam’s rights record,” Sifton said. “Vietnam’s trading partners need to face reality and make clear to the government that its broken pledges on labor rights carry consequences.”

    #7937. US: Order ‘No ICE’ Zones during Election Period

    Click to expand Image A voter carries a ballot during early voting in the New York City mayoral elections on October 25, 2025. © 2025 Olga Fedorova/AP Photo

    (Washington, DC) – The United States government should order federal Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) officers not to operate in the vicinity of polling places, early voting locations, or drop boxes during the midterm electoral period, Human Rights Watch said today. The presence of these federal agents could deter people from voting. 

    “ICE officers’ abuses have already seriously affected US citizens who have been stopped, beaten, detained, deported, and even killed,” said Philippe Bolopion, executive director at Human Rights Watch. “Voters should not have to fear being caught in an ICE dragnet when they head to the polls.”

    In-person early voting is already taking place in the majority of states. In a nationwide September poll, 40 percent of respondents said they expect some kind of ICE presence at polls, with even higher percentages among non-white respondents expecting to see ICE agents in places people vote. 

    Federal law generally prohibits federal officials from sending “troops or armed men” to voting sites. Several state laws prohibit any armed officers, including police, from engaging in acts that could interfere with the elections or intimidate voters by their presence near polling places. Some states bar law enforcement officers, including police, from being in close proximity to polling places at all unless they are responding to a specific incident.

    Despite these robust prohibitions, neither President Donald Trump nor the heads of the US Department of Homeland Security (DHS) and the Federal Bureau of Investigation have been willing to rule out the possibility that federal agents, including ICE, may be at places where people vote.

    In March, then-Deputy Attorney General and now-Attorney General Todd Blanche said, “Why is there an objection to sending ICE to polling stations? ... Illegals can’t vote. It doesn’t make any sense.” In February, Karoline Leavitt, who was White House press secretary at the time, said she could not “guarantee” that ICE agents won’t be around a polling location in November.

    In early September, DHS Secretary Markwayne Mullin said there were no plans to station ICE agents at polling places but left the door open for them to be there if “serving a warrant on someone that we have been actively tracking down” or if “there is a threat to that polling place.”

    Three lawsuits are pending in federal courts to prevent ICE from operating at the polls, with an additional court case seeking to require the government to disclose its plans regarding ICE agents at polling places. In Washington, DC, the Board of Elections in October asked the National Guard to limit their presence near drop box locations for the midterm election following complaints that troops had been spotted near a drop box in Foggy Bottom. 

    The Trump administration has long touted the idea that large numbers of noncitizens unlawfully cast ballots in US elections but has not produced any evidence suggesting that the problem exists at a scale that would have affected the result of any election, anywhere in the country. Multiple research studies, including from the Fair Elections Center, Bipartisan Policy Center, Brennan Center, and the Cato Institute, have found no evidence of mass voting by noncitizens and concluded that the problem is insignificant in electoral outcomes.

    ProPublica found that federal immigration officers have detained at least 500 US citizens since the start of Trump’s second term, including 36 incidents in which detained US citizens were prevented from contacting their family or lawyers for 24 hours or more. The same study also found that agents who questioned their claims of citizenship detained more than 100 US citizens, nearly all of them people of color.

    In Minnesota, Human Rights Watch found that ICE’s Operation Metro Surge abuses terrified many people, causing them to stay home and miss work, school, and even essential health care appointments. US citizens of color whom Human Rights Watch interviewed at the time said they were so fearful of racial profiling that they began carrying their passports everywhere. 

    Human Rights Watch analysis also suggests that in 2025, ICE used race and ethnicity in Los Angeles as a crude proxy for identifying a person’s nationality, and detained people on that basis. Perceptions of the risks associated with an encounter with ICE may well be higher among non-white communities.  

    The notoriety of those incidents and of ICE’s widespread abuses over the last two years risks dissuading some people from voting if they feel they are likely to encounter ICE agents near polling places, Human Rights Watch said. 

    “ICE agents should be publicly ordered to stay away from the polls and anywhere ballots are being cast,” Bolopion said.

    #7938. Guatemala: Prominent Anti-Corruption Lawyer Fatally Shot

    Click to expand Image People stand near a framed photo of Guatemalan lawyer and anti-corruption figure Juan Francisco Solorzano Foppa, who died after being shot by unknown assailants on Saturday while visiting a sports center, during his wake at a funeral home in Guatemala City, Guatemala, October 19, 2026. © 2026 Cristina Chiquin/Reuters

    (Washington, DC) – Guatemalan authorities should carry out a thorough, prompt, and credible investigation into the killing of Juan Francisco Solórzano Foppa, a prominent anti-corruption lawyer, former prosecutor, and former head of Guatemala’s tax agency, Human Rights Watch said today.

    On October 17, 2026, armed assailants shot and killed Solórzano Foppa at the Campo Marte sports complex in Guatemala City. Another man, identified by authorities as Gustavo Adolfo Aguilar Gamarro, was also killed in the attack. Guatemalan authorities arrested two men in connection with the crime, but prosecutors said there was no evidence linking them to the attack and a judge ordered their release. 

    “The killing of Juan Francisco Solórzano Foppa is a shocking attack against a lawyer who played a prominent role in some of Guatemala’s most important anti-corruption efforts,” said Juanita Goebertus, Americas director at Human Rights Watch. “Guatemalan authorities should ensure a thorough, prompt, and credible investigation capable of identifying not only the gunmen, but also anyone who planned, ordered, or facilitated this crime.”

    Armed attackers opened fire on Solórzano Foppa while he was at the sports complex coaching a football team, killing him at the scene, media reported. President Bernardo Arévalo condemned the killing and called for those responsible, including any who played a role in planning the crime, to be brought to justice.

    Solórzano Foppa, 43, was widely known for his anti-corruption work in Guatemala. He served as director of criminal analysis within the attorney general’s office and later led Guatemala’s Superintendency of Tax Administration, from 2016 to 2018. He helped advance investigations into high-level corruption and tax evasion, contributing to efforts to recover millions in unpaid taxes and supporting major corruption cases, including work related to the criminal case known as “La Línea” (“The Line”), which led to the resignation of President Otto Pérez Molina in 2015.

    After leaving public office, Solórzano Foppa continued to work as a lawyer and public commentator. He was known for denouncing corruption and links between criminal groups, politicians, and authorities. 

    In 2023, he briefly joined the legal defense team of the well-known journalist José Rubén Zamora, who has faced arbitrary criminal prosecution since 2022. During Attorney General Consuelo Porras’s tenure, a campaign of arbitrary criminal prosecutions against critics included the detention of Solórzano Foppa, who was accused of “hindering criminal investigations” in connection with his defense of Zamora. Solórzano Foppa said the prosecution was arbitrary and pleaded guilty to obtain an early release.

    “Solórzano Foppa’s killing is a chilling reminder of Guatemala’s long history of political murders,” Goebertus said. “Those responsible should be held accountable.”

    #7939. Saudi Arabia: Man Executed for Childhood Offenses

    Click to expand Image Riyadh's general court, Saudi Arabia, October 20, 2008. © 2008 Fahad Shadeed/Reuters

    (Beirut) – Saudi authorities announced on October 17, 2026, that they had executed Hassan Zaki al-Faraj, who was convicted of crimes allegedly committed when he was a 14-year-old boy, Human Rights Watch said today. 

    The Supreme Court has also apparently upheld the death sentence of another man, Yousef al-Manasif, who was convicted of crimes allegedly committed when he was 15 and 16, putting him at risk of imminent execution. At least three other men convicted of alleged crimes committed as children are on death row, despite allegations of torture, forced confessions, and prolonged solitary confinement.

    “The execution of Hassan al-Faraj is unconscionable compounded by alleged torture-tainted evidence and a well-documented record of unfair trials,” said Joey Shea, senior Saudi Arabia researcher at Human Rights Watch. “With the Supreme Court’s recent decision, Yousef al-Manasif is also at serious risk of execution.”

    The death sentence for al-Manasif was recently upheld by the Supreme Court, the European Saudi Organisation for Human Rights (ESOHR) reported. In October and November 2022, Saudi Arabia’s notorious Specialized Criminal Court handed down death sentences to al-Manasif and al-Faraj, as well as Ali al-Mabyook, Jawad Qureiris, Ali al-Subaiti, and Mahdi al-Mohsen for alleged terrorism crimes they committed as children, according to a report by the ESOHR and court documents. 

    Saudi authorities arrested al-Faraj on November 27, 2017, held him in solitary confinement for six months, and charged him with various counterterrorism offenses, including one allegedly committed when he was 14, which relates to meeting and developing friendships with other detained and wanted individuals, based on the charge sheet and court documents. The Specialized Criminal Court sentenced him to death on November 3, 2022, despite allegations that he had been tortured and forced to sign the confession on which the conviction was based, his defense said. 

    Court documents reviewed by Human Rights Watch show that authorities arrested al-Manasif on April 6, 2017, and al-Mabyook on January 29, 2018. Al-Manasif was arrested for protesting Saudi authorities’ treatment of its Shia minority and attending funeral processions in 2011 and 2012 when he was 15 and 16, according to the ESOHR and United Nations experts. Al-Mabyook was arrested for attending funeral processions before he turned 18, ESOHR and court documents said.  

    The court convicted and sentenced them to death on November 2, 2022, under broad and vague counterterrorism laws based almost exclusively on confessions that their defense alleges were obtained under torture, the documents showed. The authorities did not inform al-Manasif of his charges until months after his arrest or provide him with legal representation during his interrogation and held him in pretrial detention for over two years, including six months in solitary confinement, the documents show. 

    Both al-Manasif and al-Mabyook appealed to the Appellate Chamber of the Specialized Criminal Court on March 19, 2025. The appeals were based on them both having been under 18 when the alleged offenses were committed, the court documents said. On August 26, 2025, the appellate court rejected that defense, referring to the dates in their confessions, despite allegations that the confession was obtained under torture.

    Saudi authorities arrested Qureiris on December 31, 2020, court documents say. Saudi authorities charged him under the counterterrorism law for alleged offenses at the ages of 15 and 17. The Specialized Criminal Court convicted and sentenced him to death on November 5, 2022, despite allegations of severe beatings during interrogation to make him sign a confession, the documents say. Qureiris was also held in solitary confinement for 270 days to force the confession, according to the documents.

    The decision was submitted to the same court for review on November 12, 2024, where Qureiris stated that he did not commit any of the alleged offenses except participating in a protest in 2010 when he was 15, court documents showed. The court approved his death sentence on March 2, 2025. 

    The UN Working Group on Arbitrary Detention concluded in November 2024 that the detentions of al-Manasif, al-Faraj, and Qureiris were arbitrary and based on the men’s exercise of their right to free speech when they participated in protests against Saudi Arabia’s treatment of the country’s Shia minority. 

    On April 9, 2026, Saudi authorities executed Ali al-Subaiti for alleged crimes committed when he was 12, according to the Saudi Press Agency. On October 23, 2017, Saudi authorities arrested al-Subaiti, who was then 19, and charged him under counterterrorism provisions including joining and financing a terrorist organization and attempting to undermine the social fabric and national security by inciting protests, according to the charge sheet. The charges included participating in a demonstration when he was 12 and “covering up for terrorists” when he was 17. The court sentenced al-Subaiti to death on October 17, 2022, according to the ESOHR.

    Saudi Arabia’s Juvenile Law of 2018 and a 2020 executive order ended the use of the death penalty against individuals who were under 18 when the crime was committed, in cases that do not involve the 2017 counterterrorism law. In non-terrorism cases, the death penalty is replaced with a maximum 10-year prison sentence. Human Rights Watch has documented Saudi Arabia’s long-standing misuse of counterterrorism provisions to silence dissent.

    In 2025, Saudi Arabia executed Jalal al-Labbad and Abdullah al-Derazi, both accused of committing crimes as children, as part of the country’s unprecedented execution spree, despite calls from UN experts and rights groups to halt the executions. 

    Human Rights Watch has documented long-standing due process violations in Saudi Arabia’s criminal justice system, including restricted or denied access to legal representation and forced confessions, making it highly unlikely that any of the eight men received a fair trial. 

    The UN Convention on the Rights of the Child, to which Saudi Arabia is a party, strictly prohibits the use of the death penalty for offenses committed by individuals under 18. Article 6(2) of the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights and article 6 of the Arab Charter on Human Rights oblige countries that use capital punishment to use it only for the “most serious crimes.” Human Rights Watch opposes the death penalty in all countries and circumstances due to its inherent cruelty and irreversibility. 

    “Saudi Arabia’s execution spree, particularly for offenses by children, shows no signs of slowing down in 2026,” Shea said. “This underscores the urgent need for the international community to press the Saudi government to immediately halt executions and abolish the death penalty once and for all.”  

    #7940. Lebanon: Amend Media Law to Protect Free Expression

    Click to expand Image Lebanese lawmakers at the parliament building in downtown Beirut, January 9, 2025. © 2025 Hussein Malla/AP Photo

    (Beirut) – Lebanon’s parliament should amend the recently-adopted media law to fully remove all provisions in Lebanese law that restrict the right to free expression, Human Rights Watch said today. Parliament passed the law on August 11, 2026, but a new draft law introduced by 10 members of parliament on October 5 offers amendments to the law that would resolve its key problematic provisions.

    “Given the history of public officials and powerful individuals abusing Lebanon’s laws to clamp down on journalists, activists, and artists, parliament should act to prevent further abuse,” said Ramzi Kaiss, Lebanon researcher at Human Rights Watch. “No one should face abusive retaliation for peacefully expressing their views, and parliament has a chance to end these abuses right here and now.”

    The media law, which parliament adopted after 16 years of deliberation, includes significant improvements. Some of the key advances include ending pretrial detention for free expression-related crimes committed online or through media-related means. The law establishes individuals’ right to create online media platforms or issue publications without prior state authorization and limits the military court’s jurisdiction over expression-related crimes committed online or through media-related means. It also decriminalizes defamation and insult in certain cases, transferring them to civil court jurisdiction.

    The law also includes provisions that would promote media independence, including by prohibiting undue interference in journalists’ work, promoting media workers’ rights to unionize, protecting sources’ anonymity, and limiting the ability of security services to search and seize journalists’ electronic equipment and documents.

    The adopted media law, however, includes a provision, article 104(b), which criminalizes the “intentional fabrication of falsehoods and dissemination of false and harmful news” providing for a sentence of up to three years in prison. For decades, Lebanese journalists, artists, media organizations, and civil society groups have repeatedly faced lawsuits based on criminal defamation charges and other vague legal provisions in retaliation for their work.

    This provision, which includes vague and undefined terms such as “harmful news,” risks enabling continued threats of criminal proceedings against journalists as well as such lawsuits.

    While the adopted media law decriminalizes defamation and insult in some cases, provisions found in Lebanon’s Penal Code, which the law does not mention, annul, or make inapplicable, would continue to criminalize libel, slander, and insult directed against a foreign state or its president, representatives, army, flag, or emblems. 

    Such provisions also criminalize defamation against Lebanon’s president, state institutions, and army, among others, imposing prison sentences of up to two years. Provisions of the Lebanon Military Justice Code, which also remain in effect, include the criminalization of “publicly disparaging the flag or the army or infringing upon its dignity, reputation or morale,” imposing a prison sentence of up to three years.

    The amendments sought in the October 5 draft law would annul article 104(b) of the media law and make these articles, in both Lebanon’s Penal Code and Military Justice Code, inapplicable to cases covered by the media law, including for both media-related or online forms of expression. 

    The amendments would also limit security forces’ ability to interrogate and investigate media professionals for expression-related crimes, limiting these powers solely to the public prosecution’s jurisdiction. Human Rights Watch has repeatedly documented violations, including, physical and psychological interrogation tactics and bias by Lebanese security agencies in enforcing criminal defamation laws against journalists and activists. 

    Since Lebanon’s media law only applies to acts of media-related and online forms of expression, Lebanon’s parliament should fully repeal all provisions, including in Lebanon’s Penal Code and Military Justice Code, related to criminal defamation or insult, Human Rights Watch said. It should make any such cases, including offline and non-media related expression, subject to civil court jurisdiction, in accordance with international human rights standards.

    “For years Lebanese civil society has fought for increased protections to the right to freedom of expression and the press,” Kaiss said. “Passing the proposed amendments to the media law would be a breakthrough.” 

    #7941. EU, Belgium, Denmark: Raise Rights With Vietnam’s Leader

    Click to expand Image Vietnam’s leader To Lam meeting President of the European Commission Ursula von der Leyen during United Nations General Assembly meetings, September 24, 2024. © 2024 Pool Union Europe/Agence Hans Lucas via Reuters

    (Brussels) – Leaders of the European Union, Belgium, and Denmark should urge Vietnam to make concrete progress in improving its human rights record during Vietnamese President To Lam’s visit to Copenhagen and Brussels the week of October 19-24, 2026, Human Rights Watch said today.

    Human Rights Watch wrote to the prime ministers of Denmark and Belgium and, jointly with other groups, to the European Council and European Commission presidents, expressing concerns about Vietnam’s intensifying crackdown on human rights, which violates several obligations and commitments it made to the EU in a 2020 free trade agreement.

    “The Vietnamese government has broken its pledges of reform and continues to crack down on basic freedoms,” said Patricia Gossman, senior associate Asia director at Human Rights Watch. “European leaders should communicate clearly and publicly to President To Lam that human rights and labor rights violations will no longer be tolerated.”

    Human Rights Watch, in its letters, highlighted that the Vietnamese government has sharply intensified its repression of basic freedoms over the past 10 years. To Lam served as Vietnam’s minister of public security from 2016 to 2024—the head of its police force—and since then as general secretary of the Vietnamese Communist Party and later president.

    On To Lam’s watch, Vietnamese police initiated a far-reaching crackdown that decimated the country’s civil society, stepped up transnational repression, and expanded the scope and use of draconian laws that criminalize the exercise of freedom of expression in contravention of Vietnam’s international human rights obligations.

    Vietnam currently has at least 160 political prisoners imprisoned under draconian laws that violate their rights to free expression and peaceful activism for human rights and democracy. These include: Pham Doan Trang, a writer and human rights advocate; Can Thi Theu, a land rights activist; Pham Chi Dung and Nguyen Tuong Thuy, independent journalists; Hoang Duc Binh, a labor activist; and Le Dinh Luong, Nguyen Van Tuc, Tran Duc Thach, Tran Van Bang, and Nguyen Trung Ton, democracy campaigners.

    During the first 9 months of 2026, Vietnamese courts convicted and sentenced at least 28 activists, including the prominent bloggers Hoang Thi Hong Thai and Huynh Ngoc Tuan, to long prison terms. Police arrested at least 39 other people for allegedly criticizing the government or being affiliated with disfavored religious or political groups.

    The Vietnamese government has failed to implement legal reforms it pledged to undertake in its free trade and partnership agreements, including commitments to ratify International Labour Organization Convention No. 87 on Freedom of Association and Protection of the Right to Organise, and continues to thwart the establishment of independent trade unions.

    Despite Vietnam’s failure to meet its pledges, the EU in January upgraded bilateral relations with Vietnam to a Comprehensive Strategic Partnership. Concerns continue to be raised in yearly human rights dialogues that have largely failed to yield concrete results.

    “The EU and its member states should stop giving Vietnam a free pass on repression,” Gossman said. “To Lam needs to hear loud and clear that unless his crackdown ends and reforms are adopted, there will be consequences for political and trade relations with Europe.”

    #7942. Mali: Military Attacks on Civilians, Insurgent Abuses

    Click to expand Image Screenshot of a video posted online and analyzed by Human Rights Watch showing the destruction of a health center following an apparent Malian military drone strike in Talahandak village in the northern Kidal region, Mali, on July 29, 2026.   © Private Malian armed forces and their allies have since July 2026 responded to mounting attacks by Islamist and separatist armed groups with abusive counterinsurgency operations in central and northern Mali.The Malian military, armed groups, and insurgent forces are committing grave laws-of-war violations while international organizations and governments largely stand by.The AU Peace and Security Council should undertake sustained scrutiny of all parties to Mali’s conflict by convening a dedicated session on civilian protection and accountability and pressing them to halt abuses.

    (Nairobi) – Malian armed forces and their allies have since July 2026 responded to mounting attacks by Islamist and separatist armed groups with abusive counterinsurgency operations in central and northern Mali, Human Rights Watch said today. 

    Mali’s military and the allied Russia-controlled Africa Corps (formally the Wagner Group) have killed and wounded scores of civilians in attacks and in custody in violation of international humanitarian law. Islamist and separatist fighters have also violated the laws-of-war, including torturing and executing captured Malian soldiers. The African Union Peace and Security Council should urgently take action to prevent further atrocities in Mali. 

    “The Malian military, armed groups, and insurgent forces are committing grave laws-of-war violations while international organizations and governments largely stand by,” said Ilaria Allegrozzi, senior Sahel researcher at Human Rights Watch. “The AU Peace and Security Council should undertake sustained scrutiny of all parties to Mali’s conflict by convening a dedicated session on civilian protection and accountability and pressing them to halt abuses.”

    Between July 30 and September 20, 2026, Human Rights Watch remotely interviewed 30 people, including 16 witnesses to abuses, human rights defenders, community leaders, journalists, and a Malian soldier. Human Rights Watch verified and geolocated two videos posted online showing the aftermath of an apparent drone strike and analyzed 12 photographs of the drone’s debris. Human Rights Watch also reviewed photographs and videos of casualties from other incidents documented. Human Rights Watch sent a letter to Mali’s defense minister on August 5 with findings and questions but received no response. 

    Human Rights Watch documented two apparently indiscriminate Malian military drone attacks: a strike on July 29 in Talahandak village, in the northern Kidal region, that partially destroyed a health facility; and another one on August 19 at a market in Tombonkore village, in the central Bandiagara region, that killed six civilians and injured at least 20 others. Human Rights Watch also documented that on July 12 Malian soldiers shot and killed five girls during a counterinsurgency operation in the central Ségou region, and that on July 27 and 28, allied Dozo militia killed three civilians in the central Mopti region, all with impunity. 

    The Al-Qaeda-linked Group for the Support of Islam and Muslims (Jama’at Nusrat al-Islam wal-Muslimin, or JNIM) and allied separatist fighters of the Azawad Liberation Front (Front de libération de l’Azawad, or FLA) attacked a military convoy on July 18 in Gao region and then tortured and executed at least 19 Malian troops taken prisoner.

    These incidents occurred amid renewed fighting in Mali. On April 25, JNIM carried out coordinated attacks across the country alongside FLA fighters seeking to overthrow the military junta led by Gen. Assimi Goïta and backed by the Africa Corps. Malian armed forces and their allies responded with airstrikes that killed civilians and with reprisals against ethnic communities accused of collaborating with the insurgents.

    “I found myself on the ground, propelled by the blast, stuck between a cart and a tree more than 15 meters from where I had been,” said a 24-year-old man who survived the August 19 attack. “Everyone started running. I tried to get up and saw that my right leg was completely shredded. Blood was dripping from my pants.”

    Since their coordinated attacks in April, JNIM and the FLA have captured and detained increasing numbers of Malian security personnel. Concerns over their treatment intensified after the two armed groups claimed responsibility for a July 18 attack against a Malian military and Africa Corps convoy in Tabrichat, Gao region, in which at least 19 Malian troops who had surrendered were tortured and executed.

    Human Rights Watch reviewed videos posted online, also previously analyzed by Amnesty International, showing armed men beating and shooting surrendering soldiers. Human Rights Watch could not determine whether the armed men were JNIM or FLA fighters. However, in one of the videos, credible sources and international media identified one of the men as the son of JNIM’s leader, Iyad Ag Ghali. In at least two videos, as Amnesty International highlighted, a white flag with black writing, characteristic of JNIM, is visible on the front of the attackers’ vehicles.

    “Once [we made it to] Gao, over 50 military personnel were missing,” a soldier who survived the attack said. “At least 19 were executed – we saw them in the videos. The others? Dead? Captured? We don’t know. We can’t ask questions and we have been prohibited from watching the videos in the camp.”

    JNIM’s Sharia Committee, in a September 30 response (English, Arabic) to Human Rights Watch, wrote that “although the killing of captives in Tabrichat … did in fact take place, we did not give the order for it, not because it is not permitted in our religion, but because we considered it a matter of interest to leave this alone.” JNIM added that Islam “grants Muslim leaders four choices with respect to captives: release, ransom, enslavement, and execution … even though we did not order our troops to commit that incident and it is not our policy to do such things, what they did does not violate our Sharia such that we see it necessary to hold them accountable or punish them.”

    In a September 20 response to Human Rights Watch, Mohamed Elmaouloud Ramadane, FLA’s spokesperson, wrote that “[t]he FLA acknowledges that armed clashes took place between its forces and those of the Malian army and its Russian allies of the Africa Corps in Tabrichat on July 18, … However, the FLA categorically rejects any allegation that its forces carried out the execution” of Malian soldiers. He added that “the FLA has carried out and will continue to carry out … internal checks about any … violations of international humanitarian law by some of its members … especially those from Tabrichat.”

    All parties to Mali’s armed conflict are bound by international humanitarian law, notably Common Article 3, Protocol Additional of 1977 to the 1949 Geneva Conventions relating to the Protection of Victims of Non-International Armed Conflicts (Protocol II),  and customary laws-of-war. Deliberate or indiscriminate attacks on civilians or civilian property, including medical facilities, are prohibited. Indiscriminate attacks include those that do not target a specific military objective. Common Article 3 and Protocol II also provide that anyone deprived of their liberty must be treated humanely in all circumstances. In particular, they are protected against murder, torture, and cruel, humiliating, and degrading treatment. Individuals who commit serious violations of the laws-of-war with criminal intent or are responsible as a matter of command responsibility may be prosecuted for war crimes.

    At its April 28 session on Mali, the AU Peace and Security Council called for protecting civilians and regular briefings and requested increased AU support for Mali’s security sector in compliance with international human rights and humanitarian law. The council should follow through by adopting a communiqué condemning abuses by all parties to the conflict and requesting an urgent report on civilian protection measures and investigations, Human Rights Watch said.

    “The AU Peace and Security Council should follow up its commitments to protect civilians in Mali with concrete measures and regular public reporting,” Allegrozzi said. “It should press the Malian authorities to conduct independent investigations into abuses by the Malian military and its allies as well as opposition forces, and offer support in such investigations from AU human rights experts.” 

    Abuses by Malian Armed Forces and Allied Militias

    Armed Conflict Location & Event Data (ACLED), a nongovernmental organization that monitors, collects, and analyzes conflict-related data globally, reported that between April and August 2026, Malian forces and the Africa Corps carried out 49 airstrikes, killing 89 civilians, more than double the 22 strikes and 44 civilian deaths reported during the same period in 2025. ACLED data also show that between April and August, the Malian armed forces killed 268 civilians. JNIM forces killed 134 civilians over the same period.

    Following military coups in 2020 and 2021, Mali’s junta has increasingly used airstrikes in its operations against armed groups. Mali has acquired and used Turkish-made Bayraktar TB2 drones since 2022 and Turkish-made Akinci drones since 2024. Russia has since 2022 also provided drones and fighter jets to Africa Corps personnel in Mali. Human Rights Watch, as well as other human rights groups and several media and investigative organizations, have repeatedly documented unlawfully indiscriminate or disproportionate airstrikes that caused civilian casualties. 

    Apparent Indiscriminate Drone Strikes

    Tombonkore, Bandiagara Region, August 19

    On the morning of August 19, an apparent military drone released munition on civilians gathered at the cattle market in the village of Tombonkore, Kilimpo area, Bandiagara region, killing six men and injuring at least 20 others. The strike also killed several animals and destroyed market stalls and motorbikes. 

    Five witnesses, including three injured in the strike, said that JNIM forces controlled the Tombonkore vicinity, but that no JNIM fighters were at the market or in the village at the time of the strike. They said the drone flew over the village for an extended period before releasing its munition, suggesting that the area was being surveilled.

    “It [the drone] was gray and slowly flew above us from east to west and back for about 30 minutes before it bombed us,” said a cattle trader, 48, who was at the market.

    Human Rights Watch could not identify the specific drone or munition used. However, the Malian military and allied Africa Corps are the only known forces in Mali to possess the large drones described by witnesses.

    A 43-year-old witness said:

    When the drone dropped the bomb on the market, I was thrown to the ground, blood gushing from my body. I saw my right arm was almost severed, held together only by a tiny piece of flesh. The blood was flowing profusely. A man … dragged me toward a small house nearby. I felt excruciating pain, and lay in a pool of blood. I saw people running, I heard screams. I got up after 40 minutes like that, and … looked for help. A man I didn’t know rescued me, took my turban and tied it around my arm securely to stop the bleeding.

    The man’s arm was later amputated at the hospital in Sévaré. 

    A 24-year-old man injured in the strike was interviewed hours before doctors amputated his right leg at the Sévaré hospital. He said he saw the drone repeatedly fly over the village, first eastward and later westward at increasingly lower altitude, until it approached the village more directly and dropped its munition. He said:

    I saw people lying down, screaming and asking for help. It was at least an hour later that the villagers came to rescue us. I was rescued by a [ethnic] Dogon [man] who took me to his home next to the market, where I found my wife and other wounded people.… The motorcycle that my friend and I had come with to the market had caught fire. We had no means of transportation.… My foot was wrapped with a big cloth, and the pain was so severe. I spent the night with this Dogon family.… The next day, my foot looked like an elephant’s leg…. They took me to the hospital, and I am waiting for doctors to amputate my leg.

    A 29-year-old butcher said he was at the market when the drone dropped its munition:

    I fell and tried to get up, but I couldn’t. I saw blood running from my clothes, but I didn’t feel any pain. I then crawled to take cover under a cart to avoid a second strike. I looked at my leg; it was red with blood and twisted. From there, I saw several people on the ground, some screaming and others not moving. People were running everywhere. Motorcycles parked near my butcher shop caught fire. I was screaming for help, but people were running around, and no one was listening to me. After an hour and a half like that, calm returned, the market was empty, and villagers came to help the injured. I had lost a lot of blood. Friends and a cousin … tied my leg with a cloth before lifting me. I couldn’t breathe; I felt nauseated.

    The man said that his cousin took him to the hospital in Bandiagara and then to the Sévaré hospital, where his right leg was treated for a fracture. 

    The 48-year-old man said he helped evacuate the injured by putting them on carts and motorcycles and buried the bodies of those killed. He said five civilian men died on the spot, while a sixth died of his injuries at the Sévaré hospital:

    Around 3 p.m. we buried the dead at the village cemetery, each in his grave.... There were no JNIM fighters among the dead. The bodies of those killed … had deep wounds in the abdomen and back.… The bodies were almost all at the same place, at the market, a few meters one from the other. Fourteen were seriously injured and were taken to either Bandiagara or Sévaré hospitals. Others, with less serious wounds, were treated in the village – they did not want to go to the hospital because they feared reprisals from the FAMA [Malian armed forces]. Among the injured, I saw one who had almost lost an arm, another had both legs in tatters, and others had open wounds on their abdomen, back, and head.

    Witnesses shared with Human Rights Watch a video showing the aftermath of the strike. Although Human Rights Watch was not able to independently geolocate it, the footage includes an on-screen narration identifying the location and date of the attack consistent with witness accounts. The video shows the bodies of at least two men and blood on the ground.

    Human Rights Watch also reviewed 10 photographs from witnesses showing one of the bodies visible in the video, another dead man, dead animals, and other people that witnesses said were injured in the strike. One of the photographs shows a man whose right arm is severely damaged; a forensic expert who reviewed the image said that injuries to the arm were highly consistent with a blast injury. Three other photographs show three injured men, with wounds to their legs and arms, including one who sustained a traumatic amputation. 

    The chief of staff of the Malian armed forces issued a statement on August 20 that on the previous day Malian forces and their partners successfully struck a “terrorist hideout” in the Kilimpo area after long monitoring it. He added that about 20 “terrorists” were “neutralized,” and military equipment destroyed.

    Human Rights Watch reviewed a list provided by witnesses with the names of the six men, ages 21 to 54, killed in the strike. 

    “They [JNIM fighters] weren’t there that day, and still they are often around the village and also visit it,” said the 24-year-old man. “They preach and, above all, they make a lot of purchases at the market, buying fuel, pasta, spices, cereals, especially on Fridays. They also come during the week to make sure everyone follows the rules.”

    Talahandak, Kidal Region, July 29 

    On July 29 at about 4 a.m., an apparent one-way (“kamikaze”) attack drone hit and partially destroyed a health center in Talahandak, a village in a JNIM and FLA-controlled area near the Algerian border. 

    Two men, including a 45-year-old health worker who witnessed the explosion, said that no JNIM or FLA fighters were in or around the health facility at the time of the strike and that the health center did not store anything other than medicines and medical equipment. 

    The health worker said:

    At the health center, we took care of men, women, children, and elderly people suffering from, among other things, malaria. Under no circumstances was this facility used by armed groups.… Only civilians received care here. Today, the center is destroyed.… Women and children, already deprived of basic health services, are going to suffer from this situation, not the armed groups. There were never any munitions or armed vehicles on site, even in the days before the attack. The fighters never came here. No terrorist or armed man was ever treated here; we don’t even have the medical means to treat war injuries.

    The witnesses said they recovered the drone’s debris. They provided 12 photographs they said were taken at the site after the strike. The photographs analyzed by Human Rights Watch show components and remnants consistent with a Shahed-type delta wing drone, including control surfaces, brackets, bolt patterns, and internal construction. In a July 24 report, Bellingcat and Jeune Afrique geolocated footage showing Shahed-136 type one-way attack drones in operation in Mali. They verified two strikes, including one in Talahandak on July 17 that destroyed a truck carrying cooking oil and killed one civilian.

    A 35-year-old resident of Talahandak said:

    The health center was hit. The pharmacy and the room where the patients used to get treated were destroyed, as well as the patients’ beds, the lab equipment, and the pharmaceutical products. It’s completely in ruins and can’t be used for anything anymore. I can’t accurately describe the drone because it was dark. But I heard it and saw the strike, the explosion, the flames, and the fire coming from the health center. The drone sounded like a motorcycle, came at full speed, and crashed straight into its target. The next day, we went to see the ruins of the health center. We saw the drone’s engine as well as its propeller, which were not too damaged.

    Human Rights Watch also verified and geolocated two videos posted to X on July 29 showing the interior and exterior of the Talahandak health center. The footage shows extensive damage to several rooms identified as the men’s and women’s treatment rooms, the laboratory, the pharmacy, and a consultation and ultrasound room. Damaged medical equipment and supplies are visible throughout the facility. 

    The room marked as the men’s treatment room appears to be the most damaged, with a large hole in its exterior wall where a window had been. Debris is scattered through the room, and numerous fragmentation impacts are visible on the walls, along with signs of fire damage, and a charred stretcher. The videos also show structural damage to at least two of the building’s facades and its roof. Human Rights Watch also analyzed satellite imagery from August 6 that showed severe damage to the roof’s northwestern corner.

    Under international humanitarian law, all medical facilities are protected from attack, including facilities that treat members of belligerent armed forces or armed groups. This protection ceases only when medical facilities are used to commit hostile acts outside their humanitarian function, and then only after a warning that goes unheeded. 

    Abuses During Counterinsurgency Operations

    Djenné, Mopti Region, July 27 and 28

    On July 27 around noon, a militia member from a group of dozens of Dozo militiamen wearing traditional clothing and armed with Kalashnikov-style assault rifles shot and killed a 50-year-old ethnic Fulani man near the main mosque in Djenné. Minutes later, the militiamen fatally shot another Fulani man, 43, at the same location. 

    A 45-year-old resident said:

    During the attack, I was with my friend [the 43-year-old man]. We were together when we heard a gunshot. I immediately told him, ‘Let’s take cover, it’s right next to us.’ He hesitated while I ran without waiting. The Dozo then shot him. In fact, the very first gunshot we heard was aimed at another friend of mine [the 50-year-old man]. The Dozo killed him, and we saw him collapse. When I turned my head, I saw a large group of Dozo, armed with Kalashnikovs, heading toward us. I personally know them because we all live in Djenné. To save myself, I had to run and leave my friend behind. That’s when they shot him.

    Another witness provided a similar description of the incident.

    On July 28, four Dozo militiamen, believed to be members of the same group who entered Djenné the previous day, forced their way into a house in Sankore neighborhood. They dragged out a 42-year-old Fulani man, tied his hands behind his back, blindfolded him, and drove away with him. His relatives found his body the next day, about two kilometers away, with three gunshot wounds to the head and chest.

    Human Rights Watch spoke to two witnesses to the abduction, including the victim’s 32-year-old wife. She said that on the day the Dozo shot and killed the two men near the mosque, fear spread throughout Djenné’s Fulani community, prompting many to flee. She said she urged her husband to flee, but he refused, saying that he had spoken with the military based in town to report the incident and seek protection. 

    The wife said:

    The next day, on July 28, around 10 a.m., four Dozo armed with Kalashnikovs, dressed in their traditional outfits and horned hats, surrounded our house.... Two of them broke inside the house and forcibly took him [the husband] out. They grabbed his turban to blindfold him, tied his hands behind his back, put him on a motorcycle, and drove off with him. That’s the last time I saw him alive.

    The other witness, a 39-year-old man, was among the people who found the victim’s body, “under a tree [with] a bullet wound in the head and two in his chest.”

    Residents said that there were no JNIM fighters in Djenné on the days of the killings and the abduction. They said the victims were targeted because they were Fulani. Islamist armed groups, including JNIM, have long recruited from Fulani communities in the area. Malian authorities and allied ethnic militias have repeatedly equated Fulani civilians with Islamist fighters to justify killings and other serious abuses.

    Niono, Ségou Region, July 12

    On July 12 at about 3 p.m., Malian soldiers shot and killed five girls, ages 10 to 13, who were harvesting rice in a field about three kilometers from Niono town.

    The soldiers took the victims’ bodies to the morgue at Niono hospital and later apologized to their families, acknowledging that they had made a mistake. They said that earlier that day, around midday, they had come under attack approximately nine kilometers away. When they later saw heads emerging from the rice fields, they mistakenly believed they were the attackers and opened fire.

    The father of three of the victims, a 46-year-old Fulani man, said: 

    When I arrived, the hospital was cordoned off by soldiers. I went inside and a nurse told me, ‘The soldiers brought the children’s bodies here.’ … The nurse confirmed the children had been shot by the army […] and then he led me to the morgue. That’s where I found my three children lying, their bodies riddled with bullets. Devastated, I asked the nurse, “What happened to them? They were just kids going to work in the fields, they weren’t fighting in any war! Why are they coming back like this?”

    “An army captain came all the way to our home in person to apologize to us,” said the father of the two other victims, a 59-year-old ethnic Dogon man. “He told us that they were completely sorry and that their intention was never to kill children.” The other father said he received a similar explanation from the soldiers.

    It is evident that the soldiers did not do everything feasible to verify that the targets of the attack were combatants, resulting in the killing of civilians in violation of the laws-of-war. 

    As of mid-September, the government had not, to Human Rights Watch’s knowledge, opened an investigation into this incident, nor had the families been offered any reparations. 

    Abuses by JNIM and FLA Armed Groups

    Tabrichat, Gao Region, July 18

    On July 18, JNIM and FLA fighters attacked a Malian military and Africa Corps convoy traveling from Anefis to the city of Gao near Tabrichat village in the Gao region. Both armed groups claimed responsibility for the attack, with JNIM noting “heavy casualties and significant material damage to the enemy,” and the FLA stating there were “heavy losses on the enemy side, several dead left on the battlefield, dozens of injured as well as prisoners.” 

    International media and human rights organizations reported that the armed groups captured dozens of Malian soldiers, tortured several, and then executed many. Radio France Internationale reported that JNIM and FLA fighters “summarily executed Malian soldiers who had surrendered.” Amnesty International documented the beating and abuse of the captured soldiers by alleged JNIM and FLA fighters and the execution of at least 19.

    Human Rights Watch spoke to a Malian soldier who was in the convoy and with two victims’ relatives and reviewed videos posted on social media previously analyzed by Amnesty International. The videos show surrendering soldiers with their hands in the air or clasped behind their heads walking in the desert surrounded by armed men and their vehicles. Armed men can be seen beating and shooting at the captured soldiers. 

    On July 23, the Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights condemned the torture and killing of surrendered soldiers in Tabrichat and called for accountability.

    The Malian soldier said the convoy left Anefis at about 10 p.m. on July 17, bound for Gao. Africa Corps fighters, he said, led the convoy, followed by soldiers from the Aguelhokcamp. Soldiers from the Anefis base were at the rear. He said that right from the convoy’s departure, he was worried by “the huge lack of coordination between the Africa Corps, the Anefis units, and the few units that came from Aguelhok.” 

    The soldier said: 

    Breakdowns and flat tires were too frequent, while FLA and JNIM fighters were following us since we left the camp. We were intercepting their communications. They clearly intended to attack the convoy before the morning. The terrorists had surveillance drones and launched rockets at the convoy…. Near Tabrichat, everyone understood that the convoy would be attacked and that when the attack happened, nobody would be able to counter it because we were slowed down by the breakdowns and flat tires and lacked any real coordination. We didn’t know who was where, who was doing what, who was on defense, and who was dealing with the broken-down and stuck vehicles.

    He said that JNIM and FLA fighters attacked the convoy at about 7 a.m. in Tabrichat, in a sandy area where vehicles could easily get stuck. The insurgents began surrounding the convoy and split it into two groups. He said Africa Corps fighters in front did not turn back to help, escaping instead. Malian soldiers at the middle and rear of the convoy were overwhelmed despite returning fire. “Some managed to escape, others were captured and executed,” he said. “The number of soldiers we counted as missing after the ambush is over 50. Among these 50 soldiers, 19 were executed.”

    A 32-year-old woman said she learned that her husband, an army sergeant who had been deployed to Anefis since 2023, was killed in the ambush by watching videos that circulated online soon after the attack.

    She said:

    I began struggling to breathe. I immediately left the hairdresser—who had shown me the images—to go to the military camp [in Gao].… I asked a soldier if he knew about the ambush and he replied: ‘Yes, but the situation is under control.’ … I told him I identified my husband in the videos … but he told me not to pay attention to fake videos produced by the JNIM and FLA’s propaganda [outlets], which are ‘aimed at discouraging and demoralizing the military.’ He told me to go back home and wait for an official military communication.

    The woman said the following day she spoke to her husband’s brother who told her he had also gone to see the military in Gao but had received the same answers. They then decided to seek more information from an FLA commander. 

    She said:

    We wrote him the following message: ‘Was our military relative among the soldiers who were attacked in Tabrichat? Can you verify if he’s still alive?’ The rebel commander immediately responded that the FLA did not execute any soldiers but only took them prisoner and brought them to a safe place [but] a military drone bombed the place and all prisoners died. He added that if our relative was among those taken prisoner, we should not assume that he would come back home.… It was at this moment that I began mourning.

    In response to Human Rights Watch’s query, the FLA’s spokesperson, Mohamed Elmaouloud Ramadane, also suggested that drone strikes had killed the soldiers in the custody of the rebel groups: “[S]everal military personnel under the control of or near FLA forces were killed following drone strikes carried out by the Malian armed forces and their Russian Africa Corps allies after the clashes.”

    In a July 18 statement, the chief of staff of the Malian armed forces said that “alongside the air and ground response launched to support the convoy attacked in Tabrichat by the JNIM/FLA terrorist coalition, the Malian armed forces are continuing their operations ... across the country.”

    The media reported that the public prosecutor at the judicial unit specializing in terrorism cases and organized transnational crime announced on August 1 the opening of an investigation into the summary executions at Tabrichat.

    As of mid-September, the Malian army had not contacted the families of the soldiers killed or missing to provide information or support.

    A carpenter whose brother, a 37-year-old soldier, has been missing since the Tabrichat attack, said that he had identified him in one of the videos that circulated online:

    The day after the ambush, we watched the videos and we identified him. He and his comrades can be seen with their hands up, one after the other, surrounded by the armed groups.… Any attempt to get more information from the army was in vain. His surviving comrades refused to tell us more about what happened that day. So, we have to mourn and leave it in God’s hands.

    He added that no authorities contacted him or his family to provide information about Tabrichat:

    I learned that the government has opened an investigation, but ... no government official has come to ask us anything.... The government has never reached out to us. Neither to offer condolences nor to talk to us about the investigation.… Authorities have shut themselves off in silence about this incident, and in this specific situation where soldiers are captured, executed, and filmed, relatives deserve answers.

    Human Rights Watch reviewed a list, shared by the soldier and victims’ relatives, with the names of five soldiers believed killed in the attack, ages 37 to 52.

    #7943. Indonesian Author Faces Blasphemy Probe for Short Stories

    Indonesian police in September began investigating Feby Indirani, the author of the short story collection Memburu Muhammad (Hunting for Muhammad), for alleged blasphemy after an Islamist group allegedly intervened with the organizers of a literary festival at which Indirani was to appear.

    Click to expand Image Feby Indirani. © Private

    Bogor Literary Week organized an event featuring Indirani that was scheduled for September 20 but cancelled it days after the Front Persaudaraan Islam (Islamic Brotherhood Front, FPI) threatened to disrupt the discussion.

    In response to a Human Rights Watch query, an Islamic Brotherhood Front representative neither confirmed nor denied the organization’s role in the cancellation of the event, stating: “In principle, FPI respects every citizen's right to express opinions and carry out activities guaranteed by the constitution. However, this right must also be exercised while respecting religious values, public beliefs, and the constitutional rights of others as regulated in the applicable laws.”

    Indirani is a prominent author whose work often focuses on Islam, women’s rights issues, and marginalized groups. After the organizers cancelled her event, she faced a wave of sexualized and gendered online harassment, as did women who spoke up online to support her. Indirani told Human Rights Watch that these attacks sought “to silence women’s freedom to read, write, and express their opinions safely.”

    The Jakarta Metropolitan Police said on September 24 that they would investigate Indirani after social media users alleged Memburu Muhammad was blasphemous. The Ministry of Religious Affairs said on September 25 that the book contained “sensitive material” that could “offend the beliefs of religious people.”

    Prominent religious leaders have also called for a blasphemy investigation, including Amirsyah Tambunan, secretary general of the Indonesian Ulema Council (MUI), an umbrella body for Islamic organizations and scholars in Indonesia, Anwar Abbas, chairman of the Muslim social organization Muhammadiyah, and Saifullah Yusuf, the social affairs minister and a Nahdlatul Ulama figure.

    Under Indonesia’s new Criminal Code, the term “blasphemy” was replaced with a reformulated category of criminal offenses that outlaw acts or expressions of hostility against the religion or beliefs of other people or groups. It is punishable by up to five years in prison.

    Indonesian authorities have an obligation to protect the right to freedom of expression, including from unlawful interference by non-state actors. The police should investigate harassment of the event organizers and Indirani—including online gendered harassment—rather than probing alleged blasphemy in a work of fiction.

    #7944. Syria: Women Barred from Defense, Security Colleges

    Click to expand Image Officials from the Syrian Ministry of Defense hold a press conference announcing the launch of the National Defense Sciences University in Damascus, Syria, July 13, 2026. © 2026 Rami Alsayed/NurPhoto via AP Photo

    (Beirut, October 16, 2026) – Syrian authorities have imposed admissions rules that bar women from most programs at two new state universities focused on defense and security, with applications closing on October 19, 2026, Human Rights Watch said today. The discriminatory rules clearly violate Syria’s international legal obligations and are part of a concerning pattern of actions by the transitional authorities that restrict women’s role in state institutions and reinforce gender stereotypes. 

    The Ministry of Higher Education and Scientific Research announced on September 30 that admission to programs in law, economics and management, and social sciences at the National University of Defense Sciences is reserved for men. At the Syrian University for Security Sciences, women can only apply to cybersecurity programs. Elsewhere, programs on kindergarten teaching at public universities, some midwifery programs, and the nursing schools in Damascus and Aleppo are reserved for women. 

    “Syria’s authorities are rebuilding the army without women,” said Hiba Zayadin, senior Syria advisor at Human Rights Watch. “Women can’t train as officers, and they can’t even study to be the army’s lawyers or administrators. This is discrimination, plain and simple.” 

    A former higher education ministry official told Agence France-Presse in early October that some of the men-only rules predate the transition, and Damascus University, the country’s largest, still admits women to all programs. But the defense and security universities are admitting students through the general university admissions process for the first time this year and shutting women out of most tracks. 

    At the National University of Defense Sciences, at which a mandatory commitment to serve in the military is required, only men can enter the land, naval, and air force military colleges, which teach engineering, and the College of Humanities and Administrative Sciences. The only part of the university open to women, the Higher Institute for Applied Sciences and Technology, involves no commitment to military service. 

    At the Syrian University for Security Sciences, the College of Security Sciences, and the Technical Institute of Security Sciences admit men only. Graduates of the university must serve with the Internal Security Forces or another competent body for 20 years after completing college or 10 years after completing an institute program.  

    Women can study law, economics, and social sciences at other public and private universities and can join the police through a separate Women’s Police Institute, which graduated its first class in September 2026. But these new policies effectively deny women a path into the army. The exclusion is already playing out in northeast Syria, where the authorities refused in August to let the Women’s Protection Units, or YPJ, the all-female component of the Kurdish-led Syrian Democratic Forces, join the army alongside its male fighters.  

    Men-only rules also apply at the state oil and gas and railways technical institutes, whose graduates must work for the relevant ministries, and in the radio and television directing department of Idlib University’s media institute. 

    The restrictions contradict the transitional authorities’ own commitments. The Constitutional Declaration, which President Ahmed al-Sharaa signed into force on March 13, 2025, guarantees equality without discrimination based on gender, and women’s right to education and work, along with their social, political, and economic rights. Al-Sharaa has also publicly pledged to respect women’s rights.  

    Syria is also party to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights. CEDAW guarantees women equal access to education (article 10), the right to hold public office and perform all public functions (article 7), and equal employment opportunities (article 11). Syria has entered no reservations to articles 7, 10, or 11.  

    Reserving professions involving care for women while keeping military, security, and some industrial programs for men also entrenches and enforces the gender stereotypes that Syria is obliged under CEDAW to eliminate (articles 5 and 10(c)). 

    Shutting women out of these programs also undermines Syria’s transition. United Nations Security Council Resolution 1325 and the CEDAW Committee’s General Recommendation No. 30 call for women’s full participation in all post-conflict reconstruction, including security sector reform. 

    Since December 2024, women have been sidelined across Syria’s public institutions. In the October 2025 indirect parliamentary elections, women won 6 of the 119 elected seats in the People’s Assembly, about 5 percent. The UN deputy special envoy for Syria said that women were consistently underrepresented throughout the process. 

    Senior Syrian officials have publicly questioned or openly disparaged women’s place in public office. In December 2024, the government spokesperson suggested women may be biologically unsuited for some political roles and said their ability to serve as judges “could be an area of study and research for specialists.” 

    Women working in some public sector roles face new rules about their physical appearance. In January 2026, the governor of Latakia, a central government appointee, issued a directive banning female public employees, including school staff, from wearing “excessive” makeup at work, under threat of “legal accountability.” The directive does not explicitly define what counts as excessive makeup or outline specific punishment details.  

    On the other hand, in December 2024, the education minister, Nazir al-Qadri, said the right to education is not limited to one gender. The authorities should honor that commitment, Human Rights Watch said.  

    The Ministry of Higher Education should open the security and defense university programs to women on equal terms before applications close on October 19, 2026, and rescind all other gender-based admissions restrictions. The transitional authorities should revoke the Latakia makeup ban and adopt concrete measures to ensure full, equal, safe, and meaningful participation by diverse women in all public institutions, including the People’s Assembly.  

    Governments and donors supporting Syria should prioritize ending all forms of discrimination, including in the security sector. All Syrian authorities should uphold the Constitutional Declaration and Syria’s international commitments and remove any provision or policy that discriminates against women.  

    “Women are underrepresented in parliament, the police, and some public offices, and are now shut out of the army," Zayadin said. “The authorities have promised to respect women’s rights, but their actions directly contradict those promises. Women’s full participation, in all roles and sectors, is essential to building a brighter future for Syria.”

    #7945. F1’s Rwanda Race Could Be Bad News for Rights

    Click to expand Image Rwanda's President Paul Kagame delivers remarks during the announcement of the Formula 1 African Grand Prix at BK Arena in Kigali on October 13, 2026. © 2026 Jean Baptiste Nkurunziza/AFP via Getty Images

    Formula One’s decision this week to bring a Grand Prix to Rwanda in the early 2030s could be an important moment for Africa. The continent deserves more prominence on the global sporting calendar.

    But the 10-year agreement between Formula One and Rwanda also brings significant human rights risks.

    The Rwandan government’s repression means journalists and others who challenge the government’s relentlessly positive image of the country may be at risk prior to, during, and after race weekends. The Rwandan government has long targeted independent media and political opponents both within Rwandan and abroad, including through torture, unlawful military detention, enforced disappearances and extrajudicial killings.

    Human Rights Watch has also documented how Rwanda’s authorities routinely round up and detain homeless people and street venders before large events. People detained in the capital Kigali are held in a large warehouse—labeled a “transit center”—until foreign visitors have left. For example, some of the city’s poorest and most marginalized people were locked up prior to an international conference in 2021.

    Rwanda, like other countries, has also used past high-profile sports deals and races to distract from its abysmal human rights record, a tactic known as “sportswashing.” Rwanda is currently the power behind the abusive M23 rebel group in eastern Democratic Republic of Congo and its military forces have been implicated in unlawful displacement, large-scale forced recruitment and arbitrary detention campaigns, and deaths of civilians in violation of international humanitarian law.

    Human Rights Watch wrote to Formula One on February 24 to ask how it is addressing the risks of sportswashing and of human rights abuses connected to its events, including regarding Rwanda’s bid to host an F1 Grand Prix. We’ve yet to receive a response.

    Prior to giving the green light to racing in Rwanda, Formula One should conduct an in-depth assessment of human rights risks, including by consulting with civil society groups inside and outside the country. Formula One’s agreement with the Rwandan government should include commitments, with measurable benchmarks, to protect national and international journalists covering the sport. Formula One should also address the risk of the Rwandan government using motorsport to sportswash its record.

    Bringing Formula One back to Africa is good for the sport and for the continent. But the Rwandan government’s repression—at home and abroad—means Formula One can’t cut corners on human rights.

    #7946. Abusive Care Facility in Thailand Raises Broader Concerns

    Click to expand Image A person lies in bed at a nursing home in Ayutthaya, Thailand, April 9, 2016. © 2016 Athit Perawongmetha/Reuters

    A care facility in Kanchanaburi, Thailand made headlines in early October when photos of conditions in the facility went viral, including images allegedly depicting metal cages that confine residents to their beds. Further investigation by the country’s Department of Health Service Support uncovered unsanitary conditions and overcrowding, as well as evidence that the facility had been treating residents with psychosocial disabilities (mental health conditions) without an appropriate license.

    The whistleblower, known as “Mae Phat,” appeared on television to recount her experiences at Sothornchai Nursing Home as a former resident, describing how she was involuntarily committed, forced to take medication, and physically restrained, often for hours at a time.

    Thai officials promptly ordered the facility’s closure as well as inspections of 1,203 facilities for older people nationwide. While the authorities have taken appropriate first steps, conditions at the facility have raised broader concerns about institutionalization, shackling, and the treatment of older people in Thailand.

    The United Nations Department of Economic and Social Affairs predicts that 28 percent of Thailand's population will be 65 or older within the next decade. Though Thailand has launched a community-based long-term care program for older people, responsibility for care falls under several government ministries. Ongoing shortages of qualified care and support workers as well as budgetary constraints leave many older people reliant on family care, which not all families can adequately provide.

    The Thai government should take this opportunity to reconsider its fragmented policies and commit to ensuring older people are treated with dignity and empowered to make decisions about their care and support. The government should develop and implement a comprehensive, time-bound deinstitutionalization plan for older people and people with disabilities, ensuring full access to community-based services and individualized support that would enable them to live independently and participate fully in their communities.

    The government should also establish a task force to conduct regular, unannounced visits to public and private institutions housing older people and people with psychosocial disabilities, assessing living conditions and conducting confidential interviews with residents and staff. This task force should also establish a hotline to receive and investigate reports of abuse and ensure accountability.

    Thailand should move beyond institutional care and guarantee everyone the support required to live independently in the community.

    #7947. Cambodian Supreme Court Maximizes Sentence for Opposition Activist

    Click to expand Image Cambodia’s Supreme Court, Phnom Penh, June 25, 2026. © 2026 Sopheng Cheang/AP Photo

    Cambodia’s Supreme Court on October 14 denied opposition activist Voeung Samnang’s request to serve his two sentences concurrently, meaning he will spend 11 years in prison. Samnang had been convicted on charges of “plotting an attack on the state” and received sentences of five and six years.

    The case illustrates the Cambodian government’s increased use of vague plotting charges to prosecute opposition activists and social media users and sentence them to long prison terms.

    Samnang had been a local commune official for the Cambodia National Rescue Party (CNRP), the opposition party dissolved by the government-controlled Supreme Court in 2017. He fled to Thailand in 2020 after charges were filed against him. In 2021, Thai police arrested him and forcibly returned him to Cambodia, alongside opposition activist Veourn Veasna, despite both being recognized as refugees by the United Nations refugee agency.

    Samnang was charged with plotting, convicted and sentenced to five years in prison in 2022 as part of a mass trial of 36 former CNRP leaders and supporters. While incarcerated, he was tried and convicted again in 2023 on additional plotting charges and sentenced to six years in prison. The ostensible evidence against him in both cases was Facebook posts related to the CNRP and his criticism of the government.

    Samnang’s wife, Teang Chenda, joined the “Friday Women,” a group of Cambodian women who held regular protests in the capital, Phnom Penh, to call for the release of their imprisoned husbands and sons. The Friday Women have faced harassment from Cambodian authorities during their public protests.

    Samnang’s case highlights the government’s unrelenting persecution of the political opposition ahead of next year’s local elections. Veasna, the man arrested alongside Samnang, was charged and convicted in a separate case. He later apologized to then-Prime Minister Hun Sen, joined the ruling Cambodian People’s Party, and was released from prison – part of the government’s systematic use of coerced confessions to discredit political opposition members and activists. Veasna later fled Cambodia and disavowed his defection to the ruling party.

    #7948. Turkish Government Takes Over Independent TV News Channel

    Click to expand Image Halk TV general news coordinator Serhan Asker gives an interview to media after the government takeover of the channel, a police raid on the premises, and the announcement of an arrest warrant against its owner and 16 others. Halk TV, Istanbul, October 14, 2026. © 2026 Adem Altan/AFP via Getty Images

    Police in Türkiye carried out a dawn raid on the widely watched, independent news channel Halk TV on October 14, signaling its takeover by a government-appointed administrator who promptly ordered the station to end its live broadcast.

    Halk TV is close to the main political opposition New Party (Yeni Parti). Its journalists have reported on the imprisonment and trial of Istanbul’s mayor, Ekrem İmamoğlu, government moves to split the opposition, and persistent allegations of corruption in the ruling party. The seizure of the channel is part of a wider effort by the government of Recep Tayyip Erdoğan to restrict Turkish citizens’ access to independent news sources that has involved pulling the plug on several TV news outlets, prosecuting and jailing journalists, and greatly escalating internet censorship.

    The Istanbul prosecutor’s office ordered the arrest of Halk TV’s owner on accusations of bribery, money laundering, and rigging tenders, along with 16 other people. As with other takeovers of media outlets, the financial crimes investigation provides a pretext for censorship. Halk TV will now be managed by the Savings Deposit Insurance Fund, a state body attached to the presidency, which has been used over the past 10 years to manage companies under governmental administration that are subject to criminal investigations.

    Halk TV continued to report on matters of pressing public interest until the last moment. An anchor was conducting an interview about an investment fund scandal implicating prominent figures in Erdoğan’s party moments before the administrators cut the live broadcast, leaving viewers instead with a scene from an old Turkish film.

    The takeover of Halk TV came on the very day that İmamoğlu took to the stand to begin his defense in the main politically motivated trial against him.

    The Turkish government’s steps to suppress and silence a TV station that broadcasts critical content violates not only the rights of the workers and journalists targeted, but the public’s right of access to information and to freedom of expression.

    Most people in Türkiye rely on television over other media for news coverage. Halk TV has been a critical voice in a country where broadcast news is increasingly monotone and government-aligned. The Turkish authorities should take immediate steps to reverse the takeover of the channel. A free, pluralistic media offering varied views and opinions is a cornerstone of a democratic society.

    #7949. Kazakhstan Women Need Action on Domestic Violence

    Click to expand Image Participants at the international Women's Day rally in Almaty, Kazakhstan, March 8, 2022. © 2022 Stanislav Beketov

    During its October 6 review by the United Nations Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), Kazakhstan pledged new legislation to make domestic violence a standalone crime – a timely commitment. Now the government should turn words into action.

    On September 23, Zhazira Adauova, a mother of two, died after her ex-husband stabbed her 16 times in Astana, Kazakhstan’s capital. Zhazira’s family told the media she had repeatedly reported threats and violence to police. Four former police officers are under investigation for alleged negligence in handling her case. 

    Elvira Erkebayeva, from western Kazakhstan, endured two decades of domestic violence, including having boiling water thrown in her face. Instead of protecting her and holding her former husband accountable, authorities prosecuted Elvira in 2025 for allegedly conspiring to murder him because she wanted to “find a firearm.”
