The British Government has been acting as its own worst enemy.

    But I do not believe the Trump presidency will ultimately shift the U.S. position on the Falklands, despite all the stupid actions of the Labour Government. Here’s why.

    The Anglo-American special relationship still matters inside the Washington Beltway, in both the executive branch and on Capitol Hill. The King’s visit to the U.S. in April was a stunning success, with even “No Kings” Democrat Members of Congress giving King Charles a series of standing ovations when he spoke to a joint session of Congress.

    President Trump’s extremely warm reception for the King at the White House and his speech welcoming the King at the arrival ceremony, which I attended, were a glowing tribute to the enduring strength of our two great nations. The bottom line is that President Trump loves Britain, the British people and his British heritage through his Scottish mother. And like Trump, nearly 60 million Americans have British ancestry according to the 2020 census, including much of America’s political and economic leadership.

    >>> How the U.S.-Europe Rift Got Balkanized

    Throw into the mix the massive levels of trade and investment between the two countries, the vital strategic importance of the U.S./UK base at Diego Garcia, the key role played by RAF Fairford in Gloucestershire (despite the decision to temporarily remove U.S. stealth strategic bombers in the wake of an Iranian terror plot), and the extremely close intelligence ties between the CIA and MI6, and the special relationship remains very real.

    Not a single American official I have spoken with in the last few months has told me they support a change in U.S. policy over the Falklands. They understand the huge importance this issue has to the British people, and all of them speak of their admiration for Margaret Thatcher who ordered the liberation of the islands from Argentina following the brutal invasion by its military junta in 1982.

    U.S. officials also acknowledge that the Argentine position over the Falklands was viewed sympathetically by the Obama administration when Hillary Clinton was Secretary of State and far-Left Cristina Kirchner was Argentina’s president. Republicans are reluctant to take a stance that Democrats have traditionally favored in Washington, and which would be championed by the American Left.

    There remains immense goodwill in the United States towards Great Britain and the British people, and very little desire to throw America’s closest ally under the bus over the Falklands. It is firmly in the U.S. national interest to defend the vital principles of sovereignty and self-determination the American people cherish.

    #8036. Corporate Alignment with the Human Rights Campaign: Legal, Financial, and Reputational Risks for Shareholders

    In 2024, Bowyer Research conducted two coordinated shareholder engagement campaigns targeting more than 400 publicly traded U.S. companies, focusing on potential risks related to corporate alignment with the Human Rights Campaign (HRC)—particularly through participation in the HRC’s Corporate Equality Index (CEI)—and from extensive Pride Month messaging. This Backgrounder summarizes the substance and scale of those engagements and documents the legal, financial, reputational, and moral concerns raised by shareholders, with particular attention to policies and communications involving children.

    The Corporate Equality Index has served as the primary mechanism through which the HRC exerts its influence on corporate governance, employment policy, and public messaging. Participation is nominally voluntary, but companies that achieve a perfect score of 100 are required to satisfy a set of criteria that, because it is ever-evolving and regularly expanded, creates more and more compliance obligations with no defined endpoint. These criteria now include coverage of sex-rejecting procedures for minors as employee health benefits, mandatory identity-based training programs, and public corporate endorsement of contested social positions.[REF]

    Shareholders represented by Bowyer Research, most notably The Heritage Foundation, engaged these companies to identify legal vulnerabilities, financial risks, and the structural incompatibility of CEI participation with sound, long-term fiduciary governance.

    The Corporate Equality Index: Structure and Escalating Demands

    The Human Rights Campaign’s Corporate Equality Index is a benchmarking tool that scores corporations on their compliance with a defined set of employment, benefits, and public-messaging criteria. First introduced in 2002, the CEI has expanded steadily in scope, and its criteria are revised fairly frequently.

    Bowyer Research’s engagement database, compiled in collaboration with the 1792 Exchange, focused on 160 companies that publicly promote perfect CEI scores of 100. These companies span a variety of sectors, including technology, finance, health care, retail, energy, and consumer goods, and reflect the broad reach of the HRC’s influence across America’s capital markets.[REF]

    Companies frequently present perfect CEI scores as evidence of commendable corporate citizenship, but shareholders represented by Bowyer Research take a contrary view: A score of 100 is evidence not of sound governance, but rather of deep structural entanglement with a controversial external activist organization’s ideological compliance demands. Because the HRC revises its criteria frequently, companies committed to maintaining a perfect score face escalating requirements with no defined endpoint. This process is incompatible with sensible risk management and long-term shareholder value creation.

    More to the point, current CEI requirements extend well beyond foundational anti-discrimination policies. They include or have included, among other criteria, employee health benefit coverage for sex-rejecting transition procedures (including for minor dependents); mandatory participation in identity-based employee training programs (prior to this year); and a demonstrated pattern of “outreach” to the LGBTQ community, often involving support for contested social and political positions. Each of these creates distinct legal, financial, and reputational risks.

    Legal and Regulatory Exposure

    CEI participation creates legal vulnerability across several distinct areas of law. The 1792 Exchange has documented these risks in its Corporate Bias Ratings analysis, noting that the CEI “promotes policies that may violate existing civil rights laws” and that participation exposes companies to “unnecessary legal risks.”[REF]

    • Employment Discrimination. Policies that prioritize gender identity over biological sex in employment have generated litigation under the Civil Rights Act of 1964 and similar state statutes. The case of Activision illustrates how these policies can generate claims from multiple directions simultaneously—from allegations of discriminatory treatment on the basis of gender identity to allegations of discrimination arising from the company’s CEI policies that infringe on the well-grounded rights of others.[REF]
    • Religious Discrimination. Mandatory participation in gender-affirming or identity-based training programs has produced religious discrimination claims in federal courts. A lawsuit involving Alaska Airlines, in which employees alleged that mandatory diversity training conflicted with sincerely held religious beliefs, illustrates the risk companies face when compliance with external advocacy frameworks meets statutory protections for religious belief.[REF]
    • State-Level Legislative Risk. Benefits policies that include coverage for puberty blockers, cross-sex hormones, and irreversible sex-rejecting surgical procedures for minor dependents are increasingly subject to state restrictions. As more states limit or prohibit procedures for minors—including treating such procedures as child abuse[REF]—companies that include them as standard employee health benefits may find themselves in conflict with state law, creating complex compliance obligations and potential liability exposure.[REF]

    Financial and Brand Risk: Lessons from High-Profile Cases

    The financial consequences of activist-aligned corporate messaging are notably well documented. Several high-profile cases provide clear evidence of material harm to shareholders.

    • Bud Light. Anheuser-Busch InBev's 2023 promotional partnership with transgender social media influencer Dylan Mulvaney resulted in a consumer boycott that produced an estimated $1 billion or more in lost sales and a sustained, multi-quarter decline in Bud Light’s market share, dropping from the number-one brand overall to number three.[REF]
    • Target. Target Corporation’s introduction of a controversial Pride merchandise collection that included “tuck-friendly” swimsuits in 2023 generated significant consumer backlash that had a measurable adverse impact on the company’s revenues and share price. Target scaled back its 2024 Pride Month campaign significantly—a widely noted signal that even companies with historically strong activist-alignment track records need to be open to reassessing risks.[REF]
    • Disney. Disney’s public positioning against Florida’s Parental Rights in Education legislation resulted in the loss of the company’s Reedy Creek special tax district, significant negative media coverage, and a period of brand and financial turbulence.[REF] The episode demonstrated that corporate activism on politically contested issues can result in unwanted regulatory and legislative attention.

    These cases illustrate a consistent pattern: Activist-aligned corporate messaging that extends beyond non-discrimination into overt participation in polarized cultural debates creates consumer division, brand damage, and measurable financial losses. The 1792 Exchange’s analysis concludes that such alignment “divides employees, alienates customers, and distracts businesses from making good products and services.”[REF]

    Child-Related Concerns: Medical, Moral, and Reputational Dimensions

    A central and especially sensitive element of both engagement campaigns involved corporate policies and messaging that affect or appear to target children. Shareholders characterized this domain as a moral and reputational red line—one with consequences that extend well beyond standard brand-risk frameworks.

    • Health Benefits for Minors. The CEI’s rating criteria encourage or require companies to include coverage for puberty blockers, sterilizing cross-sex hormones, and sex-rejecting surgeries for minor dependents as part of standard employee benefits packages.[REF] State-level restrictions create growing compliance complexity. Additionally, studies on the long-term medical effects of puberty suppression and cross-sex hormone administration on minors have shown that the risks and harms substantially outweigh any known benefits. In fact, several American and European health authorities have recently restricted such interventions pending further evidence.[REF] Offering benefits that facilitate irreversible medical procedures for minors creates significant exposure with a substantial segment of the consumer population.
    • Marketing and Messaging Involving Children. Pride Month engagement placed particular emphasis on the intersection of sexualized content and children in corporate marketing, in-store displays, and digital communications. Consumer research consistently demonstrates that parents are highly protective of their children, as a result of which they desire to prevent their exposure to sexualized content, and that brands perceived as targeting children with such messaging suffer long-term reputational harm.

    Coerced Speech, Workplace Risk, and Employee Relations

    Beyond benefits and public-facing messaging, the Bowyer/Heritage engagement campaigns addressed the internal workplace implications of HRC-driven compliance frameworks. Three categories of concern were identified.

    • Compelled Expression of Ideological Positions. Requiring employees to display ideological symbols such as pride flags or related insignia on uniforms or company-affiliated digital profiles constitutes compelled speech. This practice raises both moral and potential legal concerns and risks alienating employees who have sincere scientific, ethical, and religious beliefs that differ from the positions they are required to endorse.
    • Mandatory Identity-Based Training (prior to 2026). Unconscious bias and privilege-acknowledgment training programs that require employees to segregate or make affirmations based on race, sex, or gender identity create both discrimination exposure and workforce morale risks. As documented above, mandatory participation in such programs has already produced federal litigation.
    • Selective Inclusion Policies. Genuine inclusion requires protection for all employees, including those whose views differ from those of activist organizations. Companies that apply inclusion principles selectively risk discrimination claims and undermine the internal cohesion that drives long-term organizational success. The CEI’s “Responsible Citizenship” rating criterion penalizes companies up to 25 points for irresponsible citizenship, including donating to “anti-LGBT” non-religious groups.[REF]

    The Scale and Scope of Shareholder Concern

    The scope of the Bowyer Research/Heritage Foundation 2024 engagement efforts reflects the breadth of shareholder concern about these issues.[REF]

    The CEI-focused engagement letter was distributed to 160 corporate contacts spanning technology, finance, health care, retail, energy, and consumer goods.[REF] Recipients included Amazon, Disney, Coca-Cola, PepsiCo, Johnson & Johnson, McDonald’s, Bank of America, BlackRock, Visa, Mastercard, Microsoft, and many others, demonstrating that shareholder concern about HRC influence is far-reaching and distributed broadly across the U.S. economy.

    Pride Month messaging engagement was distributed to nearly 400 companies.[REF] In both engagement campaigns, shareholders communicated that they hold sufficient share positions to qualify to submit a proxy resolution at the next annual meeting but also expressed a preference for constructive direct dialogue before resorting to formal shareholder mechanisms.

    A Gallup survey cited in the engagement materials found that a majority of U.S. adults do not want companies to speak publicly on political and social issues—confirming that the concerns raised by these shareholders reflect a significant segment of the consumer and investor base.[REF]

    Recommendations for Action

    Based on the evidence developed in the Bowyer Research/Heritage Foundation engagement campaigns as outlined in this analysis, it is recommended that corporate boards, investors, and policymakers seriously consider the following specific actions.

    • End or limit CEI participation. Participation in the CEI by Fortune 500 companies has declined by 65 percent in 2026. This serves as evidence that efforts to eliminate overt politicization of corporations can have a significant impact. The boards of corporations that maintain their participation in the HRC’s Corporate Equality Index should evaluate whether ongoing participation serves the long-term interests of shareholders, employees, and customers. Given the continually escalating nature of CEI requirements and their attendant, documented legal and reputational risks, companies should conduct a structured risk review before seeking renewal of participation or actions that would result in a “perfect score.”
    • Review benefits policies affecting minors. Companies should conduct a thorough legal and risk review of health benefit policies that include coverage for sex-rejecting procedures or treatments for minor dependents, accounting for evolving state law, current medical evidence, and potential litigation exposure. It should be noted in this connection that on October 22, 2025, the United States District Court for the Southern District of Mississippi issued an order vacating provisions of the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services’ “Nondiscrimination in Health Programs and Activities” final rule relating to gender-identity discrimination. Consequently, the provision of health coverage for sex-rejecting procedures is no longer required under Section 1557 of the Affordable Care Act.[REF]
    • End sponsorship of the Human Rights Campaign and the HRC Foundation.[REF] Companies should limit sponsorships and partnerships to organizations that advance the interests of shareholders. Sponsorships and partnerships that are not business-related draw unnecessary scrutiny to the company and waste shareholder resources.
    • Limit public communications to core business. Companies should resist external pressure to engage in overt cultural advocacy and focus public communications instead on products, services, and business performance. In other words, businesses should focus on business.
    • Adopt viewpoint-neutral inclusion frameworks. Rather than pursuing scores on advocacy-affiliated benchmarks, companies should adopt more viewpoint-neutral inclusion frameworks—such as the Alliance Defending Freedom’s Viewpoint Diversity Score—that protect all employees regardless of their political, cultural, or religious perspectives.[REF]
    • Establish explicit protections for content that involves children. Companies should adopt clear policies prohibiting sexualized imagery or messaging in connection with children in marketing, in-store displays, or digital communications.
    • Ensure voluntary participation in training programs. Companies should review mandatory training programs for potential legal exposure under civil rights and religious freedom statutes and ensure that no employee is segregated or required to make affirmations that conflict with his or her sincerely held beliefs.

    Conclusion

    Corporate alignment with the Human Rights Campaign—particularly through participation in the Corporate Equality Index—creates a set of escalating legal, financial, reputational, and moral risks that fiduciary-minded companies can no longer treat as cost-free. The evidence from Bud Light, Target, and Disney demonstrates that activist-aligned messaging often leads to real financial harm. The structure of the CEI ensures that today’s compliance threshold becomes tomorrow’s minimum with no natural endpoint.

    The concerns raised in the Bowyer Research/Heritage Foundation 2024 engagement campaigns—spanning more than 400 publicly traded companies—are backed by logic and evidence. They reflect the views of a broad and growing segment of the investor community and are supported by legal precedent, consumer research, and cautionary lessons from companies that have already paid a significant price for over-alignment with external activist agendas.

    Corporate boards have both the authority and the fiduciary obligation to evaluate these risks clearly. Genuine inclusion—of all employees, customers, and shareholders—requires resistance to selective ideological capture, not capitulation to it. Companies that refocus on core business performance, protect employees of all viewpoints, and resist activist pressure will be better positioned to deliver long-term value to the shareholders they serve.

    Stephen R. Soukup is a Visiting Fellow in the Free Enterprise Initiative at The Heritage Foundation. Jerry Bowyer is CEO of Bowyer Research, a proxy voting and corporate engagement consulting firm specializing in shareholder advocacy on issues of corporate governance, risk management, and fiduciary responsibility.

    Appendix A: CEI Shareholder Engagement Letter

    The following is the verbatim text of the engagement letter distributed to 160 companies regarding their participation in the Human Rights Campaign’s Corporate Equality Index.

    Bowyer Research is a proxy voting and corporate engagement consulting firm. Our clients, who are your shareholders, are extremely concerned about the company embroiling itself in non-core business advocacy of some of the most divisive issues in American life. A prime example would be the Human Rights Campaign’s Corporate Equality Index. Our data indicates that you participate in this survey and have a score of 100. It is important that you understand that for many shareholders, a 100 is not an A plus, it is an F minus, especially when some of the specific demands of the HRC are factored in, such as paying for puberty blockers, cross-sex hormones and (irreversible) surgeries for minors as a health benefit.

    We represent X investors who hold Y shares in COMPANY.

    It is always our preference to begin with a request for dialogue before moving ahead with shareholder proposals.

    We share the concerns of the 1792 Exchange, whose analysis of the CEI and its risks is as follows:

    Participation in the Human Rights Campaign’s (HRC) Corporate Equality Index (CEI) exposes corporations to unnecessary legal risks by promoting policies that may violate existing civil rights laws. For example, the CEI encourages companies to adopt controversial stances on gender identity, which can lead to litigation under the Civil Rights Act of 1964 and similar state laws. For instance, prioritizing gender identity over biological sex in employment practices has led to discrimination lawsuits, as seen with Activision. Additionally, mandating employees to attend gender-affirming training could result in religious discrimination claims, as evidenced by the lawsuit involving Alaska Airlines. Furthermore, companies open themselves up to financial risks and brand harm by adopting the policies of the CEI. High-profile cases, such as Bud Light’s collaboration with a transgender activist and Target’s controversial ‘Pride’ collection, resulted in consumer boycotts that led to financial losses of billions of dollars. Aligning with the CEI divides employees, alienates customers, and distracts businesses from making good products and services. Prioritizing shareholder interests and consumer satisfaction over the demands of radical activist groups is a better path to long-term success.

    You can view the 1792 Exchange’s evaluation of your company in their Corporate Bias Ratings database (https://1792exchange.com/spotlight-reports/corporate-bias-ratings). We note that under the influence of journalist Robby Starbuck, many companies have been, wisely, rethinking their relationship with the Human Rights Campaign. If a company participates in HRC’s survey but not in surveys such as the Alliance Defending Freedom’s viewpoint-neutral Viewpoint Diversity Survey, we are well justified in thinking of that company as taking sides in a culture war with shareholders’ dollars.

    Appendix B: Pride Month Shareholder Engagement Letter

    The following is the verbatim text of the engagement letter distributed to approximately 400 companies regarding Pride Month messaging practices.

    We know that you are under constant pressure from outside activist groups and well-organized subsectors of your employees to go full-in this June on Pride Month. We urge you to remember that they do not represent the voices of everyone. Many of your shareholders, including the ones we represent, are concerned that June messaging goes beyond the commonsense acknowledgement that all customers and employees should be treated equally and with dignity and tips into taking sides in a deeply polarizing culture war. We think you want to avoid this, and we want you to know that we will back you when you do.

    We see Disney, Target and Bud Light as cautionary tales of ways in which companies have been cajoled into brand-destructive messaging. We note that even Target is pulling back this year. That should speak volumes to those who have ears to hear.

    We especially want the company to be aware of the extreme sensitivity that the majority of your customers have regarding the mixing of highly sexualized content and minors. Parents are naturally highly protective of their children and do not want them targeted with sexual messaging. This is a red line, morally and reputationally. Don’t cross it. Customers have long memories where kids are concerned.

    We also urge you not to force sexual identity flair and paraphernalia onto the uniforms of service employees, nor onto their business-related websites. Coerced speech is morally unjustifiable and legally actionable. If an employee doesn’t want to be forced to fly the rainbow flag, they should not be forced to.

    The same is true for ‘unconscious bias training’ sessions in which employees are forced to confess to various privileges inherent in having a sexual identity that is not among the current ideologically protected classes. You should let every employee bring their whole self to work, and employees who are not ‘allies’ should have their viewpoints protected against discrimination.

    Of course, employees should be evaluated based solely on merit. LGBTQ employees should not be discriminated against, but neither should employees who dissent from the political or cultural views of that interest group. Inclusion is for everybody, otherwise it isn’t inclusion at all—it’s just taking sides. And it is not in your or your shareholders’ interests for the company to be taking sides.

    A slim majority of Americans do not want companies speaking publicly on political and social issues (Gallup, 2024). The shareholders we represent possess sufficient shares to easily qualify to propose resolutions for the proxy statement of the next annual meeting, but we believe the proposal system is a fallback for companies that are not responsive to shareholder concerns, not a first resort. We hope this is something you are willing to genuinely discuss.

    #8037. The Greenland Deal Is a Huge Success

    Ignore the naysayers: President Trump’s successful conclusion of the Greenland deal with the governments of Denmark and Greenland is a huge win for the United States, and also one that will improve Danish, Greenlandic and, indeed, trans-Atlantic security.

    This is a major diplomatic achievement. It expands U.S. defense areas in Greenland, prohibits a non-NATO military presence, and protects critical parts of the Greenlandic economy that adversarial governments could otherwise use for dual-use purposes.

    The agreement contains language clarifying the core U.S. interests at stake and the rationale behind increasing activities in the Arctic, recognizing “the security challenges in the Arctic and the High North posed by adversaries’ increased military activity and growing strategic interest in the region” and that “the security situation in the Arctic is changing and that more efforts are needed to ensure security there in the future.”

    Critics have said the deal is the same as the one we had before. Not true. The 2026 agreement augments, updates and improves the 1951 treaty, expanding U.S. military infrastructure on the island with two new bases at Narsarsuaq and Mestersvig. It also prohibits Russian or Chinese military bases in Greenland, and creates a mechanism for the governments in Copenhagen, Nuuk and Washington to veto investment in critical parts of the Greenlandic economy. Importantly, the new agreement and its provisions will continue to apply even if Greenland becomes independent.

    The strategic rationale for the United States to maintain a military presence in Greenland and secure it from adversarial influence should be obvious. Greenland commands the approaches to the North Atlantic and North America, forming the northwestern end of the Greenland-Iceland-U.K. (GIUK) Gap through which Russian ships and submarines entering the North Atlantic pass. At more than 836,000 square miles, Greenland is a major geographic shaper of the Arctic region, and U.S. radars based in Greenland play an essential role in detecting and intercepting any hostile movements toward the U.S. mainland.

    >>> Why America Should Take the Lead in Greenland—Before Our Adversaries Do

    Indeed, the strategic rationale for American military presence in Greenland is strongest when considered in the context of great power competition. The Arctic is the shortest and most likely path for strategic bombers or intercontinental ballistic missiles heading toward the United States, and Greenland in particular lies in the shortest and most likely path for these systems when fired from Russia. Pituffik Space Base, an existing U.S. military site in Greenland, supports American missile warning, missile defense and space surveillance.

    Greenland is an autonomous territory of the Kingdom of Denmark, with significant self-rule but responsibility for defense resting with the Danish government in Copenhagen. Russia and China have consistently demonstrated interest in the island and surrounding region, with Chinese investments in particular targeting Greenland’s key economic sectors and infrastructure.

    To combat this, the deal also creates a mechanism for the three parties to deny Chinese or Russian investment in key Greenlandic sectors such as ports, airports and mines. Greenland is estimated to possess 1.5 million tons of critical mineral reserves. Denmark had already played a positive role in preventing malign investment in critical sectors of the Greenlandic economy, but the new deal formalizes the need and mechanism for doing this and gives the United States a say.

    >>> How Norway Can Do More To Forestall the Russian Threat

    Denmark has long been a very strong and reliable ally of the United States. Danish troops served in combat roles alongside Americans in Afghanistan after the 9/11 attacks and suffered one of the highest casualty rates in the war among our NATO allies. Denmark is one of the highest spenders on defense within the NATO alliance, had already reached 3.2 percent of gross domestic product as of 2025, and is projected to reach the mandated 3.5 percent spending level in the near future — among the first NATO allies to do so.

    Much of Denmark’s new spending is going into capabilities relevant to defending its Arctic territory. The successful conclusion of this agreement, of course, will create new avenues for Arctic security cooperation between the American and Danish militaries.

    The American, Danish and Greenlandic peoples have a shared interest in a secure Arctic where Russia is unable to threaten North America or Europe and where China (a non-Arctic state) is unable to meddle where it doesn’t belong.

    This deal secures the sovereign security and economic interests of Washington, Copenhagen, and Nuuk and helps cement trans-Atlantic security cooperation in the Arctic for decades to come.

    #8038. The Debanking Scandal Started in Washington, Not Wall Street

    The Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) has confirmed what conservatives have said for years. The OCC's 47-page report, released Wednesday after reviewing 100,000 complaints and 50,000 internal bank records, found that between 2020 and 2025 the nation’s nine largest national banks denied service to Americans because of their political, social, or religious beliefs.

    The details are ugly. JPMorgan’s WePay payment unit closed the account of a merchant selling “MAGA” themed merchandise, and its screening system flagged phrases like “America First.” It shut down a religious non-profit because the Southern Poverty Law Center, since indicted on federal fraud charges, had branded it a hate group.

    PNC refused a line of credit to a media company because one owner’s foundation had funded Christian conservative groups. Banks also choked off financing to entire lawful industries like firearms, coal, Arctic oil and gas, private prisons, tobacco, etc. While the instinct is to blame the banks, a different culprit emerges in the report’s details.

    Banks feared negative press, protests, and organized activist criticism. But the greatest fear was reprisals from regulators and politicians, because those can actually shut down a financial institution.

    >>> Regions Financial’s Reasonable Retreat From Ideology

    A Wells Fargo review of a prospective firearms client was blunt: under the Biden administration, banks could face “greater supervisory pressure to move away from lending to the firearms industry.” Another bank dropped private-prison clients citing “changing policymaker attitudes,” then tried to win one back just months into the Trump administration. 

    Those aren’t the actions of institutions pursuing an ideological crusade but of businesses reading the weather in Washington. That’s the real scandal in the OCC report.

    Banks don’t operate in a free market so much as under the permanent supervision of federal examiners who can downgrade their ratings, initiate expensive investigations, and impose costly enforcement actions.

    For years, those examiners graded banks on “reputational risk,” a category with no financial definition or objective yardstick. While a loan defaulting is a pass-or-fail course, reputational risk is whatever a regulator wants it to be, with no financial impact required.

    A deposit account for a pro-life ministry poses no inherent danger to a bank’s balance sheet. Neither does a loan to a firearms manufacturer or payment services for a conservative think tank. But when the regulators who effectively control your fate hate those groups, a threatening phone call from the government can result in debanking.

    That’s how faceless bureaucrats circumnavigate the Constitution. Officials who could never lawfully seize a disfavored group’s money can simply make it costly for banks to hold it. The Supreme Court unanimously rejected that tactic in NRA v. Vullo in 2024, holding that regulators may not coerce the firms they oversee into punishing protected speech. Yet the practice persisted, because it rarely leaves fingerprints.

    For those who believe in free markets and individual liberty, debanking was doubly offensive because it wasn’t a market failure but a regulatory distortion. Left to sound financial judgment, banks have every reason to serve profitable, lawful customers. It took government pressure to make turning away business look like the safe choice.

    Fortunately, the Trump administration has acted. Its August 2025 executive order directed regulators to end politicized debanking, and the OCC has stopped using reputational risk in examinations. But executive orders and agency guidance can be reversed on the first day of the next administration. Another far-left White House could revive reputational risk, restore the quiet phone calls, and start the cycle over.

    >>> Elizabeth Warren’s Big Government Power Grab Will Make Depositors Pay

    Congress ought to step up and make that impossible by enshrining into law protections for bank customers and the banks themselves so that the latter cannot be unconstitutionally coerced by regulators. Federal law should prohibit non-financial and nonsensical metrics like reputational risk from supervision and forbid officials from pressuring institutions to drop lawful customers without a documented, quantifiable financial reason.

    Rules like that don’t expand government but restrain it, keeping regulators focused on safety and soundness, not their private political preferences.

    Furthermore, whatever standards must apply evenly. Fintechs, payment processors, and other non-bank firms are increasingly providing the deposit and payment services Americans use every day. It’s no accident that WePay, a payment platform, produced some of the report’s worst examples.

    If those firms are exempt, politicized gatekeeping just migrates to the less-regulated, and fast-growing, corner of the market. Firms offering the same services must play by the same rules.

    Americans should never have to wonder whether their bank account depends on their politics. The debanking pressure came from Washington, and the fix has to come from Washington too, written into law where the next administration can’t quietly undo it.

    #8039. Failure Is No Option: The U.S. Space Force Has 10,600 Personnel and Manages GPS for 6 Billion Users

    The newest American military service can fit entirely in a minor league ballpark but is already playing in the big leagues. The Albuquerque Isotopes (the Colorado Rockies’ AAA affiliate) host 13,500 nightly. The United States Space Force (USSF) is six years old and numbers 10,600 personnel. Those Guardians fight daily “above their weight class” as they manage several no “no-fail” missions of using and protecting America’s space assets.  

    Consider the work of the 2nd Navigation Warfare Squadron (2 NWS), which manages the Global Positioning System (GPS) satellite constellation. Thirty-one GPS satellites, managed by a handful of brilliant 19– to 26-year-olds, are used by three-quarters of the world’s population for everything from ordering a pizza or finding the nearest emergency room to military applications that use precise targeting to minimize collateral damage. Every day, six billion users benefit from GPS worldwide, generating more than $1.4 TRILLION in economic impact.   

    Or take the 2nd Space Warning Squadron (2 SWS). Their patch is an “All-Seeing Eye,” which is fitting, as they detect and track every ballistic missile launch worldwide. From illegal ballistic missile tests in North Korea to the thousands of missiles launched near-simultaneously by Iran earlier this year, 2 SWS tracks every single one of them and accurately predicts where they are going to impact.  

    That predicted impact service is valuable for the obvious early warning of “get out now!” But it also preserves American and our allies’ missile interceptors because defensive munitions aren’t expended on missiles that land in the ocean or uninhabited areas. As an aside, 2 SWS can also detect any nuclear detonation anywhere in the world.  

    Then there’s the 4th Space Operations Squadron (4 SOPS). It has the motto “Linking the Forces” because it protects all U.S. military satellite communications. By “all,” I mean all wideband, polar, ultra-high, and low-frequency communications worldwide for all U.S. military operations, simultaneously; from an embarked Marine near the Straits of Hormuz ordering a part for an Osprey, to a soldier using a map during a training exercise in the Mojave Desert, to the proverbial “nuclear football” that a military aide keeps near the president at all times.  

    >>> U.S. Space Force

    Or take 1st Space Operations Squadron (1 SOPS), which protects all of America’s space-based assets. It operates maneuverable vehicles for “close-proximity engagement” and conducts orbital warfare.

    It also breaks an adversary’s intelligence collection and surveillance capabilities in times of war.  

    I sleep better at night knowing these Space Guardians are working every night. But then I lose sleep when I think about the challenges they face. Each of these critical capabilities operates at the intersection of national security and global commerce with a razor-thin margin of success.  

    1 SOPS is bound by physics and orbital mechanics and must continually make “maneuvering without regret” decisions to manage orbital fuel consumption. 4 SOPS operates legacy hardware that is 15 years past its planned design lifespan. 2 SWS faces an unprecedented surge in threat activity; 10 years ago, operators might see eight to 20 missile launches in a three-year assignment—today they see that consistently in an eight-hour shift.  

    4 SOS keeps it all running with a single, lean operational team. 2 NWS lives with the risk that a single day of GPS downtime could cost the U.S. economy a billion dollars.  

    Every Space Force team is vulnerable to electrical infrastructure attacks and must defend legacy systems from by-the-minute, AI-generated cyberattacks while operating with a bare minimum of staff. I lose more sleep knowing the People’s Republic of China has increased its orbital presence 925% over the last 10 years to “…enable long-range precision strikes and [deny] other militaries the use of space-based information.”   

    >>> Golden Dome: America’s Answer to China’s Space Weapons?

    As impressive as their work is, USSF needs to scale up to do more. Congress can help all Americans sleep better by doing three things.  

    First, by fully authorizing USSF’s $7.859 billion FY2027 Budget request. These funds are the minimum needed to build the resilience required to keep each of these mission areas fully functioning in the event of an attack.  

    Second, eliminate the habit of using Continual Resolutions (CRs) instead of passing budgets. CRs adversely affect all military services, but USSF feels it most acutely because it is software-dependent, and each “fix” or “upgrade” counts as a CR, a legally prohibited new start. CRs create a cascading cession of advantage to our adversaries.  

    Third, prioritize and fund USSF’s Single-Component Transfer Program. Unique in the military organizational structure, this plan lets members of other services’ reserve forces work “part time” for USSF and eases shortages in highly technical fields.  

    The Albuquerque Isotopes did not have a winning season this year. But with congressional help, Space Force can win every day, every year.

    #8040. Stop the Blame Game: Time To Restore Bipartisanship in Foreign Aid

    Last year saw the lowest number of people dying of AIDS around the world in over 30 years. This should be big news, given the millions the disease has killed and the billions the United States government has spent to combat it. Yet it isn’t. Why?

    It’s not just the media’s natural aversion to reporting good news. The real problem is when that good news undercuts the foreign aid industry’s narrative that President Donald Trump’s dismantling of the U.S. foreign aid apparatus has been an unmitigated global catastrophe.

    The U.S. Agency for International Development’s demise last year prompted severe criticism. For thousands of people that lost their jobs and careers it is understandable. But one should expect more calibrated responses from prominent foreign aid advocates.

    It was the opposite. U2 frontman Bono cited an estimate suggesting that as many as 300,000 people may already have died as a result of the cuts, while acknowledging that the figure was unproven. Bill Gates warned that millions could die if cuts are not reversed. UNAIDS warned that the collapse of U.S.-supported HIV services could lead to around 4 million additional AIDS-related deaths between 2025 and 2029. A Lancet study projected that continued USAID defunding could result in more than 14 million additional deaths from all causes by 2030.

    Missed amid the histrionics was that according to UNAIDS, 570,000 people died of AIDS worldwide in 2025, the lowest in years and a 73% decline from a peak of over 2.1 million in 2004. While still too high, the vast improvement is a testament to America’s unique and durable ability to mobilize massive resources—particularly through the U.S. President’s Emergency Plan for AIDS Relief, or PEPFAR, and taxpayer generosity—to respond to any global crisis anywhere. The predicted mass death did not happen last year, nor so far this year, and the industry looks unserious.

    >>> Response to Venezuela’s Crisis Proves That America Can Still Help in Substantial Ways—Even Without USAID

    It is over 18 months since Trump ordered a review and suspension of U.S. foreign assistance, leading to USAID’s subsequent dismantling, and the reimagining of foreign aid as an unapologetic tool of national security. This was the basis for USAID’s establishment during the Cold War.

    We can now better assess the impact. Emerging is a picture not of death and doom but, rather, a growing self-reliance from once aid-dependent countries where social services were regularly outsourced to foreign groups. They are now stepping up.

    An accelerated journey to self-reliance

    As the Democratic Republic of Congo struggles to contain its 17th Ebola outbreak—amid concerns about reduced U.S. assistance—in a region beset by armed conflict, neighboring Uganda was declared free of the Ebola virus by the World Health Organization in August.

    Kenya, meanwhile, has mobilized testing, contact tracing, and surveillance after confirming its first imported Ebola case in October. As of Oct. 11, authorities had reported no additional infections.

    Both prove Africa’s growing competence to handle serious epidemics.

    Ghana responded to its aid funding gap by removing a cap on the National Health Insurance Fund, freeing up an estimated $300 million, to “take our destinies into our own hands.”

    African leaders are mobilizing continentwide to develop new financial models not with donor agencies but with the private sector. They rightly see local manufacturing of medicines as “central to health sovereignty and long-term development.” Africa CDC estimates that the continent imports around 70%-90% of its medicines and medical devices and 99% of its vaccines.

    Kenya’s President William Ruto, one of the continent’s vocal advocates of a shift away from dependence on foreign assistance, sums up Africa's reaction to U.S and Europewide foreign aid cuts: “Africa wants investment and equal partnership, not aid.”

    Aid cuts are not just a MAGA obsession. European socialist and conservative governments alike are also reducing aid budgets amid fiscal pressures and changing priorities, and questions of aid effectiveness.

    The new U.S. bilateral health agreements

    In part, the African continent’s accelerated move to self-reliance springs from the U.S. administration’s restructuring of its global health programs. It has struck 36 government-to-government agreements. The administration says that countries will receive predictable multiyear financing and technical support to transition to full financial ownership of these lifesaving programs. I understand this is designed to cut out international NGOs, U.N. agencies, and contractors in favor of direct engagement with the private drug producers, and is this not what was needed?

    Corruption and the legacy of foreign aid

    For years, open-ended foreign aid subsidized African budgets that, in many cases, fostered a culture of corruption and rewarded bad economic policies. Meanwhile, aid diversion risks undercutting congressional and popular support for foreign aid.

    >>> PEPFAR: From AIDS Relief to Leftwing Funding Apparatus

    If the new approach creates stronger incentives for accountability and domestic ownership, that would be a welcome change.

    Also, fears that placing America’s global disaster response capacity under the State Department proved misplaced, as shown by the U.S. response to Venezuela’s earthquakes.

    Nevertheless, the sector’s condescending dismissal of foreign aid critics continues. The administration’s approach to foreign aid is based on working oneself out of a job by transitioning from traditional grants-based aid to what we all know creates wealth: foreign investment and trade.

    This was the core of USAID’s Journey to Self-Reliance during the first Trump administration. Throughout the decades, foreign aid achieved much in advancing American interests and doing good, but it went on too long without a credible and sustainable pathway to self-reliance.

    Still, much of the media coverage focuses on the disruption caused by the dismantling of USAID, covering views of former aid officials and defunded partners. Without an honest accounting of both the costs of the cuts and the results of the new approach, rebuilding bipartisan confidence in foreign aid will remain difficult.

    #8041. The Public School System Has Failed American Kids. Now What?

    Writing in the London Times on Sept. 29, author Evan Barker wrote a sobering account about her experience in a Kansas City, Missouri, school two decades ago:

    Every day is a fight for survival, not a place to get an education. During passing periods, I’d hurry as fast as I could to avoid being 'jumped.’ I’d walk down the hallways seeing fights break out with alarms blaring and the school’s lone police officer running through masses of pupils after a kid had been beaten up. It’s impossible to learn when you’re constantly in survival mode.

    The next day, the Daily Mail published a piece headlined “Huge room full of distraught children confront school district over violent behavior at chaotic New York school.” This was not a troubled New York City school, but a high school in upstate Henrietta, south of Rochester. Students “claim the entire student body has been desensitized to fights, substance use and physical threats.”

    This is useful context for Nicole Hannah-Jones’ recent reentry into the national conversation. In her article “In Fighting for Every Black Child, Did I Betray My Own?” she discusses her daughter’s experience in a New York City school. In brief: absent or incompetent teachers; misbehaving and violent students; kids who can’t read or write.

    >>> France’s Education Collapse Is a Warning for America

    Ms. Hannah-Jones deliberately placed her daughter in New York’s Public School 307, hoping the presence of enlightened parents such as herself would make them better. It didn’t. Neither will more money. Her daughter’s elementary school spends $52,000 per pupil, so each classroom costs taxpayers more than a million bucks a year.

    For that, they should expect teachers who show up, grade papers and keep kids under control. They also could hope for better than 17% proficiency in math and 28% in reading at a school with a ratio of only nine pupils per teacher.

    Harvard economists Will Dobbie and Roland Fryer found that class size, per-pupil expenditure, teacher certifications and advanced degrees are not correlated with school effectiveness. What is? “Frequent teacher feedback, the use of data to guide instruction, high-dosage tutoring, increased instructional time and high expectations.” The proof of this concept is in the field.

    In New York, charter schools—where discipline and accountability are possible—outperform regular city schools in reading and math. These are not bastions of “white privilege,” but highly diverse schools with 95% non-white students. In London, Katherine Birbalsingh’s Michaela School continually churns out top exam results, with a student body that is mostly non-White and non-wealthy.

    As a former teacher, my heart sinks for the millions of American kids held captive to left-wing ideology. Their teachers no doubt read Paulo Freire’s “Pedagogy of the Oppressed” in teacher college, because research shows it is one of the most often assigned books in colleges of education. There, they learned not so much how to teach but how to foment activism.

    Many of the good teachers move to better schools or simply give up teaching. The worst stay, protected by their labor unions from ever being fired.

    >>> Stop Writing Blank Checks to Campuses That Protect Hate

    The national teachers’ unions want lifetime employment with no accountability. They spend millions to elect politicians who will pay them more and expect less. They resist efforts to fire teachers for poor work or conduct, implement performance pay, or test teachers’ competence and knowledge. And of course, they most bitterly resist charter schools, school choice, or other attempts to give parents a choice to escape failed schools in the public K-12 system.

    Meanwhile, the National Assessment of Educational Progress reveals that students have made no gains since they began tracking performance 30 years ago. iPads, PCs and now artificial intelligence are not magic bullets that can replace study and discipline. Faddish nonsense such as Critical Race Theory, equitable grading, emotional and social learning, ethnic studies, and multicultural awareness takes valuable, limited time away from reading, writing, math and science.

    We owe it to our children to end this expensive failure and resume what works. As I wrote in “The Ten Woke Commandments (You Must Not Obey)”, the solutions are obvious, but the teachers’ unions will fight them to the death: no phones in class, student discipline, testing, teacher competency, longer hours, more homework and parental responsibility.

    It’s not rocket science. But if we fix K-12, maybe we’ll get some more rocket scientists out of the wasted talent stagnating in schools such as P.S. 307.

    #8042. France’s Riots Echo 1968. But Macron Is No de Gaulle

    On March 29, 1968, at the height of leftist protests crippling France, President Charles de Gaulle boarded a secret flight to a French military base in Germany. France was teetering on chaos, and the iconic French leader wanted to know whether the army would remain loyal.

    There are strong similarities but also differences with the present situation in France, whose streets are now engulfed daily in violent riots that are spreading to other European countries.

    Clement Liebard, president of La Cocarde Etudiante, a conservative student group fighting the Left to keep schools open, emailed me one difference with ’68: Trade unions are less engaged now than they were back then. That reflects the trend of industrial workers being less and less interested in pursuing societal change.

    But some elements of today’s protests are integral to France. The French character can be explosive, which means that France periodically succumbs to massive street violence, and this violence can topple not just governments but entire societal systems.

    >>> France’s Education Collapse Is a Warning for America

    De Gaulle was despondent when he landed at the base in Baden-Baden. According to Gen. Jacques Massu, the commander of French forces in Germany, de Gaulle said to him, “Everything is ruined, the communists have caused a total paralysis of the country. I command nothing anymore. I’m going to quit.”

    Massu, who revealed this exchange years later, well after de Gaulle’s death from a heart attack in 1970, said he reassured his president that the military was behind him in case he needed to use it to quell the unrest. He urged him to return to Paris.

    That de Gaulle did. The next day, back in Paris, de Gaulle resolutely told his countrymen: “I will not resign.” He dissolved the National Assembly and called for snap elections. A massive demonstration in his favor took place on the Champs-Elysees, and weeks later the Gaullists triumphed in the elections, re-establishing de Gaulle’s authority.

    President Emmanuel Macron, however, is no Charles de Gaulle. The former is a weak man leaving behind him a failed economy and a country gripped by riots; the latter led the Free French during World War II.

    Another difference in today’s riots is the participation of Islamic demonstrators, who can be seen waving Palestinian and Algerian flags and who have forged a “red-green alliance” with communists, also coordinating the riots. We see this alliance here in the U.S. as well.

    France in the 1960s had a Muslim population of just over 1%, nowhere near the 10% of today. And today they are highly concentrated in the urban areas where the riots are taking place. In Paris it is close to 20%, and in Marseille, just under 30%.

    But when you hear today’s rioters chant slogans from ’68, you realize that the similarities are numerous, too.

    De Gaulle was well aware back then that street violence could lead to change in France, having himself returned to power in 1958 after demonstrations over the war in Algeria shook France, and he was asked to form a government.

    The turmoil proved fatal for the Fourth Republic, with its unstable coalitions, and led to today’s Fifth Republic with its strong presidential system, initially under de Gaulle.

    The ’58 crisis is not called “the Algiers putsch” for nothing; constitutional niceties were kept as the National Assembly invested de Gaulle as president and authorized him to write a new constitution. But it was street violence that delivered the death blow. Massu himself seized government buildings in Algiers and formed a “Committee of Public Safety.”

    And then there was that other massive street uprising, the French Revolution, which ended Europe’s old order and rewrote borders, politics, and society, and the street rebellions of the 19th century. Baron Georges-Eugene Haussmann, Napoleon III’s prefect, had parts of Paris demolished and ordered the construction of the wide, straight boulevards we admire today—to make it easier for troops to shoot protesters.

    >>> Why This Legacy Newspaper Is Siding With Illegal Aliens Over American Citizens

    Today, street violence is helping the Right electorally, just like the Revolution led to Napoleon and ’58 to de Gaulle. Marine Le Pen, leader of Rassemblement National, is poised to win the upcoming presidential elections, defeating by a 67-33 margin Jean-Luc Melenchon, the communist leader of La France Insoumise. Melenchon is expected to face Le Pen in the second round of voting in May.

    RN is described in the media as “far right,” but this is wrong. It is a strong supporter of French identity, and Le Pen has responded to the riots by saying, “I will immediately conduct mass deportations. Mass immigration has been a disaster to France.” Liebard tells me that the riots and LFI’s involvement give Le Pen “the perfect arguments.”

    However, Liebard adds that the riots help Melenchon mobilize his electorate and train his activists. “He will have the largest activist striking force to occupy the field during the weeks leading up to the election,” he wrote to me.

    Melenchon has already promised to install a Sixth Republic with a new Marxist constitution if he is elected. Let’s hope France finds a way to save herself and not export her revolution to the rest of Europe—as it did during the period known as “the Terror” in the 1790s.

    #8043. Time Bought, Leverage Preserved: Outcomes of the Trump–Xi Summit in Washington

    From September 23 to 25, 2026, Chinese General Secretary Xi Jinping visited Washington, DC, for a summit with President Donald Trump. A previous Heritage Foundation analysis expected the summit to be limited to tactical agreements rather than transformative breakthroughs.[REF] The outcomes largely matched those expectations. This Issue Brief reviews the Washington Summit’s deliverables, assesses its outcomes, and looks ahead at the U.S.–China relationship’s trajectory.

    Outcomes

    The Washington Summit marked the second series of in-person meetings between President Trump and General Secretary Xi in 2026. Shortly before the summit began, the United States and China announced that the Busan trade truce, which was reached in 2025 to stabilize tariff rates and the flow of rare earths, was extended from November 10, 2026, to January 10, 2027.[REF]

    On September 25, the White House published a fact sheet detailing the summit’s outcomes.[REF] The outcomes include:

    • The United States welcomed Chinese progress on cracking down on fentanyl precursor chemicals while “strongly encouraging” additional actions, including the “permanent scheduling” of morphine and the imposition of “maximum sentences, including the death penalty where applicable,” for 21 Chinese citizens arrested for illicit drug production.
    • The United States welcomed two pandas destined for the Atlanta Zoo.
    • The two countries operationalized the Board of Trade by reaching a consensus for “more favorable tariff treatment for $30 billion of non-sensitive goods in each direction.”
    • China committed to importing “at least 10 million metric tons of coal from the United States in 2027 and again [in] 2028.”
    • The two countries “established” the Board of Investment but provided no additional operational details.
    • The two countries “continue to work on U.S. concerns regarding supply chain shortages related to rare earths and other critical minerals, with the goal of ensuring shipment levels return to appropriate levels.”
    • President Trump will visit China in November for the meeting of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (APEC) forum while General Secretary Xi will visit the United States in December for the G20.
    • The leaders “agreed that Iran can never have a nuclear weapon and no country or institution can be allowed to impose tolls on international waterways.”
    • While stating that the United States and China were “allies in World War II and fought side by side to win the war,” the fact sheet immediately follows up by stating that the United States’ “forward military presence has preserved a Free and Open Indo–Pacific contributing to generations of American prosperity.”
    • The leaders also discussed Russia, North Korea, “trilateral arms control,” and “super intelligence.”
    • The two countries “established the U.S.–China Super Intelligence (SI) Dialogue to exchange views on risks and benefits related to SI,” which includes a “bilateral communication channel for SI incidents.”

    On September 26, the Chinese Ministry of Foreign Affairs published its own readout outlining eight outcomes:[REF]

    1. The two countries agreed to build “a constructive China–U.S. relationship of strategic stability on the basis of respect, fairness, and reciprocity.”
    2. The two heads of state intend to attend each other’s hosting of APEC and the G20.
    3. Both leaders agreed that Iran “should honor its commitment to not developing nuclear weapons, and no country or institution can be allowed to impose tolls on international waterways.”
    4. Both leaders “recall that China and the U.S. were allies in World War II and fought side by side to win the war.”
    5. The two countries reached an agreement on reciprocal tariff reductions on $30 billion worth of goods.
    6. The two countries highlighted cooperation on counter-narcotics and law enforcement operations.
    7. Both sides agreed to establish the “China–U.S. AI Dialogue” and “establish a bilateral communication channel for AI [artificial intelligence] incidents.”
    8. The United States welcomed two pandas arriving at the Atlanta Zoo.

    Interestingly, the Chinese readout also claimed that the United States and China agreed to “conclude a memorandum of understanding on crisis communication and prevention as soon as possible.”[REF] The United States fact sheet did not include this reference, and a U.S. official has since stated that the claim “is not accurate and was not even discussed.”[REF] One issue not included in the White House fact sheet was addressing wrongfully detained Americans and other political prisoners, such as Min Zin, who was detained shortly after the May summit, and Jimmy Lai. President Trump has since confirmed to reporters that “I did” raise their cases during his meetings with General Secretary Xi.[REF]

    Combined, the White House fact sheet and Chinese readout show that the U.S.–China relationship’s “floor” remains steady, but the “ceiling” remains constrained to short-term agreements that do not address the most contentious issues that underlie the bilateral relationship.

    Analysis

    The Washington Summit produced three main takeaways:

    1. The United States Did Not Concede on Strategic Policy Issues. Ultimately, this is perhaps the best U.S. outcome from the summit. Based on the agreements announced, the United States did not alter its policy positions on Taiwan, export controls, and strategic economic sectors. General Secretary Xi’s most recent attempt to have the United States oppose Taiwanese independence did not succeed.[REF] Unlike the May summit, President Trump has kept to a stricter script when discussing Taiwan policy publicly, only telling reporters that Xi “understands very much how I feel.”[REF] This should reduce some anxieties across the Indo–Pacific, including in Taiwan. Furthermore, through the Board of Trade, China is expected to import “agricultural goods, fish and seafood, logs and wood products, cosmetics, and medical devices,” while the United States will import “primarily consumer products, including appliances, toys, holiday decorations, and car seats.”[REF]

      This list is important because the goods with reduced tariffs are non-sensitive, with few national security implications. The Trump Administration rightly kept export controls “off the table.”[REF] Ultimately, the United States reached agreements that support its economic interests without sacrificing strategically significant policy positions or sectors to get an agreement across the finish line. That is a desirable and defensible outcome.

    2. Time Was Bought, But Structural Issues Are No Closer to Being Resolved. One of the summit’s most important outcomes is the two-month extension of the U.S.–China trade truce, which extends the deadline from November 10, 2026, to January 10, 2027.[REF] Initially, the United States reportedly preferred a six-month extension.[REF] By contrast, some analysts believe that the Chinese side was seeking a longer extension, perhaps up to a two-year extension, to the end of President Trump’s second term.[REF] The Administration was right to prefer a shorter extension because it allows the United States to regularly assess China’s compliance, and the Administration’s messaging suggests China has not been entirely upholding its end of the bargain. At the same time, this extension will not fundamentally alter U.S.–China relations. Both sides will continue to compete while “buying time” to strengthen their advantages and reduce external vulnerabilities. While the truce extension incentivizes compliance, it is unlikely to result in any breakthroughs.

    3. Optics Mattered, But They Were More Complicated Than Perceived. Much attention has been paid to General Secretary Xi’s reception in Washington. On the surface, many have criticized President Trump’s willingness to, literally, roll out the red carpet for the Chinese leader. This summit served as the first state visit for a Chinese leader in more than 10 years and an unusual instance of President Trump personally greeting a visiting foreign leader on the tarmac. A closer review, however, reveals that the optics may have been more mixed than some have perceived.

      As General Secretary Xi was welcomed by President Trump at Joint Base Andrews, two B-1B Lancer bombers flew over both leaders,[REF] sending a very different message than President Trump’s greeting in Beijing by dozens of children waving flags and singing songs. The next day, a B-2 Spirit bomber and four F-22 Raptors flew over the White House as President Trump and Xi participated in a military ceremony.[REF] Then, during the state dinner, performances included selections from Les Misérables, including “Do You Hear the People Sing?” which was censored in China following 2019 pro-democracy protests in Hong Kong.[REF] At the National Archives, President Trump showed General Secretary Xi America’s founding documents.

      A “last minute” change was made, however, to remove a display of Chinese artifacts after Beijing became “irate” over the planned inclusion of the 1951 Treaty of San Francisco, because “the U.S. and Japan signed it, but not China.”[REF] Notably, the treaty “leaves the status of Taiwan undetermined and does not state that the island belongs to Beijing.”[REF] The United States also reportedly considered showcasing the Six Assurances and the Three Communiqués, but the documents were “ultimately not included.”[REF] Overall, while it will not fundamentally alter the trajectory of U.S.–China relations, the summit’s pageantry carried different messages and symbolism designed to demonstrate American strength while potentially pressing into Chinese sensitivities, even as some actions may be perceived as unnecessary.

    Looking Ahead

    With the trade truce sustained and future meetings expected to occur in only two months, policy analysts should track five primary issues through the start of 2027.

    1. The Trade Truce. While it is possible for the truce to be extended further in 2027, analysts should also consider under which conditions the truce will not be extended. Both sides are emphasizing a “reciprocal” nature to “constructive strategic stability,” with rare earths, fentanyl, Taiwan, and trade already known to be potential flashpoints. Before the summit, the United States believed that China was only “fulfilling two-thirds of [its rare earth commitments].”[REF] Almost immediately after the summit, U.S. Ambassador to China David Perdue warned that “it’s going to be a bumpy road over the next year” as the United States seeks to “establish independence again on rare earth elements, pharmaceuticals, [and] commercial shipbuilding.”[REF] These statements raise the question of whether the United States will determine that extending the truce is no longer in its interest. China could also make the same decision if it believes it could extract U.S. concessions by reimposing significant export controls on rare earths. China could also reassess its position following the U.S. midterm elections. Therefore, while both sides currently have incentives to continue the truce, analysts should not automatically assume this will be the case, especially if evidence grows of Chinese noncompliance.
    2. APEC and the G20. It is possible for the leaders’ APEC and G20 meetings to result in additional deliverables, but if the September summit itself produced few unexpected outcomes, these two meetings will likely have even fewer outcomes. Instead, the purpose of these meetings will likely be to sustain a floor in the relationship, maintain leader-to-leader interactions, serve as a “safety valve” to address any immediate disputes, and lay the groundwork for 2027 interactions, including a potential trade truce extension. One emerging issue, however, could be establishing “reciprocal nuclear site visits” following “US concerns about China’s rapidly growing nuclear weapons program and after the last remaining arms control agreement between the US and Russia expired earlier this year.”[REF] If the reporting is accurate, the discussions demonstrate the issue’s increased importance to the Trump Administration, even if potential visits would provide only limited visibility into China’s broader and rapidly expanding nuclear arsenal.
    3. Taiwan Policy. On one hand, the Trump Administration did not offer any concessions on Taiwan. On the other hand, questions about a stalled $14 billion arms package for Taiwan remain. The additional planned meetings may complicate the timing of the package, potentially pushing action into early 2027, which is another reason why the two-month truce extension matters. To be clear: The Trump Administration approved nearly 40 percent more arms sales to Taiwan in 2025 alone than the Biden Administration did in its full four years.[REF] In fact, no U.S. President has approved more arms sales to Taiwan in American history than President Trump.[REF] Still, it is in America’s security and economic interests to approve the currently stalled arms sale package as soon as reasonably possible because it supports deterrence across the Taiwan Strait and helps to sustain a free and open Indo–Pacific. It is also important to avoid setting the precedent that Taiwan arms packages are negotiable.
    4. AI Policy. Debates within the United States about AI safety, namely about the potential need to “slow down” AI development or “pace the frontier,” are increasingly relevant to the U.S.–China relationship. China engages in widespread distillation attacks on U.S. large language models, such as Claude, ChatGPT, and Gemini.[REF] This means that Chinese developers can generate large volumes of outputs from U.S. models and use those outputs to train Chinese models to replicate aspects of the U.S. models’ capabilities.[REF] In other words, China’s AI “advancements” are linked in important, though not exclusive, respects to U.S. AI advancements. The Trump Administration has in recent months grown more vocal in condemning these practices.[REF] Furthermore, as AI-related security incidents continue to occur, including breaking out of digital sandboxes and conducting autonomous hacking,[REF] the Chinese Communist Party (CCP) must tackle a significant challenge: How can China advance AI without potentially compromising the CCP’s grasp on power?[REF] Thus, the establishment of a U.S.–China Super Intelligence Dialogue is a reasonable starting point to address shared interests and concerns about AI developments, but it should be viewed as merely the starting point for bilateral conversations regarding AI. Additionally, as with other issues, a “dialogue” will not solve significant and ongoing policy challenges about AI, and mistrust between the two countries will severely restrict cooperation. To this point, the United States should be wary of Chinese AI commitments and operate under the assumption that China will seek to gain advantages while protecting the CCP’s legitimacy in any cooperative arrangement.
    5. Wrongfully Detained Americans. While it is good news that President Trump raised Min Zin’s case directly with General Secretary Xi, it is unfortunate that no additional breakthrough has yet been announced. It did take multiple weeks following the May summit to secure and announce Pastor Ezra Jin’s release, however, so it is possible that discussions are ongoing through diplomatic channels. More broadly, it is important that “hostage diplomacy” does not become a routine part of China’s playbook as a means of extracting concessions from the United States. If a suitable deal, in accordance with U.S. national security interests, cannot be reached to secure Min Zin and Dr. Youlin Chen, the United States should consider imposing costs in response to Beijing’s unjust actions against American citizens.

    Conclusion

    The Washington Summit met expectations. While diplomatic interactions will continue, the United States and China remain locked in a state of competition, and meetings can only hide so much of the deep mistrust between them. In a highly competitive relationship, avoiding bad outcomes and limiting the escalation of tensions can be a meaningful success. Even within a narrow operating lane, the Trump Administration’s China policy should continue to prioritize holding China accountable to its commitments, safeguarding U.S. advantages, and putting the American people first.

    Andrew J. Harding is Policy Analyst for National Security and Indo–Pacific Affairs in the Asian Studies Center at The Heritage Foundation.

    #8044. Preventing the Next World War

    It’s clear from the increasingly bellicose actions of the People’s Republic of China that the PRC seeks to return to its self-appointed role as the “Middle Kingdom” at the center of the world. To do this it must supplant the United States, both economically and militarily, by becoming the preeminent power on the world stage—at which point it will be able to rewrite the rules of the existing world order established by America after World War II.

    The first step to overturning that order (and replacing it with a world order dominated by Beijing) is for China to become the world’s largest economy. The second and most threatening step is for China to make itself the dominant military power in the Western Pacific and expel American forces from the region while ending the long-standing system of alliances that the United States has maintained in the Indo-Pacific for the past several decades. The most decisive way for the PRC could do this would be to defeat U.S. forces in a large-scale military conflict.

    China today is a threat in a way that the Soviet Union, even at the height of its power, never was. At its strongest, the Soviet Union’s economy was only one-half that of the United States and was handicapped by not having a population large enough to fully support the necessary economic growth of a world power. Today, the PRC economy is slightly more than two-thirds as large as the U.S. economy. China has the world’s largest navy and largest concentration of missiles, is building the largest fleet of fifth-generation aircraft, and is the world’s most rapidly growing military power.

    Admittedly, the United States maintains military technological superiority over China, but quantity has a quality all its own, and China’s military expansion is impressive. The PRC regularly threatens its neighbors with economic punitive action, uses its military to carry out hostile actions against them, and even questions the legitimacy of elected governments. And it does all this while exporting its population around the world under the guise of economic support to smaller countries but actually to create economic dependence and situations for coercion.

    >>> Securing and Defending America’s Interests in the Pacific: A Strategy to Prevent the Next World War

    For these and other reasons, the United States—across both Republican and Democrat administrations—has prioritized China as its primary foreign military adversary. The United States Department of War has embarked upon a massive modernization effort to field a force that can credibly deter and, if necessary, decisively defeat Chinese aggression across the Indo-Pacific theater.

    And we do mean massive. This revitalization includes investments in shipyards, missile production lines, next-generation fighters and bombers, more strategic airlift and sealift capabilities, air refueling tankers, the Golden Dome missile defense system, and a strategic deterrent to carry the United States through most of the 21st century.

    Given American commitments to and responsibilities in parts of the world beyond the Indo-Pacific—including the Western Hemisphere, Europe, and the Middle East—the United States must and will work with allies and partners to deter aggression from the Beijing-led axis while other U.S. allies and partners that are threatened by Russia and Iran assume a larger share of the burden for their own security.

    Everyone will have to step up. Such an axis is designed to overturn the existing U.S.-led world order, which ensures much of the stability so many rely on today. Working with allies and partners to deter aggression from such an axis means identifying which capabilities, provided by which countries, are needed in the world’s theaters to deter aggression and so avoid conflict from a position of strength.

    Should deterrence fail, however, the United States and its allies and partners must field the forces and capabilities—and maintain the political will—to defeat decisively any aggressive action from the PRC or its vassal, North Korea. There are positive trends that indicate this strategy would be successful. They include especially the U.S.’s military modernization plans, which include new systems such as the B-21 that will provide a credible deterrent to Chinese military planners, and the seriousness with which American allies and partners like Japan, Australia, the Philippines, and Taiwan are taking the threat from China as exemplified by their expanded defense budgets and security cooperation.

    >>> Reintroduce Nuclear Weapons to the Pacific To Reduce the Chances of War With China
