    Yet while building U.S. military capability and capacity while working with regional partners and allies as a means to deter adversary aggression is critically important, it should be remembered that the most credible (and therefore the most effective) deterrence posture is the one that can achieve victory on the battlefield. There is essentially no difference between the fielding of capabilities that can best deter aggression and the fielding of those that can decisively defeat an adversary.

    To secure peace, deter aggression, and if necessary, decisively defeat the authoritarians in Beijing, the United States should:

    • Support a peaceful and free Pacific with the United States as the status quo power against an aggressive PRC that seeks to overturn the regional balance of power and order in its favor.
    • Encourage U.S. allies in the Indo-Pacific to adopt the NATO 5 percent spending model.
    • Expand U.S. airpower across the region.
    • Proliferate air and missile defenses across the theater.
    • Forward deploy ground-based long-range precision fires across the theater.
    • Create a U.S. anti-access/aerial denial (A2/AD) zone within China’s A2/AD zone before a conflict erupts.
    • Finance and sell inexpensive, asymmetric capabilities for Taiwan and the Philippines.
    • Forward deploy non-strategic nuclear weapons to Guam and Korea.
    • Expand maritime presence within the theater, to include destroyer and submarine operations.

    “To be prepared for war is one of the most effectual means of preserving peace,” President George Washington said in his first annual address to Congress. Only by heeding his advice can we prevent China from achieving complete hegemony over the United States.

    #8045. Childhood Vaccine Schedules Across Countries

    There is a wide disparity across the globe when it comes to childhood vaccination policies. American kids receive up to 75 vaccines; twice as many as kids in other countries. That is why if Dr. Heidi Overton is confirmed as the next director of the Food and Drug Administration (FDA), one of her top priorities should be helping to drive President Trump’s mandate to study the safety and comparative outcomes current vaccine schedule to that of other countries. 

    High on her list of priorities will surely be the two executive orders President Trump signed this year to promote the health of American children, especially since there doesn’t appear to be any movement on either. 

    He issued Executive Order 14407 on May 29, 2026, directing the federal government to realign US childhood vaccine recommendations with best practices from peer nations. Section 3 of the order directs HHS, through the Task Force on Safer Childhood Vaccines, to present plans within 90 days to assess timing and sequencing, as well as “improve vaccine safety monitoring, transparency, and research.” 

    Next, on August 10, the president signed Executive Order 14420 to reduce immunization recommendations from 17–18 diseases down to 11, spacing out shots across more doctor visits, and splitting the combined MMR vaccine into three separate injections. 

    The president is fulfilling his commitment to vaccination study and reform, but it doesn’t appear that anyone is listening. Ninety days have passed and the task force, chaired by NIH Director Jay Bhattacharya, just recently resurrected in August 2025 after 27 years dormant, still hasn’t shared the names of anyone (other than the always present HHS heads) who have been appointed to take charge of designing the study, along with statistical plans, according to a rudimentary internet search. 

    >>> Parents, Not Bureaucrats, Should Call the Shots

    To date, there has not been any publicly proposed draft study design, outline, list of experts needed, proposed safety endpoints, request for public comment, or even a single primary or secondary safety or efficacy study objective shared. 

    The Task Force does not owe Congress a report until 2027. 

    Comparing Childhood Vaccination Schedules to Peer Countries 

    The inaction on this priority is concerning because the president has good reason to scrutinize the current vaccine schedule. A cursory review of international vaccine standards reveals that no two countries are quite the same when it comes to the number of vaccines or recommendations versus mandates. 

    For example, by age 18, a child in the United States will receive roughly 30 to 75 total vaccine doses depending on the state. All 50 states mandate a robust set of childhood vaccines. On top of CDC requirements, some states follow American Academy of Pediatrics guidelines and recommend even more. 

    Various peer countries have a mixture of mandated or recommended schedules when it comes to their childhood schedules. For example, Denmark recommends 30 to 38 vaccines. Bulgaria recommends 56 to 61. France and Italy mandate most of their vaccines in kids. Norway, Sweden, and Spain mandate none. 

    Here is a breakdown of the various countries I looked up: 

    United Kingdom:

    19 injections, 44 different vaccines by age 14

    Australia (NSW):

    As many as 26 total vaccines by the age of 10, with recommendations varying for aboriginal versus the European populations. 

    China:

    Up to age 7, there are 26-29 total vaccines, depending on if the live versus inactivated strains are used. There are no vaccine mandates in China, and there are “no punishments associated with noncompliance.” 

    Yes, you read that correctly. 

    After Chinese citizens contended that the mandate violated the principles of “informed consent and voluntariness” the government withdrew its Covid mandates after just one day. And Chinese children aren’t mandated to take any other vaccines either. 

    Despite the fact that China is a one-party authoritarian government run by the ruling Chinese Communist Party, Chinese citizens face less pressure to obey vaccine mandates. Yet American citizens had Covid shot and vaccine mandates before they could attend schools, restaurants, or even go to work. 

    Japan: 

    By the age 16 in Japan, 14 injections total, 29-32 total vaccines, depending on the population and/or vaccine product used. 

    Norway: 

    Through the age of 15 years, Norway has 37-44 total vaccines/boosters recommended, depending on the population and/or vaccine regimen used. In Norway, no vaccine is mandatory. 

    Sweden:

    Through the age of 18 years, Sweden has 41-50 vaccines/boosters recommended, depending on the population and/or vaccine regimen used. In Sweden, no vaccine is mandatory. 

    Austria:

    Through the age of 18 years, Austria has 49-54 vaccines/boosters recommended, depending on the population and/or vaccine product used. In Austria, no vaccine is mandatory.

    Belgium:

    Through the age of 18 years, in Belgium 49-57 vaccines/boosters are recommended or mandated, depending on the population and/or vaccine product used. 

    Bulgaria: 

    Through the age of 18 years, in Bulgaria 56-61 vaccines/boosters are recommended, depending on the population and/or vaccine product used. In Bulgaria, no vaccine is mandatory.

    Czech Republic: 

    Through the age of 18 years, in Czechia 46-59 vaccines/boosters are recommended or mandated, depending on the population and/or vaccine product used. 

    Denmark:

    Through the age of 18 years, in Denmark 30-38 vaccines/boosters are recommended, depending on the population and/or vaccine product used. In Denmark, no vaccine is mandated. 

    France:

    Through the age of 18 years, in France 46-53 vaccines/boosters are recommended or mandated, depending on the population and/or vaccine product used. 

    Italy: 

    Through the age of 18 years, in Italy 45-50 vaccines/boosters are recommended or mandated, depending on the population and/or vaccine product used. 

    Spain:

    Through the age of 18 years, in Spain 42-51 vaccines/boosters are recommended, depending on the population and/or vaccine product used. In Spain, no vaccine is mandatory.

    Germany: 

    Through the age of 18 years, in Germany 49-53 vaccines/boosters are recommended or mandated, depending on the population and/or vaccine product used. 

    As is clear, peer nations significantly differ from each other and the United States, when it comes to the number of vaccines they recommend and/or require. 

    Buried in that variation are the questions Trump wants his HHS officials to answer. They include: Which country’s vaccine schedule produces the best short- and long-term overall health outcomes? With both severe and serious adverse events reported and important questions about safety, how far out would those safety evaluations take place after vaccine administration? What will be done to assure that those measurements will be accurately collected? 

    No one has answered these or a multitude of other important questions, and the delays in appointing a team to ask, plus address these questions, make it seem like nobody’s even trying. 

    Separate MMR Shots: Japan Got a “Yes”…Will Trump Get a “No?”  

    Trump’s Executive Order 14420 separating vaccine combinations and spreading them out across doctor’s visits signed in August has both a regulatory and clinical precedence in the real word, rooted in bona fide drug-safety epidemiology findings. 

    Japan withdrew its combined measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine in 1993 because data showed that it had led to an unexpectedly high incidence of meningitis (an inflammation of the membrane surrounding the brain—a clinical emergency). When the government became aware of that, they requested that manufacturers split apart the triple combination MMR, allowing Japanese citizens the option to take separate components. While pharmaceutical manufacturers accommodated Japan’s request, no manufacturer has stepped up to accommodate Trump’s order. 

    Task Force on Safer Childhood Vaccines

    Trump’s executive order on childhood vaccines mandates to present plans within 90 days to assess timing and sequencing and “improve vaccine safety monitoring, transparency, and research.” 

    Plans. Not studies. Ninety days to plan a study of a schedule that had already been rewritten by declaration. And we don’t even know any details about those planned studies. Are Trump’s executive orders being ignored? Are these childhood vaccine study plans confidential? When are they planning on their first meeting? How often will they meet? The task force still hasn’t shared the names of whom (if anyone) has been appointed to take charge of designing a study according to a rudimentary internet search. Are the task force members’ names a secret? 

    In sum: It’s been two months following the second of two executive orders, and to date, there’s been no publicly proposed draft study design, study outline, list of experts, proposed safety endpoints, request for public comment, or even a single primary or secondary study objective. 

    HHS Delays Needed Vaccines Studies for Over a Decade 

    While the time it is taking to address this issue is disappointing, Trump’s HHS is not the only one to blame. Questions regarding vaccine safety go back much further. In 2013 the Institute of Medicine reported that “studies designed to examine the long-term effects of the cumulative number of vaccines or other aspects of the immunization schedule have not been conducted.” The Institute of Medicine authors recommended building that capacity into the Vaccine Safety Datalink. Will that be part of the testing methodology for Trump’s EO? 

    Your guess is as good as mine. 

    Thirteen years later, a Congressional report aptly calls our postmarket vaccine surveillance “limited” and “underutilized.” The same finding, twice, under both parties, with nothing built in between. 

    The 18-disease schedule the courts restored following the reduction proposed by Secretary Robert F. Kennedy, Jr. in March 2026 was never tested as a whole or in comparison to peer-developed countries. And neither was the 11-disease schedule written to replace it. Washington has now rewritten the schedule twice without producing a single proposed study design for comparative outcomes measurement. 

    Trump Ordered Childhood Vaccine Schedule Scrutiny for Good Reason 

    The assessment that the FDA and CDC keep citing declares that manufacturers: “have inadequate incentives to study vaccine adverse effects” and that vaccine “safety and risks are therefore often poorly characterized, quantified, or understood.” The same Congressional report called for gold-standard, double-blind, placebo-controlled trials. If manufacturers can’t or won’t conduct objective data, NIH officials are obligated to do so. Not immediately proceeding with studies is to surrender to the status quo on providing Americans answers vaccine safety and efficacy. 

    The pattern held on August 21, 2026, when HHS sought input on whether its recommendation labels are the right ones and invited comment on: “The appropriate approach when randomized controlled trial evidence is limited or absent.” 

    Did you catch that? 

    You might want to read that sentence again: 

    It appears that officials are on track to blow past their 90-day EO deadline. They have not publicly mentioned assembling a team of outside experts for input on how to design and generate better vaccine evidence to protect America’s kids…they appear to be asking for advice on how to proceed without it! 

    Agency and Outside Experts Need to Do Their Jobs

    Studying the childhood schedule doesn’t require new Congressional authority, novel theory, regulatory modification, or even special permission. The NIH, FDA and CDC already have that ability and discretionary funding—and have had so for decades—they just need to do their jobs to protect kids. 

    First, they should build the study around the variation that already exists: our schedule against peer countries’ schedules, combination products against single—injection components, spaced administration versus concomitant. Create strict criteria to make sure that investigators diligently collect all safety and efficacy failures, and report their findings in real time. 

    >>> Preparing Now for the Next Deadly Pandemic

    Second, they should share the study protocol and publish it before a single child is enrolled. Ask for input from a multi-disciplinary group of experts to make sure important details are not omitted. After all, an essential dialectic of science is discussion and debate between experts on how to best answer a clinical question.

    With that, design a study protocol with an independent, multi-disciplinary team, including experts in pharmacology, statistics, epidemiology, and clinical trial design—not manufacturers, not “sponsored” special-interest groups, not a biased panel hand-picked to ratify a premeditated conclusion. Carefully design primary and secondary endpoints, so it is done correctly the first time. Hold all overseers and career federal employees who design and author the trial accountable.

    Third, they should convert passive adverse-event reporting into active surveillance and fund it permanently. Demand complete ingredient transparency of all vaccine ingredients from manufacturers. After all, one cannot fully evaluate the safety or efficacy of any type of vaccine unless the exact vaccine ingredients are known (which they aren’t). 

    Finally, they must finish what was proposed in 2013. Prominently publish all data, positive or negative, and have the HHS scientists who designed and conducted the studies hold public town halls and press conferences to discuss those findings and give careful and measured answers to all questions, perpetually as dose rollouts and data rolls in. 

    In sum, Americans have had questions about their vaccines for many years now, and that number has only exploded with mRNA development. Americans are desperate for answers, but are instead getting even more mRNA FDA approvals. 

    Trump appointees and career employees at the CDC, NIH, and FDA now have the chance to provide Americans with answers and be transparent, if they choose. Under Trump, we finally have the opportunity to study and learn about the risks and benefits of the childhood vaccine schedule. 

    DISCLAIMER: This article is NOT medical advice. Do not start or discontinue any drug without first discussing it with a pharmacist or physician you know and trust. 

    #8046. When Union Leaders Are Not Working for Their Workers

    Last month, a House hearing exposed eight years of America’s industrial unions spending members’ money on union leaders’ personal priorities rather than on members.

    The findings should change the way Washington hears union leaders when they claim to speak for workers.

    First are political donations. The railroad brotherhoods’ PACs sent 95% to 99% of their party committee dollars to Democrats, and the conductors’ union made far-left EMILY’s List its single largest recipient.

    Yet when the Teamsters, parent of some rail unions, polled its members in 2024, roughly 60% backed President Trump.

    Then there are questionable expenditures. These include the Sheet Metal, Air, Rail and Transportation Workers—Transportation Division, the largest railroad labor union in North America, spending $3.9 million at Caesars Entertainment in 2024—part of nearly $10 million on casinos and resorts over eight years.

    >>> Randi Weingarten’s Convention Exposes the Teachers’ Union as a Political Machine

    Meanwhile, its president earned more than five times the median pay of a railroad worker. Apparently, it is wrong only when management does it.

    Similarly, two former United Auto Workers presidents went to prison for spending members’ dues on golf, cigars and Palm Springs villas.

    The same divide appears in the policies that union leaders push, such as the Faster Labor Contracts Act, which passed the House in June. Under the act, if a union and an employer cannot agree on a first contract within roughly four months, a three-member arbitration panel would impose one, binding for two years, without workers themselves being able to vote on it.

    It guarantees that union leadership collects dues without risking members’ rejection of the terms. It short-circuits a process by which members have a basic check on those allegedly bargaining in their name.

    Union leaders have also spent years pushing to replace secret-ballot elections with public card signings, letting organizers know how members vote and thereby exposing those members to pressure and even retaliation.

    The rail unions’ latest top priority, the Railway Safety Act, fits the pattern. Recently attached to the House transportation bill, its centerpiece is a federal mandate to increase freight crew sizes. Leadership loves it because every additional crew seat written into federal law is a stream of dues that cannot be bargained away for things members might actually want, such as higher pay or better schedules.

    Although union leaders claim this is about crew safety and cite the East Palestine, Ohio, derailment as their motivation, they have been pushing for enlarged crews for years before the Ohio disaster. Furthermore, the East Palestine crash train crew had an extra crew member on board, and that practice did not prevent the accident.

    Likewise, federal regulators have repeatedly conceded they lack data showing that an additional crew member prevents accidents. The industry reports that 2025 was its safest year on record across several measures, largely thanks to technology. These are advancements that the bill threatens.

    When BNSF Railway tested automated track inspection, its track geometry cars found 64,657 defects while covering 97% fewer miles than human inspectors, who found only 4,796.

    Rail unions fought that shift at every turn, and now they want the Railway Safety Act to undo this technological progress, expanding manual inspection mandates and freezing detector rules just as better monitoring arrives.

    >>> The Anti-ICE Campaign Protecting Mexico’s Train-Robbing Cartels

    These moves would not only make the rails less safe by catching fewer defects but also expose inspectors to greater danger.

    Sadly, union leadership keeps rejecting railroads’ better offers to members. Union Pacific proposed piloting ground-based conductor jobs with regular shifts and the chance for workers to sleep in their own beds every night, a real improvement over the unpredictable, days-away-from-home schedules widely considered the worst part of the job. SMART-TD agreed to the pilot only on the condition that Union Pacific maintain current crew sizes and drop negotiations to reduce crew size.

    Rank-and-file railroaders could have regular schedules, safer jobs and technology that makes their work less dangerous, but the Railway Safety Act delivers none of that. Union leadership prefers headcount and dues instead.

    This failure of leadership will also harm families because the compliance costs of these draconian measures are in the tens of billions of dollars, costs that flow to farmers, manufacturers, energy producers—anyone who uses railroads—and to family budgets. Higher rail costs also shift freight to trucks, leading to more highway congestion and crashes.

    Union leaders have a responsibility to do what is best for their members, not pad their own pockets or enrich their political cronies. Washington would do well to remember as much.

    #8047. France’s Education Collapse Is a Warning for America

    France spent the past three weeks watching its high schools burn. What originally began in late September at a suburban school outside Paris as a protest over missing teachers, overcrowded classrooms, and decaying buildings spread to hundreds of schools and quickly metastasized into riots that included Marxists, Antifa, and Islamists.

    On the biggest day, the interior ministry counted more than 250,000 people in the streets. Prime Minister Sébastien Lecornu called the casualties the heaviest toll on the school system in decades: at least 215 teenagers and 85 staff injured, plus more than 760 police officers. Twenty-four schools have been burned or ransacked.

    The original grievances were actually real. The response is the predictable product of a state monopoly on education. France already spends more per upper-secondary student than the European Union average, according to the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD), yet the buildings still fall apart.

    Parents in France have almost nowhere else to go. Since 2022, the country has essentially banned homeschooling for ordinary families. Raising your own children at home now requires government permission, granted only for narrow exemptions such as a disability, intensive sports or artistic training, geographic isolation, or a family that travels constantly. The default is the state school. When that school fails, the street is the remaining outlet.

    >>> Randi Weingarten’s Dirty Back-to-School War on Target

    American parents should recognize the script. Our government-school cartel spends more per pupil than almost any country on earth and still leaves most children below proficiency. On the latest Nation’s Report Card, only about one in four eighth-graders is proficient in math. Union leaders respond to that failure with another political campaign.

    At its Portland convention last summer, the National Education Association pledged to defend democracy against what it called President Trump’s “embrace of fascism,” and directed staff to use that label in union materials. This July in Washington, the American Federation of Teachers (AFT) adopted a resolution to “mobilize for the 2026 elections,” casting the midterms as a “which side are you on” moment and committing the union to an unprecedented turnout machine.

    The same convention heard Jeff Freitas, president of the California federation, tell delegates that “we need to be in the streets” to “fight back against this fascist regime.” His red shirt read “Solidarity is the Lesson Plan,” with “May Day” printed on the book in the graphic. A separate resolution turned May 1 into a day of civic action, complete with political education and with students used as political pawns in the marches and rallies.

    Politics don’t stop at the ballot box. The AFT has been a major backer of the “No Kings” protests. Randi Weingarten, who is paid over half a million dollars per year to run the union, used her 2025 convention speech to call for people “in the streets” and to threaten lawmakers who refused to listen.

    This week the union’s own social-media account posted her outside Delaney Hall, the immigration detention center in Newark, shouting that detainees “play by the rules” and “should not be detained or deported because we have a broken immigration system.” If Weingarten fought this hard for instruction, more than one in four American children might be able to do grade-level math.

    The New York City affiliate, the United Federation of Teachers, endorsed Zohran Mamdani. Nationally, about 99 percent of the AFT’s campaign contributions in the last federal cycle went to Democrats. The same union urged members to skip Target for back-to-school shopping because the company had not denounced immigration enforcement, and Weingarten leaned on pension funds that directly hold about 6.8 million Target shares to try to force a political statement out of a retailer.

    >>> Randi Weingarten’s Convention Exposes the Teachers’ Union as a Political Machine

    A Freedom Foundation review of the union’s own federal filings found at least $1.4 million in AFT resources tied to producing and promoting her book, “Why Fascists Fear Teachers.” Congress is now scrutinizing that spending. Members paid for the manifesto. She kept a cut of the proceeds.

    Teachers who want to teach reading and algebra are financing a permanent political operation that marches their students, boycotts their stores, and labels half the country fascist. France shows where that model ends: a system that cannot staff a classroom, a law that keeps most families inside it, and children in the street when the adults fail them.

    Two exits would break the pattern. Teachers who are not on board can resign from the government union and join an alternative such as the Teacher Freedom Alliance, which offers liability coverage and advocacy without the partisan machine. A real exodus would hit the bosses where the dues are and force them to represent members instead of a party.

    Parents have the other lever. School choice lets a family take the education dollars assigned to a child and spend them elsewhere. The school that drills math and history gets the money. The school that treats May Day as the lesson plan loses it.

    That escape valve does more than rescue one household from indoctrination. A monopoly that cannot hold its customers cannot keep marching the next generation into socialist politics. France is the control group. America still has time to save itself from teachers’ union tyranny.

    #8048. How India-Philippines Cooperation Reinforces America’s Interests in the South China Sea

    With so much media focus in recent months on the war in Iran, it’s no surprise that Undersecretary of War for Policy Elbridge Colby’s speech in Manila received so little coverage. That means very few know what the U.S. and its allies are doing to deter China from launching any hostilities of its own.

    In April, Filipino marines undertook one of the most expansive iterations of Balikatan, the annual bilateral U.S. and Philippines military exercise. They took part in simulated firing of the BrahMos anti-ship missile during the joint maritime strike phase of the exercise. The BrahMos is an Indian missile, and while India was not a participant in Balikatan, it joined as an observer. The image of a U.S.-Philippine drill with an Indian weapons platform aptly captures India’s role in the South China Sea.

    India is not explicitly present and may never put boots on the ground, and yet its presence is felt in how it strengthens the deterrence of Southeast Asian partners through hardware, intel sharing, and strategic diversification. This is not necessarily a case of policy alignment but of strategic convergence: India’s pursuit of its own interests produces outcomes that complement Washington’s regional objectives.

    Washington’s strategy in the first island chain rests on a strategy of denial by making it too costly for China to strike against a partner nation. The Trump administration has emphasized burden sharing, pushing allies and partners do their part in ensuring that they can defend themselves. This was captured best and most recently by Elbridge Colby’s speech. He not only spoke about the importance of maintaining a favorable balance of power in the Indo-Pacific but also how partners are expected to do their part.

    >>> Why Trump’s India Policy Is Working

    “When we ask our allies and partners to step up, to invest more in their own defense,” he said, “we are not signaling abandonment.” Colby argued that deterrence along the first island chain cannot rest on American power alone but must also rely on capable partners able to defend their own territory.

    The Philippines has been doing its part, despite a rather small budget of approximately $6.12 in 2024 and $6.7 billion in the proposed 2027 budget—some of its highest figures to date. Manila also adopted Comprehensive Archipelagic Defense Concept in 2024 to focus on territorial and maritime defense. And the Self Reliant Defense Posture Law hopes to build indigenous defense capabilities, reinforced by the Horizon I, II, and III plans.

    India is playing its own role albeit for its own interests of expanding defense exports, deepening its Act East Policy, and perhaps even hedging against China—a known challenge for India as much as it is for the United States and the Philippines. India is doing so through three levers: hardware, intel sharing, and enabling a strategic diversification strategy; all of which allow Philippines to burden share and reinforce denial strategies.

    Regarding hardware, India sold to the Philippines three shore-based batteries for $375 million in 2022. India even offered a $100 million line of credit for the sale, though Manila decided to finance it from its defense appropriations. The batteries arrived on time in April 2024 and the first became operational in November in Zambales (in West Luzon) under the Marine Corps coastal defense regiment, providing a strike capability of 290 km that reaches Scarborough Shoal. India has since also offered extended range variants of 400 km with future batteries reaching up to the Spratlys when deployed from Palawan.

    Recently the Philippines Ambassador to India Ignacio remarked how the BrahMos missiles Manila imported from New Delhi are a game changer and provide the Philippines with “real credible deterrence” as territorial challenges continue to rise in the South China Sea. India has also offered Akash surface to air missiles, Tejas LCA fighter jets, helicopters, and submarines and naval vessels. The sale of these platforms is commercially useful to India, but its second-order effect is also to strengthen the same maritime denial architecture Washington is pursuing in the region.

    >>> Can the U.S.-India Defense Partnership Keep Pace With AI?

    Regarding intel, India invited the Philippines to the Information Fusion Center for the Indian Ocean Region (IFC-IOR) in 2025. The IFC-IOR is India’s maritime domain awareness hub which tracks activity in the Indian ocean region. The move builds on a 2023 coastal guard agreement and white shipping arrangement where the two navies are already collaborating on information sharing, maritime training, exchanges, joint search and rescue operations, and more. Greater maritime domain awareness improves Manila's ability to identify, track, and respond to Chinese activity, complementing the strike capabilities India is helping provide.

    Last year, India and the Philippines also had their warships conduct their first joint maritime activity inside the Philippine exclusive zone and the South China Sea. Notably, Chinese vessels were shadowing them during the joint sail. India has also publicly backed Manila’s position in the South China Sea including the 2016 arbitral award in Philippines favor which China has ignored by continuing to extend its territorial claims through artificial islands and the nine dash line.

    There are limitations. India is highly unlikely to ever put boots on the ground for the Philippines, but this may actually matter less. By providing strategic diversification, India strengthens the Philippine’s deterrence, making regional balancing look less like simply joining an American bloc. And that is the paradox here: the same strategic autonomy that limits how far India will pursue formal alliances also increases the value of what it does do.

    Because New Delhi is neither a U.S. nor Philippine treaty ally, its cooperation is harder to portray simply as an extension of American alliance politics. By pursuing its own interests, India is in the process also reinforcing American interests.

    #8049. Trump’s Census Proposal Is a Big Step in Restoring National Unity

    The Trump administration has made bold moves in its attempt to rescue America from cultural balkanization, as its fights with the Smithsonian Institution and the educational establishment demonstrate. But no move is more vital than its proposal to eliminate race, ethnicity, and sexual preference from the U.S. Census.

    Trump’s proposed rule states: “No question about race, ethnicity, or sexual orientation shall appear on the short form decennial census questionnaire or any questionnaire used for the enumeration of the population. . . . With the exception of questions about biological sex, date of birth, and household relationships, no person shall be obligated to furnish a response to any demographic question as part of the decennial census.” The rule would also exclude illegal aliens and temporary foreign workers, students, and visitors—none of whom can legally vote—from congressional apportionment.

    These changes are significant and worthy of support. Dismantling the grievance industry, the oppressor-oppressed paradigm, identity politics, and other ideas that rend the national fabric is an essential cause. The Census is a frequently overlooked theater in that struggle.

    >>> Lonnie Bunch’s Smithsonian Exit Is a Culture-War Win

    The high stakes of these Census reforms may be news to many Americans, especially conservatives and nonactivists minding their own business and trying to make ends meet. No doubt many see the Census as a mundane task that happens at the start of every decade, undertaken to provide the necessary data for apportioning seats in the House of Representatives.

    It’s considerably more than that. The Indo-European root of the word “census” means “to evoke in speech, almost to call a thing into existence by naming it,” in the words of the French classical historian Claude Nicolet. In the wrong hands, a census can change the character of a republic.

    For nearly four decades, leftists have used the Census to do just that, fragmenting the U.S. into a nation of groups, some of them invented. Few know today, for example, the meaning of MENA—short for the Middle East and North Africa, a designation that encompasses peoples as diverse as Kurds, Arabs, Persians, and Berbers. If the Left’s plans to add MENA to the 2030 Census succeed, it may become the next official ethnicity in the public mind.

    That’s how Hispanics and Asian-Americans became fixed categories in the public consciousness. Few used these terms before the Office of Management and Budget created them in 1977 and added them to the Census for the first time in 1980. I remember it happening: my uncle, a journalist, walked into our semi-detached home in Queens in the 1970s and announced, “They’re going to start calling us Hispanics.” We were dumbfounded.

    Of course, the bureaucrats didn’t come up with the idea on their own. It was pressed on them by radical groups such as the National Council of La Raza (now called UnidosUS) and the Mexican American Legal Defense and Educational Fund (MALDEF), both founded with seed money from the Ford Foundation.

    UCLA sociologist Cristina Mora, who is sympathetic to the creation of the Hispanic category, wrote in Making Hispanics, “U.S. Census Bureau records reveal that census officials were reluctant to create a Hispanic pan-ethnic category, in part because they judged that persons of Latin American descent were quite diverse and would eventually assimilate and identify as white.” Chicano activists couldn’t bear the idea of assimilation. “They used militant tactics and described Mexican-American communities as internal colonies that needed to shed the yoke of assimilation,” Mora writes. In the end, “Mexican American activists played an important role in pressuring hesitant state and census officials to institutionalize the Hispanic category.”

    At the policy level, the Census reforms will help on fronts where the administration is already fighting elsewhere with other measures, eliminating several societal ills. Among these: the corrosive doctrine of disparate impact, which holds that group discrimination doesn’t have to be intentional to be illegal; racial preferences in university admissions and hiring; court holdings that the Voting Rights Act requires the creation of racial voting districts; and a society manipulated by interest groups.

    The activists cloaked each of these actions in the rhetoric of noble intent. But America’s focus on race has unsurprisingly failed to produce the desired results. As the sociologist Nathan Glazer wrote in 2002, “As we follow this process further and further into the details that have shaped the census, the element of nobility declines.”

    The Census’s racial questions are a betrayal of the vision of the American Founders, who wanted immigrants to “get assimilated to our customs, manners and laws: in a word, soon become one people,” as George Washington put it.

    Trump’s Census proposal revives Washington’s vision by attacking the source of the problem: racial, ethnic, and sexual preference categories. As the proposal explains, the Census exists because of a constitutional mandate to count, for the apportionment of congressional seats, the “whole number of persons in each State” based on “usual residence.” This is “a process that factors in an element of allegiance but otherwise should be colorblind and should not be distorted in any way by questions about immaterial personal characteristics, such as race.” The proposal acknowledges that this step is of a “(potentially) sensitive nature” but hastens to add, “Substantial doubts about the meaning, significance, and impact of attempting to categorize persons by race and ethnicity, in particular, have featured prominently in our national discourse and have been reflected in shifts in the law.”

    >>> People, Place, Principles: This Is America

    The boldness of the proposal seems to have caught the Left off guard, but the Trump administration should hold the line against the inevitable blowback. The proposal is indispensable to the success of his overall project. It must, of course, be accompanied by a return to assimilation and a more rational immigration policy.

    The proposal itself is not perfect. While it rightly aims to stop counting all transient or illegal aliens for apportioning House seats, it doesn’t specify how the Census Bureau should determine legal status. The Supreme Court has already held that the Constitution “lawfully permits the Census Bureau to at least inquire about citizenship on the census questionnaire,” and it should absolutely do so. The racial questions should also be eliminated from the much smaller American Community Survey. The best way to support the proposal is to go to the Federal Register, submit a comment supporting it, and then ask that a question on citizenship be added. There’s no time to waste, as the comment period ends on November 2.

    In his seminal 2004 book, Who Are We?, Samuel Huntington worried that if the balkanization process continued much longer, whites would join the process rather than fight it. At that point, he warned, “the cultural divide in America will be formalized.”

    The second Trump administration has made reuniting Americans one of its signature missions. It deserves our support in this effort.
